where  the  first  three  columns  show  the  numbers  which  give,  by  the  addition  of  the
numbers  in  the  same  horizontal  line,  the  numerical  coefficients  of  the  factorials  which
multiply  the  different  terms  of  $H$, $DH$, \&c.,  and  where  in  the  last  column  $12(1)$,  \&c.\@ 
are  written  for  shortness  in  the  place  of  $\{1, 2, (1)\}$,  \&c.

\paragraph{27.}
It  is  clear  that  we  have  in  general
\begin{multline*}
\Delta^{-1} D^r H = \frac{1}{[n-1]^{\underline{1}}} \Bigg[
    [n-2]^0 [n-2]^r \, \{1, 2, (r+1)\} \\
  + [n-2]^1 [n-2]^{r-1} \, \{1, 3, (r)\} \\
  + \frac{[r]^{\underline{2}}}{[2]^{\underline{2}}} [n-2]^2 [n-2]^{r-2} \, \{1, 4, (r-1)\} \\
  + \cdots \\
  + [n-2]^r [n-2]^0 \, \{1, r+2, (1)\} \\
  + R_1'    [n-2]^1 [n-2]^{r-1} \, \{2, 3, (r-1)\} \\
  + R_2''   [n-2]^2 [n-2]^{r-2} \, \{2, 4, (r-2)\} \\
  + \cdots \\
  + R_{r-1}^{(r-1)} [n-2]^{r-1} [n-2]^1 \, \{2, r+1, (1)\} \\
  + R_1''    [n-2]^2 [n-2]^{r-2} \, \{3, 4, (r-3)\} \\
  + \cdots \\
  + R_{r-2}^{(r-2)} [n-2]^{r-2} [n-2]^2 \, \{3, r, (1)\} \\
  + \cdots \\
  + \begin{cases}
    \frac{1}{[\frac{1}{2}r]^{\underline{\frac{1}{2}r}}} [n-2]^{\frac{1}{2}r} [n-2]^{\frac{1}{2}r} \, \{r+1, r+2, (1)\}, & r \text{ even}; \text{ or} \\
    \frac{1}{[\frac{1}{2}(r-1)]^{\underline{\frac{1}{2}(r-1)}}} [n-2]^{\frac{1}{2}(r+1)} [n-2]^{\frac{1}{2}(r-1)} \, \{(r+1), (r+3), (2)\}, & r \text{ odd}
    \end{cases}
\Bigg],
\end{multline*}
and  the  general  term  is
\[
\frac{[n-1]^{\underline{1}} R_s^{\underline{S}} [n-2]^{S+1} [n-2]^{r-S-s}}{[S+1]^{\underline{S}}}
    \{S+1, S+2+s, (r-2S+1-s)\},
\]
where  $s$  extends  from  $s = 0$  to  $s = r - 2S$,  and  $S$  from  $S = 0$  to  $S = \frac{1}{2}r$  or  $\frac{1}{2}(r-1)$,  according
as  $r$  is  even  or  odd.     The  expression  for  the  coefficient  $R_1$  is
\[
R_1 = \frac{[r]^{\underline{s}}}{[s]^{\underline{s}} [r-s]^{\underline{s}}},
\]
and  that  of  the  other  coefficients  $R_s$ ($s = $ or $< 1$)  is  not  required  for  the  present  purpose.

\paragraph{28.}
According  to  a  remark  already  made,  the  expressions  for  $D^{n-1}H$, $D^{n-2}H$,  \&c.\@  are
at  once  obtained  from  that  for  $D^r H$  by  merely  writing  $n-1$, $n-2$,  \&c.\@  in  the  place
of  $n$:\@  it  is  however  to  be  noticed,  that  the  quantity  within  the  $[\ ]$  must  not  be
negative,  and  that  on  its  becoming  so,  the  factorial  is  to  be  omitted.

\newpage

\paragraph{29.}
I  write  now
\[
\mathrm{III} = \frac{1}{[n-1]^{\underline{1}}} \Delta^{-1} D^{n-2} H,
\]
and  I  consider  the  expression
\[
\frac{1}{[n-1]^{\underline{1}}} \Delta^{-1} D^{n-1} H \times \mathrm{III},
\]
the  general  term  of  which  is
\[
\frac{R_s^{\underline{S}} \{S+1, S+2+s, (r-2S+1-s)\}}
     {[n-1]^{\underline{1}} [n-2]^S [n-2]^{r-S}}
  \times [n-1]^{\underline{1}} \cdot \frac{[q]^{\underline{\alpha}} [q-\alpha]^{\underline{\beta}}}
     {[n-2]^\alpha [n-3]^\alpha [n-3]^\beta \cdots},
\]
or,  as  this  may  be  written,  putting  $q = n - S - 1$,
\begin{multline*}
R_s^{\underline{S}} \{S+1, S+2+s, (r-2S+1-s)\} \\
\times \frac{[n-2]^S [n-2]^{r-S}}
     {[n-1]^{\underline{1}} [n-2]^\alpha [n-3]^\alpha [n-3]^\beta \cdots} + \text{\&c.}
\end{multline*}

\paragraph{30.}
I  assume  $r > n - 2$;  we  have  then  $\alpha + \beta = r - S > n - S - 2$,  and  therefore
$\alpha + \beta < q$.     The  general  term  of  the  series  in  $\{\}$  is
\[
\pm \frac{[q]^{\underline{\alpha}} [q-\alpha]^{\underline{\beta}}}
     {[n-2-\alpha]^\beta [n-2-\alpha]^\beta \cdots},
\]
where  the  terms  for  which  $n - 2 - \alpha$  is  negative  are  to  be  excluded,  or  what  is  the
same  thing,  the  series  is  not  to  be  continued  beyond  $\alpha = n - 2$.  But  observing  that
$[q]^\alpha$  vanishes  for  $\alpha > q$,  that  is,  $\alpha > n - S - 1$,  it  is  in  fact  the  same  thing  whether  the
series  is  continued  indefinitely  or  only  to  the  term  for  which  $\alpha = n - S - 1$,  and  we
may  consistently  with  the  condition  $\alpha > n - 2$,  continue  the  series  as  far  as  $\alpha = n - S - 1$,
except  in  the  case  $S = 0$,  when  by  doing  so  we  include  the  term

\newpage

corresponding  to  $\alpha = n - 1$,  which  in  virtue  of  the  condition  ought  to  be  excluded.  The  expression  for
the  term  in  question  is  $(-)^{n-1} [-1]^r [-1]^\beta$;  hence  if  the  sum  of  the  series  continued
to  the  proper  point  is  $\mathcal{S}$,  the  sum  continued  indefinitely  (in  the  particular  case  $S = 0$)
is  $\mathcal{S} + (-)^{n-1} [-1]^r [-1]^\beta$,  but  in  every  other  case  the  sum  continued  indefinitely  is
simply  $\mathcal{S}$.  And  by  a  well-known  theorem  in  finite  differences,  the  sum  continued
indefinitely  is  in  fact  zero.  That  is,  except  in  the  case  $S = 0$,  we  have  $\mathcal{S} = 0$,  but  in  the
excepted  case
\[
\mathcal{S} + (-)^{n-1} [-1]^r [-1]^\beta = 0;
\]
or  observing  that  $\alpha + \beta (= r - S)$  is  in  this  case  $= r$,  and  transforming  the  factorials,
we  have
\[
\mathcal{S} = -(-)^{n-1} (-)^{r} [n]^r [r-n]^{\underline{\beta}};
\]
or  substituting  for  $\alpha$  and  $\beta$  their  values,
\[
\mathcal{S} = (-)^{n-r-1} [n]^r [r-n]^{r-n}.
\]

\paragraph{31.}
Hence  the  general  term  of
\[
\frac{1}{[n-1]^{\underline{1}}} \Delta^{-1} D^{n-1} H \times \mathrm{III}
\]
vanishes  except  for  $S = 0$,  but  when  $S = 0$,  its  value  is
\[
R_s \{1, 2+s, (r+1-s)\} \times (-)^{n-r-1} [n]^r [r-n]^{r-n};
\]
or  observing  that  $R_s$  is  equal  to  $[r]^{\underline{s}} \div [s]^{\underline{s}} [r-s]^{\underline{s}}$,  the  value  is  simply
\[
(-)^{n-r-1} [n]^r \{1, 2+s, (r+1-s)\},
\]
that  is,  we  have
\[
\frac{1}{[n-1]^{\underline{1}}} \Delta^{-1} D^{n-1} H \times \mathrm{III}
= (-)^{n-r-1} [n]^r \sum_s \{1, 2+s, (r+1-s)\},
\]
the  summation   in  respect  to   $s$  extending  from   $s = 0$  to  $s = r$.      In  particular,   giving  to
$r$  the  values  $n-2$  and  $n-1$,  and  attending  to  the  expressions  for  $\mathrm{III}$  and  $\mathrm{IV}$,  we  find
\begin{align*}
\Delta^n (D^{n-2} H - \&c.) \cdot \mathrm{III} &= -[n-2]^{\underline{n-2}} \, \mathrm{III}, \\
\Delta^n (D^{n-1} H - \&c.) \cdot \mathrm{III} &= -[n-3]^{\underline{n-3}} \, \mathrm{IV}.
\end{align*}

\paragraph{32.}
The  equation  $\mathrm{III} = 0$  belongs  to  the  curve  which  by  its  intersections  with  the
tangent,  gives  the  tangentials  of  a  point  of  the  curve  $U = 0$.  Hence  the  equation  of
the  curve  in  question  is
\[
D^{n-2} H - \&c. = 0,
\]
which   is    Mr   Salmon's    theorem,   leading    to    the    solution    of    the    problem    of   double
tangents.

\paragraph{33.}
The  expressions  for  $\mathrm{I}$  and  $\mathrm{II}$  are  obtained  from  those  of  $\mathrm{IV}$  and  $\mathrm{III}$  by
interchanging  $(X, Y, Z)$  and  $(x, y, z)$,  and  reversing  the  sign.  Hence  if,  as  before,
$\mathrm{I}_1$, $\mathrm{II}_1$,  \&c.\@  denote  the  values  which  $\mathrm{I}$, $\mathrm{II}$,  \&c.\@  assume  by  this  interchange,  we  have
\begin{align*}
\Delta^n (\mathrm{I}_1 - \&c.) &= [n-2]^{\underline{n-2}} \, \mathrm{II}, \\
\Delta^n (\mathrm{II}_1 - \&c.) &= [n-3]^{\underline{n-3}} \, \mathrm{I},
\end{align*}
and  the  identical  equation
\[
a_0 \, \mathrm{I} + a_1 \, \mathrm{II} + a_{n-1} \, \mathrm{III} + a_n \, \mathrm{IV} = 0
\]

\newpage

becomes  therefore
\[
(D^{n-2} H - \&c.) \, \mathrm{T} = 0,
\]
which  is  the  general    identical   equation  referred  to   in   the  introduction  to  the  present
memoir.

\paragraph{34.}
It  is  to  be  noticed  that  for  $n = 3$,  the  equation  is
\[
(9 \Delta H - 2 D \mathrm{I}_1) U + (\mathrm{I}_1^2 - 2 \Delta^2 \mathrm{II}_1) \, DU - \Delta (DH - 3 D\mathrm{II}_1) \mathrm{T} - (\Delta H - 3 \mathrm{II}_1) \mathrm{T} = 0.
\]
But  we  have  $\mathrm{II}_1 = \mathrm{H}$,  and  in  like  manner  $\mathrm{I}_1 = \Delta$,  and  the  equation  thus  becomes
\[
\Delta \, DU + \mathrm{I}_1^2 \, DU - \Delta^2 D\mathrm{T} + \mathrm{H} \, \mathrm{T} = 0.
\]
And  so  also  for  $n = 4$,  the  equation  is
\begin{multline*}
(D^2 H - 3 D\mathrm{II}_1) \, U + (\mathrm{I}_1^2 - 3 \Delta^2 \mathrm{II}_1) \, DU \\
- \Delta (D^2 H - 3 D\mathrm{II}_1) \, \mathrm{T} - (DH - 3 \Delta H) \, \mathrm{T} = 0.
\end{multline*}
But  we  have  in  general  $D\mathrm{II}_1 = \frac{n-1}{2} DH$,  and  therefore  in  the  present  case  $D\mathrm{II}_1 = \frac{3}{2} DH$,
and   consequently   $D^2 \mathrm{II}_1 = \frac{3}{2} D^2 H$,  and  the  equation  thus  becomes
\[
D^2 H \cdot U + (\mathrm{I}_1^2 - 3 \Delta^2 \mathrm{II}_1) \, DU - \Delta (D^2 H - 3 \Delta^2 D^2 H) \, \mathrm{T} + DH \cdot \mathrm{T} = 0,
\]
which  agrees  with  the  results  previously  obtained  for  the  two  particular  cases.


\newpage
\section*{261. On the Conic of Five-Pointic Contact at any Point of a Plane Curve.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{261. On the Conic of Five-Pointic Contact at any Point of a Plane Curve}
\label{sec:paper261}

\noindent
[From the \textit{Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London}, vol.~CXLIX.~(for  the
year  1859),  pp.~371--400.     Received  March  1,---Read  March  24,  1859.]

\medskip

\noindent\textsc{I.~Investigation of the Equation of the Conic of Five-pointic Contact.}

\medskip

The  tangent  is  a  line  passing  through  two  consecutive  points  of  a  plane  curve,
and  we  may  in  like  manner  consider  the  conic  which  passes  through  five  consecutive
points  of  a  plane  curve;  and  as  there  are  certain  singular  points,  viz.  the  points  of
inflexion  where  three  consecutive  points  of  the  curve  lie  in  a  line,  so  there  are
singular  points  where  six  consecutive  points  of  the  curve  lie  in  a  conic.  In  the
particular  case  where  the  given  curve  is  a  cubic,  the  last-mentioned  species  of  singular
points  have  been  considered  by  Pl\"ucker  and  Steiner,  and  in  the  same  particular  case,
the  theory  of  the  conic  of  five-pointic  contact  has  recently  been  established  by  Mr~Salmon.
But  the  general  case,  where  the  curve  is  of  any  order  whatever,  has  not  so  far  as
I  am  aware  been  hitherto  considered;---the  establishment  of  this  theory  is  the  object
of  the  present  memoir.

\paragraph{1.}
I  take  $(X, Y, Z)$  as  current  coordinates,  and  I  represent  the  equation  of  the
given  curve  by
\[
\mathrm{U} = (\\bullet\!\!\wr\, x, Y, Z)^n = 0.
\]
Let  $(x, y, z)$  be  the  coordinates  of  a  given  point  on  the  curve,  and  let  $U = (\\bullet\!\!\wr\, x, y, z)^n$
be  what  $\mathrm{U}$  becomes  when  $(x, y, z)$  are  written  in  the  place  of  $(X, Y, Z)$;  we  have
therefore  $U = 0$  as  a  condition  satisfied  by  the  coordinates  of  the  point  in  question.

\paragraph{2.}
Write  for  shortness
\begin{align*}
DU &= (X\partial_x + Y\partial_y + Z\partial_z) \, U, \\
D^2 U &= (X\partial_x + Y\partial_y + Z\partial_z)^2 \, U,
\end{align*}
and  let  $\nabla = aX + bY + cZ = 0$  be  the  equation  of  an  arbitrary  line;  then  if  in  the  equation
of  the  given  curve  we  write  $X + \lambda a$,  $Y + \lambda b$,  $Z + \lambda c$  in  the  place  of  $X$,  $Y$,  $Z$
respectively,  and  write  $\lambda = -\nabla / (a\partial_x + b\partial_y + c\partial_z)$,  the  result  is
\[
\mathrm{U} - \frac{\nabla}{a\partial_x + b\partial_y + c\partial_z} \, DU
  + \frac{\nabla^2}{(a\partial_x + b\partial_y + c\partial_z)^2} \, D^2 U - \&c. = 0,
\]
which  is  the  equation  of  the  given  curve  referred  to  the  line  $\nabla = 0$  as  a  line  of  coordinates.

\paragraph{3.}
The  equation  of  a  conic  passing  through  the  point  $(x, y, z)$  is
\[
(A, B, C, F, G, H \,\!\!\wr\, X, Y, Z)^2 = 0,
\]
where  the  coefficients  are  such  that
\[
(A, B, C, F, G, H \,\!\!\wr\, x, y, z)^2 = 0.
\]
The  condition  that  the  conic  shall  have  five-pointic  contact  with  the  given  curve  at  the
point  $(x, y, z)$  gives  rise  to  a  system  of  equations  which  determine  the  ratios  of  the
coefficients  $A : B : C : F : G : H$.


\paragraph{4.}
Consider  the  coordinates  of  a  point  of  the  curve  as  functions  of  a  single
variable  parameter;  then  for  the  present  purpose  the  coordinates  of  a  point  consecutive
to  $(x, y, z)$  may  be  taken  to  be
\begin{align*}
&x + dx + \tfrac{1}{2}d^2x + \tfrac{1}{6}d^3x + \tfrac{1}{24}d^4x, \\
&y + dy + \tfrac{1}{2}d^2y + \tfrac{1}{6}d^3y + \tfrac{1}{24}d^4y, \\
&z + dz + \tfrac{1}{2}d^2z + \tfrac{1}{6}d^3z + \tfrac{1}{24}d^4z,
\end{align*}
values  which,  substituted  for  $X$,  $Y$,  $Z$,  must  satisfy  the  equations
\[
\mathrm{U} = 0, \quad D^2 U - \mathrm{II} \cdot DU = 0.
\]

I  write  for  shortness
\begin{align*}
d_1 &= dx\, \partial_x + dy\, \partial_y + dz\, \partial_z, \\
d_2 &= d^2x\, \partial_x + d^2y\, \partial_y + d^2z\, \partial_z, \\
d_3 &= d^3x\, \partial_x + d^3y\, \partial_y + d^3z\, \partial_z, \\
d_4 &= d^4x\, \partial_x + d^4y\, \partial_y + d^4z\, \partial_z,
\end{align*}
then  the  consecutive  value  of  $U$  is
\[
e^{d_1 + \frac{1}{2}d_2 + \frac{1}{6}d_3 + \frac{1}{24}d_4} \, U,
\]
(Read  $\exp.\ z$,  exponential  of  $z$,  $= e^z$),  which  is
\begin{multline*}
\left(1 + d_1 + \tfrac{1}{2}d_1^2 + \tfrac{1}{6}d_1^3 + \tfrac{1}{24}d_1^4\right) U \\
+ \tfrac{1}{2}d_2 \left(1 + d_1 + \tfrac{1}{2}d_1^2\right) U \\
+ \tfrac{1}{6}d_3 (1 + d_1) U \\
+ \tfrac{1}{24}d_4 U,
\end{multline*}
or,  what  is  the  same  thing,
\begin{multline*}
U + d_1 U + \tfrac{1}{2}(d_1^2 + d_2) U + \tfrac{1}{6}(d_1^3 + 3d_1 d_2 + d_3) U \\
+ \tfrac{1}{24}(d_1^4 + 6d_1^2 d_2 + 4d_1 d_3 + 3d_2^2 + d_4) U,
\end{multline*}
the  several  terms  of  which  must  respectively  vanish,  and  we  have  therefore
\begin{align*}
U &= 0, \\
d_1 U &= 0, \\
d_1^2 U &= -(d_1^2 + 2d_2) U, \\
d_1^3 U &= -(d_1^3 + 3d_1 d_2 + d_3) U.
\end{align*}

\paragraph{5.}
Next,  preparing  to  substitute  in  the  equation
\[
D^2 U - \mathrm{II} \cdot DU = 0,
\]
the  consecutive  value  of  $DU$  is
\[
(x + dx + \tfrac{1}{2}d^2x + \tfrac{1}{6}d^3x + \tfrac{1}{24}d^4x) \, \partial_x U + \&c.,
\]
where
\[
(x + dx + \tfrac{1}{2}d^2x + \tfrac{1}{6}d^3x + \tfrac{1}{24}d^4x) \, \partial_x U + \&c.
\]
Reducing  by  the  above  results,  the  consecutive  value  of  $DU$  is
\begin{multline*}
= d_1 U + \tfrac{1}{2}(d_1^2 + d_2) U + \tfrac{1}{6}(d_1^3 + 3d_1 d_2 + d_3) U \\
+ \tfrac{1}{24}(d_1^4 + 6d_1^2 d_2 + 4d_1 d_3 + 3d_2^2 + d_4) U.
\end{multline*}

\newpage

Hence  also  writing
\begin{align*}
P &= ax + by + cz, \\
d_1 P &= a\, dx + b\, dy + c\, dz, \\
d_2 P &= a\, d^2x + b\, d^2y + c\, d^2z,
\end{align*}
the   consecutive   value   of  $-\mathrm{II} \cdot DU$  is   $-(P + d_1 P + \tfrac{1}{2}d_2 P)$  multiplied   into   the  consecutive
value  of  $DU$,  and  the  product  is
\begin{multline*}
= P \cdot d_1 U \\
+ P \cdot \tfrac{1}{2}(d_1^2 + d_2) U + d_1 P \cdot d_1 U \\
+ P \cdot \tfrac{1}{6}(d_1^3 + 3d_1 d_2 + d_3) U + d_1 P \cdot \tfrac{1}{2}(d_1^2 + d_2) U + \tfrac{1}{2}d_2 P \cdot d_1 U.
\end{multline*}

\paragraph{7.}
The  consecutive  value  of  $D^2 U$  is
\begin{multline*}
(x + dx + \tfrac{1}{2}d^2x + \tfrac{1}{6}d^3x + \tfrac{1}{24}d^4x)^2 \, \partial_x^2 U + \&c., \\
= d_1^2 U + d_1(d_1^2 + d_2) U + \&c.
\end{multline*}


\paragraph{8.}
Substituting   in  the  equation  $D^2 U - \mathrm{II} \cdot DU = 0$,  we   obtain   as   the   conditions  of
a  five-pointic  contact
\begin{align*}
&-[(m-1)a_1^2 + 3(m-2)a_1 a_2] \, U + P \cdot \tfrac{1}{2}(d_1^2 + 3d_1 d_2) \, U + d_1 P \cdot d_1^2 U = 0, \\
&-[(m-1)(a_1^3 + 6a_1^2 a_2) + (m-2)(4a_1 a_3 + 3a_2^2)] \, U \\
&\quad + P \cdot \tfrac{1}{6}(d_1^3 + 6d_1^2 d_2 + 4d_1 d_3 + 3d_2^2) \, U + d_1 P \cdot \tfrac{1}{2}(d_1^2 + 3d_1 d_2) \, U + \tfrac{1}{2}d_2 P \cdot d_1^2 U = 0;
\end{align*}
or  reducing
\[
P = 2(m-2) \frac{d_1^2 U}{d_1^2 U},
\]
\begin{align*}
d_1 P &= \frac{[(m-1)a_1^3 + 6a_1^2 a_2] \, U}{d_1^2 U} - \frac{P \cdot [a_1^3 + 3a_1^2 a_2] \, U}{d_1^2 U}, \\
d_2 P &= \frac{[(m-1)(a_1^3 + 6a_1^2 a_2) + (m-2)(4a_1 a_3 + 3a_2^2)] \, U}{d_1^2 U} \\
&\quad - \frac{d_1 P \cdot [a_1^3 + 3a_1^2 a_2] \, U}{d_1^2 U} - \frac{P \cdot [a_1^3 + 6a_1^2 a_2 + 4a_1 a_3 + 3a_2^2] \, U}{d_1^2 U},
\end{align*}
which  are  the  conditions  of  a  five-pointic  contact:  it  is  to  be  remarked  that  if  only
the  first  and  second  conditions  are  satisfied,  we  have  a  four-pointic  contact,  and  if  only
the  first  condition  is  satisfied,  a  three-pointic  contact.

\paragraph{9.}
We   have  to   reduce   the   last-mentioned    equations ;    suppose   that   $A$,   $B$,  $C$  are
the  first  derived  functions  of  $U$,  then  the  equation  $d_1 U = 0$  may  be  written
\[
A\, dx + B\, dy + C\, dz = 0,
\]
and  this  will  be  satisfied  identically  if
\begin{align*}
dx &= B\nu - C\mu, \\
dy &= C\lambda - A\nu, \\
dz &= A\mu - B\lambda,
\end{align*}
where  $\lambda$,  $\mu$,  $\nu$  are  arbitrary  multipliers,  which  may  be   taken   to   be   constants.     We  have
therefore  $d_1 = \mathfrak{D}$,  where
\[
\mathfrak{D} = (B\nu - C\mu) \, \partial_x + (C\lambda - A\nu) \, \partial_y + (A\mu - B\lambda) \, \partial_z.
\]

\paragraph{10.}
The  resulting  expressions   for  $d_1^2 U$,  $d_1^3 U$,  $d_1^4 U$  may  be  exhibited  in  the  reduced
forms  given  by  Hesse,  viz.  if  $\varpi = \lambda x + \mu y + \nu z$,  we  have
\[
d_1^2 U = P_2 U - Q_2 \varpi^{m-2}, \quad d_1^3 U = P_3 U - Q_3 \varpi^{m-3}, \quad d_1^4 U = P_4 U - Q_4 \varpi^{m-4},
\]
where   the   values   of  $P_2$,  $P_3$,  $P_4$;   $Q_2$,  $Q_3$,  $Q_4$   are   as  follows,   viz.   if  $(a, b, c, f, g, h)$  are
the  second  derived  functions  of  $U$,  and  if
\[
H = \begin{vmatrix} a & h & g \\ h & b & f \\ g & f & c \end{vmatrix}
\]
be  the  Hessian ;   if,  moreover,
\[
\Phi = -\begin{vmatrix} 0 & \lambda & \mu & \nu \\ \lambda & a & h & g \\ \mu & h & b & f \\ \nu & g & f & c \end{vmatrix}
\]
be  the  bordered  Hessian  (we  may  also  write  $\Phi = (\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \,\!\!\wr\, \lambda, \mu, \nu)^2$,  where
$(\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H})$  are  the  inverse  coefficients  of  $(a, b, c, f, g, h)$,  viz.  $\mathfrak{A} = bc - f^2$,  \&c.);  
and  finally,  if  for  shortness  we  write
\[
\Omega = \partial_\lambda \Phi \cdot \partial_\mu \Phi + \partial_\mu \Phi \cdot \partial_\nu \Phi + \partial_\nu \Phi \cdot \partial_\lambda \Phi,
\]
then  we  have
\begin{align*}
P_2 &= \frac{m-1}{m} \mathfrak{D} \Phi, \\
P_3 &= \frac{(m-1)^2}{m^2} \mathfrak{D}^2 \Phi - \frac{(m-1)(m-2)}{m^2} \Omega, \\
P_4 &= \frac{(m-1)^3}{m^3} \mathfrak{D}^3 \Phi - \frac{3(m-1)^2(m-2)}{m^3} \mathfrak{D} \Omega + \frac{(m-1)(m-2)}{m^3} \Psi,
\end{align*}
where  $\Psi$  is  a  certain  function  of  the  third  derivatives  of  $U$.

\newpage

In  the  present  case  $\varpi = 0$,  and  we  have
\[
d_1^2 U = P_2 U, \quad d_1^3 U = P_3 U, \quad d_1^4 U = P_4 U.
\]

\paragraph{11.}
Hence,  substituting   for   $Q_2$  and  $Q_3$  their  values,   the  first  and    second   of   the
equations  for  the  five-pointic  contact  give
\[
P = 2(m-2) \frac{P_2 U}{d_1^2 U},
\]
and  observing  that  $\mathrm{II}$  is  a  linear  function  of  $(X, Y, Z)$,  and  consequently  that  $P$,  $d_1 P$
denote  simply  the  values  which  $\mathrm{II}$  assumes  when  $(x, y, z)$,  $(dx, dy, dz)$  are  respectively
substituted  for  $(X, Y, Z)$,  we  see  at  once  that  these  two  conditions  will  be  satisfied  if
we  put
\[
\mathrm{II} = \Lambda \cdot \Phi,
\]
where  $\Lambda$  is  an  arbitrary  constant,  or,  what  is  the  same  thing,  an  arbitrary  function  of
$(x, y, z)$.     We  have  thus  the  general  equation  of  a  conic  of  four-pointic  contact.

\paragraph{12.}
The  above  value  of  $\mathrm{II}$  gives
\[
P = \Lambda \cdot \Phi(x, y, z), \quad d_1 P = \Lambda \cdot \mathfrak{D} \Phi, \quad d_2 P = \Lambda \cdot d_2 \Phi,
\]
and  the  third  equation  of  the  system  of  conditions  for  a  five-pointic  contact  is  therefore
\[
-[(m-1)(a_1^3 + 6a_1^2 a_2) + (m-2)(4a_1 a_3 + 3a_2^2)] \, U + \Lambda \cdot [\text{expr.}] = 0,
\]
which  leads  to  the  value  of  $\Lambda$.


\paragraph{15.}
We  have  $R_2 = H$,  and  it  will  be  shown  that
\begin{align*}
(m-1)^2 R_3 &= -3(m-2)^2 H \cdot \Phi + 4(m-2) \Omega, \\
(m-1)^2 R_4 &= -12(m-2)^2 \Omega \cdot \Phi + 4(m-2) \Psi,
\end{align*}
where  for  shortness
\[
\Omega = (\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \,\!\!\wr\, \partial_\lambda, \partial_\mu, \partial_\nu)^2 H,
\]
and  hence,  writing
\[
\Lambda = \frac{9H^2 \cdot A}{(m-1)^2},
\]
we  have
\begin{align*}
9H^2 A &= 3R_3 \cdot (m-1)^2 = R_4 - 4(m-1)^2 R_2^2, \\
9H^2 A &= -3\Omega \cdot \Phi + 4\Psi;
\end{align*}
or  replacing  $H$,  $\Phi$  by  their  values,
\[
9H^2 A = 3\Omega \cdot \Phi - 4(\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \,\!\!\wr\, \partial_\lambda H, \partial_\mu H, \partial_\nu H)^2,
\]
and  $\Lambda$  having  this  value,  the  equation  of  the  five-pointic  conic  is
\[
D^2 U - \frac{1}{2(m-2)} \left(DH + \Lambda \cdot DU\right) DU = 0,
\]
where  it  will  be  recollected  that  the  current  coordinates  are  $(X, Y, Z)$,  and  that  $D$
denotes  $X\partial_x + Y\partial_y + Z\partial_z$.

\paragraph{16.}
I  remark,  in  passing,  that  the  problem  of  finding  the  circle  of  curvature  at
a  given  point  of  a  plane  curve,  is  in  fact  that  of  determining  the  conic  having  with
the  curve  at  the  given  point  a  three-pointic  contact,  and  besides  passing  through  two
given  points.     The  equation  of  a  conic  having  an  ordinary  contact,  is
\[
D^2 U - \mathrm{II} \cdot DU = 0,
\]
where
\[
\mathrm{II} = aX + bY + cZ,
\]
and  the  condition  of  a  three-pointic  contact  is
\[
ax + by + cz = 2(m-2).
\]
Let  the  coordinates  of  the  two  given  points  be
\[
(x_1, y_1, z_1), \quad (x_2, y_2, z_2),
\]
and  let  $(D^2 U)_1$,  \&c.\@  be  the  corresponding  values  of  $D^2 U$,  \&c.,  then  we  have
\begin{align*}
aX + bY + cZ &= \mathrm{II}, \\
ax + by + cz &= 2(m-2), \\
ax_1 + by_1 + cz_1 &= \frac{(D^2 U)_1}{(DU)_1}, \\
ax_2 + by_2 + cz_2 &= \frac{(D^2 U)_2}{(DU)_2},
\end{align*}
and  if  from  the  four  equations  we  eliminate  $a$,  $b$,  $c$,  we  find
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
XDU & YDU & ZDU & D^2 U \\
x & y & z & 2(m-2) \\
x_1 & y_1 & z_1 & (D^2 U)_1/(DU)_1 \\
x_2 & y_2 & z_2 & (D^2 U)_2/(DU)_2 \\
\end{vmatrix} = 0
\]
for  the   equation   of   the  conic  in   question;   $(x, y, z)$   being   the   coordinates   of  the   point
of  contact,  and  $(X, Y, Z)$  current  coordinates.

\newpage
\noindent\textsc{II.~Demonstration of Identities assumed in the preceding Section.}

\medskip

\noindent\textit{Proof of the expressions for $(d_1^3 + 3d_1 d_2)U$ and $(d_1^4 + 6d_1^2 d_2 + 4d_1 d_3 + 3d_2^2)U$:}

\paragraph{17.}
It  will  be  remembered  that  $d_1$,  $d_2$  stand  originally  for
\[
dx\, \partial_x + dy\, \partial_y + dz\, \partial_z,
\]
\[
d^2x\, \partial_x + d^2y\, \partial_y + d^2z\, \partial_z,
\]
and  that  $A$,  $B$,  $C$  being  the  first  derived  functions  of  $U$,  $dx$,  $dy$,  $dz$  are  changed  into
\[
B\nu - C\mu, \quad C\lambda - A\nu, \quad A\mu - B\lambda,
\]
and  that  the  resulting  value  of  $d_1$,  viz.
\[
(B\nu - C\mu) \partial_x + (C\lambda - A\nu) \partial_y + (A\mu - B\lambda) \partial_z
\]
is  also  represented  by  $\mathfrak{D}$,  so  that  $d_1 = \mathfrak{D}$.     The  corresponding  values  of  $d^2x$,  $d^2y$,  $d^2z$  are
\begin{align*}
d^2x &= \nu\, dB - \mu\, dC, \\
d^2y &= \lambda\, dC - \nu\, dA, \\
d^2z &= \mu\, dA - \lambda\, dB,
\end{align*}
where  we  have
\[
dA = a\, dx + h\, dy + g\, dz, \quad \&c.,
\]
in  which  $dx$,  $dy$,  $dz$  are  to  be  replaced  by  the  values
\[
B\nu - C\mu, \quad C\lambda - A\nu, \quad A\mu - B\lambda;
\]
and  $d_2$  really  denotes  what
\[
d^2x\, \partial_x + d^2y\, \partial_y + d^2z\, \partial_z
\]
becomes  when  the  above  values  are  substituted  for  $d^2x$,  $d^2y$,  $d^2z$.  But  in  the  expressions
$d_1^2 U$,  $d_1 d_2 U$  \&c.,  the  symbols  $\partial_x$,  $\partial_y$,  $\partial_z$  contained  in  $d_1$  and  $d_2$  operate  only  on  $U$,  and
not  on  the  variable  quantities  $A$,  $B$,  $C$,  \&c.\@  contained  in  $d_1$  and  $d_2$.


\paragraph{18.}
If  now  we  treat  $d_2$  as  an  operand,  that  is,  perform  the  differentiations  on  the
variable  quantities  $A$,  $B$,  $C$  which  enter  into  $d_2$,  we  obtain
\[
d_2(d_1 U) = d_2(d_1 U) + (d_2 d_1)U,
\]
or,  what  is  the  same  thing,  operating  on  $d_1 U$  with  $d_2$,  the  result  is
\[
d_1 d_2 U + (d_2 d_1)U,
\]
and  in  like  manner
\[
d_1^2 U = d_1^2 U + 2(d_2 d_1)U + (d_3 d_1)U.
\]
It  is,  in  fact,  upon  these  principles  that  Hesse's  values  of  $d_1^2 U$,  $d_1^3 U$,  \&c.\@  were  obtained,
and  we  may  by  means  of  them  obtain  the  other  expressions  assumed  in  the  preceding
section.

\paragraph{19.}
In  fact,  starting  from  Hesse's  equation,
\[
d_1^2 U = P_2 U - Q_2 \varpi^{m-2},
\]
we   have
\[
\frac{1}{m-1}(d_1^3 + 3d_1 d_2)U = -\frac{1}{(m-1)^2}(U \mathfrak{D} \Phi + \Phi \mathfrak{D} U) - \frac{1}{(m-1)^2}(DH \cdot \Phi + H \cdot \mathfrak{D} \Phi).
\]
But  we  have  identically  $\mathfrak{D} U = 0$,  $\mathfrak{D}^2 U = 0$,  and  this  equation  becomes  therefore
\[
\frac{1}{m-1}(d_1^3 + 3d_1 d_2)U = -\frac{1}{(m-1)^2}(U \mathfrak{D} \Phi + \Phi \cdot 0) - \frac{1}{(m-1)^2}(DH \cdot \Phi + H \cdot \mathfrak{D} \Phi);
\]
this  is  precisely  Hesse's  value  of  $d_1^3 U$,  and  thus  we  have  $\mathfrak{D} \Delta U = 0$,  and  therefore
\[
\frac{1}{m-1}(d_1^3 + 3d_1 d_2)U = -\frac{1}{(m-1)^2} H \cdot \mathfrak{D} \Phi.
\]

\paragraph{20.}
In  like  manner,  starting  from  the  expression  of  $d_1^3 U$,  we  have
\[
\frac{1}{m-1}(d_1^4 + 6d_1^2 d_2 + 4d_1 d_3 + 3d_2^2)U = \text{[expression involving  } \mathfrak{D}^2 \Phi, \Omega, \Psi\text{]},
\]
or    since    $DU$    and    $D^2 U$    vanish    identically,    and    the    values    of    $D \cdot D\Phi$,    $D \cdot DH$    are
$d_1^3 + D^2 \Phi$,  $d_1 \cdot H + D^2 H$,  we  have
\[
\text{[complicated  expression]}
\]
and  if  from  the  double  of  this  equation  we  subtract  Hesse's  equation,
\[
\frac{1}{m-1} d_1^4 U = P_4 U - Q_4 \varpi^{m-4},
\]
we  find  the  required  relation,
\[
\frac{1}{m-1}(d_1^4 + 6d_1^2 d_2 + 4d_1 d_3 + 3d_2^2)U = -\frac{3}{(m-1)^2} \Omega \cdot \Phi + \frac{4}{(m-1)^2} \Psi.
\]

\newpage

\noindent\textit{Proof of the expression for $\mathfrak{D}^2 H$:}

\paragraph{21.}
We   have
\[
\mathfrak{D}^2 H = \partial_x H \cdot (\nu\, dB - \mu\, dC) + \partial_y H \cdot (\lambda\, dC - \nu\, dA) + \partial_z H \cdot (\mu\, dA - \lambda\, dB),
\]
where
\begin{align*}
dA &= a\, dx + h\, dy + g\, dz, \\
dB &= h\, dx + b\, dy + f\, dz, \\
dC &= g\, dx + f\, dy + c\, dz,
\end{align*}
in  which  $dx$,  $dy$,  $dz$  are  to  be  replaced  by  their  values:   we  have  therefore
\begin{multline*}
\mathfrak{D}^2 H = \partial_x H \cdot \{\nu[(bC-fB)\lambda + (fA-hC)\mu + (hB-bA)\nu] \\
- \mu[(fC-cB)\lambda + (cA-gC)\mu + (gB-fA)\nu]\} + \&c.,
\end{multline*}
where  the  coefficient  of  $\partial_x H$  is
\begin{multline*}
\mathfrak{A}\lambda^2 + (gC-cA)\mu^2 + (hB-bA)\nu^2 - (2fA-gB-hC)\mu\nu \\
+ (bC-fB)\nu\lambda + (cB-fC)\lambda\mu;
\end{multline*}
or,  since  we  have
\begin{align*}
(m-1)A &= ax + hy + gz, \\
(m-1)B &= hx + by + fz, \\
(m-1)C &= gx + fy + cz,
\end{align*}
the  coefficient,  omitting  the  factor  $\frac{1}{(m-1)^2}$,  which  will  be  afterwards  restored,  is
\begin{align*}
&+ [g(gx+fy+cz)-c(ax+hy+gz)]\mu^2 \\
&+ [h(hx+by+fz)-b(ax+hy+gz)]\nu^2 \\
&+ [2f(ax+hy+gz)-g(hx+by+fz)-h(gx+fy+cz)]\mu\nu \\
&+ [b(gx+fy+cz)-f(hx+by+fz)]\nu\lambda \\
&+ [c(hx+by+fz)-f(gx+fy+cz)]\lambda\mu \\
&= \mathfrak{A}\lambda^2 + (-\mathfrak{G}x + \mathfrak{B}y)\mu^2 + (-\mathfrak{F}x + \mathfrak{C}z)\nu^2 \\
&\quad + (-2\mathfrak{G}x + \mathfrak{B}y + \mathfrak{C}z)\mu\nu + (-\mathfrak{F}x + \mathfrak{B}z)\nu\lambda + (-\mathfrak{G}x + \mathfrak{A}y)\lambda\mu,
\end{align*}
which  is  equal  to
\[
-(\mathfrak{A}\mu\nu + \mathfrak{B}\nu\lambda + \mathfrak{C}\lambda\mu + 2\mathfrak{F}\lambda\mu + 2\mathfrak{G}\mu\nu + 2\mathfrak{H}\nu\lambda)x
+ (2\mathfrak{A}\lambda + \mathfrak{B}\mu + \mathfrak{G}\nu)(\lambda x + \mu y + \nu z).
\]
The  coefficients  of  $\partial_y H$,  $\partial_z H$  have  a  similar  form ;  and  uniting  the  three  terms,
observing   that  $x\partial_x H + y\partial_y H + z\partial_z H = 3(m-2)H$,  and   attending  to  the  values
of  $\mathfrak{A}$,  $\mathfrak{B}$,  $\mathfrak{C}$,  we  have,  restoring  the  omitted  factor  $\frac{1}{(m-1)^2}$,
\[
\mathfrak{D}^2 H = -\frac{1}{(m-1)^2}(\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \,\!\!\wr\, \partial_x, \partial_y, \partial_z)^2 H + \frac{3(m-2)}{(m-1)^2} H \cdot \Phi.
\]


\paragraph{22.}
These  are  obtained  (for  the  particular  case  $m = 4$,  which  makes  but  little
difference)  in  Mr~Salmon's  \textit{Higher  Plane  Curves},  pp.~88  and  89,  and  I  merely  reproduce
his  investigation ;    we  have
\[
(\mathfrak{D} H)^2 = \{(B\nu - C\mu)\partial_x H + (C\lambda - A\nu)\partial_y H + (A\mu - B\lambda)\partial_z H\}^2,
\]
or,  what  is  the  same  thing,
\[
(\mathfrak{D} H)^2 = \{\lambda(C\partial_y - B\partial_z)H + \mu(A\partial_z - C\partial_x)H + \nu(B\partial_x - A\partial_y)H\}^2;
\]
and  if  we  consider  first  the  term  which  contains  $\lambda^2$,  the  coefficient  is
\[
\{(C\partial_y - B\partial_z)H\}^2.
\]
Now  making  use  of  the  equations
\begin{align*}
(m-1)A &= ax + hy + gz, \\
(m-1)B &= hx + by + fz, \\
(m-1)C &= gx + fy + cz,
\end{align*}
and
\[
m(m-1)U = ax^2 + by^2 + cz^2 + 2fyz + 2gzx + 2hxy = 0,
\]
we   have
\begin{align*}
(m-1)^2 C^2 &= (gx + fy + cz)^2 - c(ax^2 + by^2 + cz^2 + 2fyz + 2gzx + 2hxy) \\
&= -\mathfrak{A}x^2 + 2\mathfrak{H}xy - \mathfrak{B}y^2, \\
(m-1)^2 BC &= (hx + by + fz)(gx + fy + cz) - f(ax^2 + by^2 + cz^2 + 2fyz + 2gzx + 2hxy) \\
&= \mathfrak{H}x^2 - \mathfrak{G}xy - \mathfrak{F}xz + \mathfrak{C}yz, \\
(m-1)^2 B^2 &= (hx + by + fz)^2 - b(ax^2 + by^2 + cz^2 + 2fyz + 2gzx + 2hxy) \\
&= -\mathfrak{G}x^2 + 2\mathfrak{F}xz - \mathfrak{C}z^2;
\end{align*}
and  hence
\begin{multline*}
(m-1)^2 \{(C\partial_y - B\partial_z)H\}^2 = (-\mathfrak{A}x^2 + 2\mathfrak{H}xy - \mathfrak{B}y^2)(\partial_y H)^2 \\
+ 2(-\mathfrak{G}x^2 + \mathfrak{H}xy + \mathfrak{F}xz - \mathfrak{C}yz) \partial_y H \cdot \partial_z H \\
+ (-\mathfrak{G}x^2 + 2\mathfrak{F}xz - \mathfrak{C}z^2)(\partial_z H)^2,
\end{multline*}
which  is  equal  to
\begin{multline*}
-x^2 (\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \,\!\!\wr\, \partial_x H, \partial_y H, \partial_z H)^2 \\
+ 2x (y\partial_y H + z\partial_z H)(\mathfrak{H}\partial_y H + \mathfrak{G}\partial_z H) \\
- \mathfrak{A} (y\partial_y H + z\partial_z H)^2,
\end{multline*}
and
\[
y\partial_y H + z\partial_z H = 3(m-2)H - x\partial_x H,
\]
so   that   the   term   is
\begin{multline*}
+ 2x\{\mathfrak{H}(3(m-2)H - x\partial_x H) + \mathfrak{G}\partial_z H\} \\
- \mathfrak{A}\{3(m-2)H - x\partial_x H\}^2;
\end{multline*}
or,  reducing,  we  have
\begin{multline*}
(m-1)^2 \{(C\partial_y - B\partial_z)H\}^2 = \\
x^2 (\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \,\!\!\wr\, \partial_x H, \partial_y H, \partial_z H)^2 \\
+ 6(m-2)Hx(\mathfrak{H}\partial_y H + \mathfrak{G}\partial_z H) - 9(m-2)^2 \mathfrak{A} H^2.
\end{multline*}

\paragraph{23.}
The    other  terms  may   be   obtained   in   a   similar   manner;    and    it    is    easy    to
see  that,  collecting  all  the  terms,  the  sum  will  be
\begin{multline*}
-(\lambda x + \mu y + \nu z)^2 (\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \,\!\!\wr\, \partial_x H, \partial_y H, \partial_z H)^2 \\
+ 3(m-2)H(\lambda x + \mu y + \nu z)
  [(\mathfrak{A}\lambda + \mathfrak{H}\mu + \mathfrak{G}\nu)\partial_x H
  + (\mathfrak{H}\lambda + \mathfrak{B}\mu + \mathfrak{F}\nu)\partial_y H
  + (\mathfrak{G}\lambda + \mathfrak{F}\mu + \mathfrak{C}\nu)\partial_z H] \\
- 9(m-2)^2 H^2 (\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \,\!\!\wr\, \lambda, \mu, \nu)^2;
\end{multline*}
or,  attending  to  the  signification  of  the  symbols  $\mathfrak{A}$,  $\Phi$,  $\mathfrak{D}$,  $\Omega$,  we  have
\[
(m-1)^2 (\mathfrak{D} H)^2 = -9(m-2)^2 H^2 \Phi + 3(m-2)H \cdot \Omega.
\]

\newpage

\paragraph{24.}
Next
\[
\mathfrak{D}^2 H = \{(B\nu - C\mu)\partial_x + (C\lambda - A\nu)\partial_y + (A\mu - B\lambda)\partial_z\}^2 H,
\]
or,  what  is  the  same  thing,
\[
\mathfrak{D}^2 H = \{\lambda(C\partial_y - B\partial_z) + \mu(A\partial_z - C\partial_x) + \nu(B\partial_x - A\partial_y)\}^2 H;
\]
and  if  we  attend  first  to  the  term  which  contains  $\lambda^2$,  the  coefficient  is
\[
(C\partial_y - B\partial_z)^2 H.
\]
Now  substituting  for  $C$,  $B$  as  before,  we  have
\begin{multline*}
(m-1)^2 (C\partial_y - B\partial_z) = (-\mathfrak{A}x^2 + 2\mathfrak{H}xy - \mathfrak{B}y^2)\partial_y^2 H \\
+ 2(-\mathfrak{G}x^2 + \mathfrak{H}xy + \mathfrak{F}xz - \mathfrak{C}yz)\partial_y \partial_z H \\
+ (-\mathfrak{G}x^2 + 2\mathfrak{F}xz - \mathfrak{C}z^2)\partial_z^2 H \\
= -x^2 (\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \,\!\!\wr\, \partial_x, \partial_y, \partial_z)^2 H \\
+ 2x(y\partial_y + z\partial_z)(\mathfrak{H}\partial_y + \mathfrak{G}\partial_z)H - 2\mathfrak{A}(y\partial_y + z\partial_z)^2 H.
\end{multline*}


\newpage

\noindent\textit{Proof of the expressions for $(m-1)^2 R_3$, $(m-1)^2 R_4$:}

\paragraph{26.}
We  have
\begin{align*}
(m-1)^2 R_3 &= (m-1)^2 (\mathfrak{D} H) \cdot \Phi \\
&= -9(m-2)^2 H^2 \Phi + 3(m-2)H \cdot \Omega, \\
(m-1)^2 R_4 &= (m-1)^2 \mathfrak{D}^2 H \cdot \Phi + (m-1)^2 (\mathfrak{D} H)^2 - (3m-6)(m-1)^2 \Phi H \\
&= -12(m-2)^2 H^2 \Phi + 4(m-2) \Omega^2,
\end{align*}
which  are  the  expressions  required.

\noindent\textit{Proof  of  corresponding  expressions  for  $(m-1)^2 Q_3$,  $(m-1)^2 Q_4$:}

\paragraph{27.}
We  have
\begin{align*}
(m-1)^2 Q_3 &= (m-1)^2 (\mathfrak{D} H) \cdot \Phi \\
&= -9(m-2)^2 H^2 \Phi + 3(m-2)H \cdot \Omega, \\
(m-1)^2 Q_4 &= (m-1)^2 \mathfrak{D}^2 H \cdot \Phi - (m-1)^2 \Omega \cdot \Phi + (3m-6)(m-1) H \cdot \Omega \\
&= -6(m-2)(m-3) H^2 \Phi + 2(m-3) \Omega^2,
\end{align*}
to  which  I  join
\[
(m-1)^2 Q_2 = H.
\]

\paragraph{28.}
We  have  consequently
\[
(m-1)^2 (3Q_2 Q_4 - Q_3^2) = -9(m-2)(m-3) H^2 \Phi + 3(m-4) H \Omega^2 + (-3\Omega H + \Psi) \Phi^2;
\]
and
\[
(m-1)^2 (3(m-2)Q_2 Q_4 - 2(m-3)Q_3^2) = (-3(m-2)H \Phi + 2(m-3)\Omega^2) \Psi,
\]
which  for  $m = 4$  becomes
\[
729(3Q_2 Q_4 - Q_3^2) = (-3H \Phi + \Psi) \Psi.
\]

\paragraph{29.}
In  the  case  $m = 4$,  we  have  Hesse's  theorem,  that  the  equation  $3Q_2 Q_4 - Q_3^2 = 0$
gives  a  curve  of  the  $14$th  order,  which  passes  through  the  points  of  contact  of  the
double  tangents,  viz.  substituting  for  $\Omega$,  $\Psi$  their  values,  the  equation  of  this  curve  is
\[
-3\Phi (\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \,\!\!\wr\, \partial_x H, \partial_y H, \partial_z H)^2
+ (\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \,\!\!\wr\, \partial_x H, \partial_y H, \partial_z H)^2 = 0.
\]

\newpage
\noindent\textsc{III.~Application  of  the  Formul\ae\ to  the  Cubic.}

\paragraph{30.}
I    shall   apply    the    formul\ae\  for   the    five-pointic    conic    to    a    cubic;    to    avoid
confusion  as  to  a  numerical  factor,  I  write  $U'$,  $H'$  in  the  place  of  $U$,  $H$,  so  that  we  have
\[
\Lambda = \frac{-3(\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \,\!\!\wr\, \partial_x, \partial_y, \partial_z)^2 H \cdot H}{9H^2 \Phi + 4(\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \,\!\!\wr\, \partial_x H, \partial_y H, \partial_z H)^2},
\]
and  then  the  equation  of  the  five-pointic  conic  is
\[
D^2 U' - \frac{1}{2(m-2)}(DH' + \Lambda \cdot DU') \, DU' = 0.
\]
I  take  as  the  equation  of  the  cubic,
\[
U = x^3 + y^3 + z^3 + 6lxyz = 0;
\]
the  formul\ae\  Table  No.~70   of  my  Third   Memoir  on    Quantics,  Phil.\ Trans.,   vol.~CXLVI.
(1856),  pp.~627--647,  [144],  putting  $H$  for  $H(U)$,  give
\[
H = l^4(x^3 + y^3 + z^3) - (1 + 2l^3) xyz.
\]
Hence  writing
\[
U' = \tfrac{1}{3}(x^3 + y^3 + z^3 + 6lxyz),
\]
the  first  derived  functions  are
\[
\tfrac{1}{3}(3x^2 + 2lyz), \quad \tfrac{1}{3}(3y^2 + 2lzx), \quad \tfrac{1}{3}(3z^2 + 2lxy);
\]
the  second  derived  functions,  or  $(a, b, c, f, g, h)$,  are  $(x, y, z, lx, ly, lz)$,  whence  $W = -U$;
the  inverse  coefficients  $(\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H})$  are
\[
(yz - l^2x^2,\ zx - l^2y^2,\ xy - l^2z^2,\ l^2yz - lx^2,\ l^2zx - ly^2,\ l^2xy - lz^2);
\]
and  putting  $U' = \tfrac{1}{3}U$  and  $H' = -H$,  we  have
\[
\Lambda = -\frac{1}{9} \cdot \frac{-3(\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \,\!\!\wr\, \partial_x, \partial_y, \partial_z)^2 H \cdot H}
               {9H^2 \Phi + 4(\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \,\!\!\wr\, \partial_x H, \partial_y H, \partial_z H)^2},
\]
and  the  equation  of  the  five-pointic  conic  is
\[
D^2 U - \tfrac{1}{2}(DH + 3\Lambda \cdot DU) \, DU = 0.
\]

\paragraph{31.}
We  have
\begin{multline*}
(\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \,\!\!\wr\, \partial_x, \partial_y, \partial_z)^2 H \\
= (yz - l^2x^2) \cdot 6lx + (zx - l^2y^2) \cdot 6ly + (xy - l^2z^2) \cdot 6lz \\
+ 2(l^2yz - lx^2) \cdot (-(1 + 2l^3)x) + 2(l^2zx - ly^2) \cdot (-(1 + 2l^3)y) \\
+ 2(l^2xy - lz^2) \cdot (-(1 + 2l^3)z) \\
= 18l^3 xyz - 6l^2(x^3 + y^3 + z^3) - 6l(1 + 2l^3)xyz + 2l^2(1 + 2l^3)(x^3 + y^3 + z^3) \\
= (12l^6 - 12l^3)xyz + (2l - 2l^4)(x^3 + y^3 + z^3) \\
= 2(l - l^4)(x^3 + y^3 + z^3 + 6lxyz),
\end{multline*}
\[
(\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \,\!\!\wr\, \partial_x, \partial_y, \partial_z)^2 H = -2S \cdot U,
\]
where   $S$  is  the  quartic  invariant  (see  the  Table  No.~70).     For  the  present  purpose  $U = 0$,
and  consequently
\[
(\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \,\!\!\wr\, \partial_x, \partial_y, \partial_z)^2 H = 0.
\]


\newpage
\paragraph{34.}
If,  however,  we  continue  to  represent  the  cubic  by  the  canonical  equation
\[
x^3 + y^3 + z^3 + 6lxyz = 0,
\]
the  result  may  be  further  reduced.     We  have,  putting  $U = 0$,  or  writing  $x^3 + y^3 + z^3 = -6lxyz$,
\[
H = -(1 + 8l^3) xyz;
\]
moreover,  putting  $U = 0$,  the  Table  No.~70  gives
\[
\Theta = (1 + 8l^3)^2 (y^3 z^3 + z^3 x^3 + x^3 y^3) - 3l^2 x^2 y^2 z^2;
\]
or  substituting  for  $H$  the  last-mentioned  value,  and  putting  for  shortness
\[
Q = y^3 z^3 + z^3 x^3 + x^3 y^3 - 3l^2 x^2 y^2 z^2,
\]
we  have
\[
\Theta = (1 + 8l^3)^2 Q,
\]
and  with  these  values  of  $H$  and  $Q$,  the  equation  of  the  five-pointic  conic  is
\[
D^2 U - \frac{1}{2} \left(DH + \frac{3\Theta}{H} \cdot DU\right) DU = 0.
\]

We  have
\begin{align*}
DU &= 3\{(x^2 + 2lyz)X + (y^2 + 2lzx)Y + (z^2 + 2lxy)Z\}, \\
D^2 U &= 6\{(X^2 + 2lYZ)x + (Y^2 + 2lZX)y + (Z^2 + 2lXY)z\}, \\
DH &= (3l^2 x^2 - (1 + 2l^3)yz)X + (3l^2 y^2 - (1 + 2l^3)zx)Y + (3l^2 z^2 - (1 + 2l^3)xy)Z;
\end{align*}
whence,  finally,  the  equation  of  the  five-pointic  conic  of  the  cubic
\[
X^3 + Y^3 + Z^3 + 6lXYZ = 0
\]
at  the  point  $(x, y, z)$  is
\begin{multline*}
Q(1 + 8l^3) x^2 y^2 z^2 (x, y, z, lx, ly, lz \,\!\!\wr\, X, Y, Z)^2 \\
+ \{(x^2 + 2lyz)X + (y^2 + 2lzx)Y + (z^2 + 2lxy)Z\} \times \\
\left[\frac{-x^2 y^2 z^2}{H} \{(3l^2 x^2 - (1 + 2l^3)yz)X + \cdots\} \right. \\
\left. - Q\{(x^2 + 2lyz)X + (y^2 + 2lzx)Y + (z^2 + 2lxy)Z\}\right] = 0,
\end{multline*}
a  result  which  I  had  previously  obtained  by  a  special  method.

\paragraph{35.}
But  the  expression  may  be  exhibited  in  a  different  form  by  a  transformation
suggested  by  a  geometrical  theorem  of  Mr~Salmon's.  In  fact  the  tangent  at  the  point
$(x, y, z)$  meets  the  cubic  in  the  tangential  of  this  point,  and  the  coordinates  of  the
tangential  are  $x(y^3 - z^3)$,  $y(z^3 - x^3)$,  $z(x^3 - y^3)$.  Calling  these  $\xi$,  $\eta$,  $\zeta$,  the  equation  of
the  tangent  to  the  cubic  at  the  tangential  is
\[
(\eta^3 + 2l\eta\zeta)X + (\zeta^3 + 2l\zeta\xi)Y + (\xi^3 + 2l\xi\eta)Z = 0.
\]

\newpage
\noindent\textsc{IV.~Independent investigation for the Cubic.}

\medskip

\paragraph{47.}
The  following  is,  in  substance,  the  method  by  which  I  first  obtained  the
equation  of  the  five-pointic  conic,  for  the  cubic
\[
X^3 + Y^3 + Z^3 + 6lXYZ = 0.
\]
Write  for  shortness
\begin{align*}
U &= x^3 + y^3 + z^3 + 6lxyz, \\
V &= (x^2 + 2lyz)X + (y^2 + 2lzx)Y + (z^2 + 2lxy)Z, \\
W &= (X^2 + 2lYZ)x + (Y^2 + 2lXZ)y + (Z^2 + 2lXY)z, \\
T &= X^3 + Y^3 + Z^3 + 6lXYZ, \\
\mathrm{II} &= aX + bY + cZ,
\end{align*}
then,  $X$,  $Y$,  $Z$  being  current  coordinates,  and  $x$,  $y$,  $z$  the  coordinates  of  a  point  of
the  cubic  (so  that  $U = 0$),  the  equation  of  the  cubic  will  be
\[
T = 0,
\]
and  the  equation  of  a  conic  having  with  it  an  ordinary  (two-pointic)  contact  at  the
point  $(x, y, z)$,  will  be
\[
2VW - \mathrm{II} \cdot V = 0.
\]

\paragraph{48--57.}
[The  remaining  articles  contain  the  detailed  verification  of  the  formul\ae\  by  an
independent  method,  and  the  extension  to  curves  of  any  order,  following  the  lines
indicated  in  the  preceding  analysis.  The  investigations  confirm  the  general  formula
for  the  five-pointic  conic  obtained  in  the  earlier  sections.]


\newpage
\section*{262. On the Equation of Differences for an Equation of any Order, and in particular for the Equations of the Orders Two, Three, Four, and Five.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{262. On the Equation of Differences for an Equation of any Order}
\label{sec:paper262}

\noindent
[From   the   \textit{Philosophical  Transactions}  of  the  Royal  Society   of  London,  vol.~CL.~(for  the
year  1860),  pp.~93--112.     Received  March  2,---Read  March  29,  1860.]

\medskip

The  term,  equation  of  differences,  denotes  the  equation  for  the  squared  differences
of  the  roots  of  a  given  equation ;  the  equation  of  differences  afforded  a  means  of
determining  the  number  of  real  roots,  and  also  limits  for  the  real  roots,  of  a  given
numerical  equation,  and  was  upon  this  account  long  ago  sought  for  by  geometers.
In  the  Philosophical  Transactions  for  1763,  Waring  gives,  but  without  demonstration
or  indication  of  the  mode  of  obtaining  it,  the  equation  of  differences  for  an  equation
of  the  fifth  order  wanting  the  second  term :  the  result  was  probably  obtained  by  the
method  of  symmetric  functions.  This  method  is  employed  in  the  \textit{Meditationes  Alge-}
\textit{braicae}  (1782),  where  the  equation  of  differences  is  given  for  the  equations  of  the
third  and  fourth  orders  wanting  the  second  terms;  and  in  p.~85  the  before-mentioned
result  for  the  equation  of  the  fifth  order  wanting  the  second  term,  is  reproduced.
The  formul\ae\  for  obtaining  by  this  method  the  equation  of  differences,  are  fully
developed  by  Lagrange  in  the  \textit{Trait\'e  des  \'Equations  Num\'eriques}  (1808);  and  he  finds
by  means  of  them  the  equation  of  differences  for  the  equations  of  the  orders  two  and
three,  and  for  the  equation  of  the  fourth  order  wanting  the  second  term ;  and  in
Note~III.\  he  gives,  after  Waring,  the  result  for  the  equation  of  the  fifth  order  wanting
the  second  term.  It  occurred  to  me  that  the  equation  of  differences  could  be  most
easily  calculated  by  the  following  method.  The  coefficients  of  the  equation  of  differences,
qua    functions    of    the    differences    of    the    roots    of    the    given  equation,   are    leading
coefficients  of  covariants,  or  (to  use  a  shorter  expression)  they  are  ``Seminvariants,''
that  is,  each  of  them  is  a  function  of  the  coefficients  which  is  reduced  to  zero  by  one
of  the  two  operators  which  reduce  an  invariant  to  zero.  In  virtue  of  this  property
they  can  be  calculated,  when  their  values  are  known  for  the  particular  case  in  which
one  of  the  coefficients  of  the  given  equation  is  zero.

To  fix  the  ideas,  let  the  given  equation  be  $(a, b, \ldots \wr\, v,  1)^n = 0$;  then,  when  the
last  coefficient  or  constant  term  vanishes,  the  equation  breaks  up  into  $v = 0$  and  into
an  equation  of  the  degree  $n-1$,  which  I  call  the  \textit{reduced  equation};  the  equation  of
differences  will  break  up  into  two  equations,  one  of  which  is  the  equation  of  differences
for  the  reduced  equation,  the  other  is  the  equation  for  the  squares  of  the  roots  of  the
same  reduced  equation.


The  equations  successively  considered  are
\begin{align*}
(a, b, c \,\!\!\wr\, v, 1)^2 &= 0, \\
(a, b, c, d \,\!\!\wr\, v, 1)^3 &= 0, \\
(a, b, c, d, e \,\!\!\wr\, v, 1)^4 &= 0, \\
(a, b, c, d, e, f \,\!\!\wr\, v, 1)^5 &= 0.
\end{align*}
The  equation  of  differences  for  the  quadric,  and  that  for  the  squares  of  the  roots,
are  considered  to  be  known,  and  the  other  results  are  derived  from  them.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Quadric Equation.}

\noindent
Equation  of  differences  is,  $0 =$
\[
\begin{array}{r|rrr}
a^2 x & +1 & ac + 4 & b^2 - 1 \\
\hline
& a^2 x & +1 & ac + 2 \\
& & b^2 - 1 & c^2 + 1 \\
\end{array}
\]

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Cubic Equation.}

\noindent
Equation  of  differences  is,  $0 =$
\[
\begin{array}{r|rrrrr}
a^3 x & +1 & ac + 6 & a^2 c^2 + 9 & a^3 c^3 + 27 & \\
& & b^2 - 2 & ab^2 c - 6 & b^4 + 1 & \\
& & & ac^3 + 4 & a^2 bc d - 18 & \\
& & & & b^2 c^2 - 1 & \\
\end{array}
\]

\noindent
Equation  for  the  squares  of  the  roots  is,  $0 =$
\[
\begin{array}{r|rrrr}
a^2 x & +1 & ac + 2 & b^2 - 1 & \\
& & bd - 2 & c^2 + 1 & \\
& & & d^2 + 1 & \\
\end{array}
\]

\newpage
\noindent\textbf{Quartic Equation.}

\noindent
Equation  of  differences  is,  $0 =$
\[
\begin{array}{r|rrrrrr}
a^4 x & +1 & ac + 8 & a^2 c^2 + 8 & a^3 c e + 16 & a^4 e^2 - 112 & \\
& & b^2 - 3 & a^2 b d - 2 & a^3 c d^2 + 26 & a^3 b c d e + 56 & \\
& & & a^2 c e + 22 & a^2 b^2 c e - 6 & a^3 b d e^2 + 216 & \\
& & & b^4 + 3 & a^2 b^2 d + 8 & a^3 c d^3 - 192 & \\
& & & & a b^2 c^2 + 8 & a^3 d^4 - 27 & \\
& & & & a b^2 c d + 38 & a^2 b c^2 d e + 120 & \\
& & & & a c^4 + 4 & a^2 c d^2 e - 54 & \\
& & & & b^3 d - 6 & a b^3 c e + 32 & \\
& & & & b^2 c^2 + 2 & a b^2 c d^2 - 25 & \\
& & & & & a b^2 d^3 + 18 & \\
& & & & & a b c^3 d + 42 & \\
& & & & & a c^2 e + 16 & \\
& & & & & b^4 e + 6 & \\
& & & & & b^3 c d - 12 & \\
& & & & & b^2 c d^2 - 9 & \\
& & & & & b^2 c^3 e + 6 & \\
& & & & & b c^2 d^2 + 1 & \\
\end{array}
\]

\medskip
\noindent
Equation  for  the  squares  of  the  roots  is,  $0 =$
\[
\begin{array}{r|rrrrr}
a^2 x & +1 & ac + 2 & b^2 - 1 & & \\
& & ae + 2 & bd - 2 & c^2 + 1 & \\
& & & ce + 2 & d^2 - 1 & \\
& & & & e^2 + 1 & \\
\end{array}
\]

\medskip

The  multiplication  of  the  equation  of  differences  and  the  equation  of  the  squares
of  the  roots  of  the  quadric  equation,  gives  the  equation,  $0 =$
\[
\begin{array}{r|rrrr}
a^4 x & +1 & ac + 6 & a^2 c^2 + 9 & a^3 b^2 c - 6 \\
& & b^2 - 2 & a b^2 c - 6 & b^4 + 1 \\
& & & a c^3 + 4 & b^2 c^2 - 1 \\
\end{array}
\]
where  all  the  coefficients  except  the  last  are  reduced  to  zero  by  the  operator
\[
3a\partial_b + 2b\partial_c + c\partial_d,
\]
and  they  are  consequently  (without  any  alteration)  coefficients  of  the  equation  of  differences  of  the  cubic  equation.


\newpage
\noindent\textbf{Quintic Equation.}

The  multiplication  of  the  equation  of  differences,  and  the  equation  for  the  squares
of  the  roots  of  the  quartic  equation,  gives,  in  like  manner,  the  equation  of  differences
for  the  quintic  equation,  except  as  to  the  terms  involving  $f$;  and  these  are  obtained
as  above,  viz.  each  coefficient  is  of  the  form  $L_0 + L_1 f + L_2 f^2 + \&c.$,  where  $L_0$  is  known;
such  coefficient  is  reduced  to  zero  by  the  operator
\[
5a\partial_b + 4b\partial_c + 3c\partial_d + 2d\partial_e + e\partial_f,
\]
or  putting  for  shortness  $V = 5a\partial_b + 4b\partial_c + 3c\partial_d + 2d\partial_e$,  the  operator  $V + e\partial_f$.  We  have
therefore
\[
L_1 = -\tfrac{1}{e} VL_0, \quad L_2 = -\tfrac{1}{2e} VL_1, \quad L_3 = -\tfrac{1}{3e} VL_2, \quad \&c.
\]
which  give  $L_1$,  $L_2$,  \&c.\  by  means  of  $L_0$.

For  the  calculation  of  $VL$  (where  $L$  is  any  one  of  the  coefficients  $L_0$,  $L_1$,  \&c.),
it  is  proper  to  separate  the  terms  involving  the  different  powers  of  $e$,  and  write
$L = M_0 + M_1 e + M_2 e^2 + \&c.$,  $V = V' + 2d\partial_e$,  where  $V' = 5a\partial_b + 4b\partial_c + 3c\partial_d$.  We  have  then
\begin{align*}
VL &= V'M_0 + e(V'M_1 + 2d\partial_e M_0) \\
   &\quad + e^2(V'M_2 + 2d\partial_e M_1) + \&c.
\end{align*}

\medskip

[The  memoir  continues  with  detailed  calculations  of  each  coefficient  for  the  quintic
equation,  following  the  method  explained  above.  The  calculations  involve  extensive
tabular  arrangements  showing  the  terms  of  each  coefficient  $L_0$,  $L_1$,  $L_2$,  \&c.,  and
the  application  of  the  operator  $V$  to  obtain  the  successive  coefficients.  The  complete
equation  of  differences  for  the  quintic  equation  is  thus  obtained.]

\medskip

\noindent\textbf{General  Equation  of  Differences  by  Elimination.}

At  the  conclusion  of  the  memoir,  I  show  how  the  equation  of  differences  for  an
equation  of  the  order  $n$  can  be  obtained  by  the  elimination  of  a  single  quantity  from
two  equations  each  of  the  order  $n-1$;  and  by  applying  to  these  two  equations  the
simplification  which  I  have  made  in  Bezout's  abridged  method  of  elimination,  I  exhibit
the  equation  of  differences  for  the  given  equation  of  the  order  $n$,  in  a  compendious
form  by  means  of  a  determinant.

\vspace{2em}

\noindent\rule{\textwidth}{0.4pt}

\medskip

[End  of  Papers  260--262,  covering  pages  201--250  of  Cayley's  Collected  Mathematical
Papers,  Volume~IV.]

%======================================================================
\section[261. On the Conic of Five-Pointic Contact]{261. On the Conic of Five-Pointic Contact at any Point of a Plane Curve (concluded)}
%======================================================================

\textbf{[From the \textit{Philosophical Transactions} of the Royal Society of London, vol.~CL (1860), pp.~539--578. Received Feb.~2, --- Read March 22, 1860.]}

\vspace{6pt}

\noindent\textit{(Continuation from p.~228 of this Volume)}

\vspace{6pt}

41. I have not sought to verify this theorem by my formulae. I remark, that combining it with the before-mentioned theorem, the five-pointic conic is completely determined as follows; viz.

\begin{theorem}
The five-pointic conic touches the conic at the point of contact (two conditions); it passes through the two points in which the polar conic is intersected by the tangent to the cubic at the tangential of the point of contact (two conditions); and it passes through the point which is the third point of intersection with the cubic of the line joining the point of contact with its second tangential.
\end{theorem}

42. The construction for the point of simple intersection leads at once to that for the sextactic points; in fact, consider a point having for its tangential a point of inflexion: a point of inflexion is its own tangential, and the second tangential of the first-mentioned point is therefore the point of inflexion: the line joining the point with the second tangential is therefore the tangent at the point, and the point of simple intersection coincides with the point itself, that is, the point in question is a sextactic point.

43. I represent the equation of the five-pointic conic by
\[
(a,b,c,f,g,h\,\fat\, X,Y,Z)^2=0;
\]
the value of $a$ is
\begin{align*}
a &= 9(1+8l^3)x^2y^3z^3\\
&\quad +6x^4yz^2\{3ly^2-(1+2l^3)zx\}\{x^2+2lyz\}\\
&\quad -2Q(x^2+2lyz)^2,
\end{align*}
in which equation
\[
Q = y^4z^2+z^4x^2+x^4y^2-2l^2x^2y^2z^2;
\]
or, reducing by the equation $x^3+y^3+z^3+6lxyz=0$,
\[
Q = y^4z^2+x^2(-x^3-6lxyz)-2l^2x^2y^2z^2,
\]
that is,
\[
-Q = x^5y^2z+6l^2x^3y^2z^2+8l^3x^2y^3z^3-y^4z^4,
\]
and we have
\begin{align*}
a &= 9(1+8l^3)x^2y^3z^3\\
&\quad +6x^4yz^2\{3ly^2-(1+2l^3)zx\}\{x^2+2lyz\}\\
&\quad +\{x^5y^2z+6l^2x^3y^2z^2+8l^3x^2y^3z^3-y^4z^4\}(x^2+4xl^2yz+4l^2y^2z^2).
\end{align*}
We have in like manner
\begin{multline*}
2f = 18(1+8l^3)x^3y^2z^3\\
+3x^4yz^2\bigl[\{3ly^2-(1+2l^3)zx\}(z^2+2lxy)+\{3lz^2-(1+2l^3)xy\}(y^2+2lzx)\bigr]\\
-2Q(y^2+2lzx)(z^2+2lxy);
\end{multline*}
the coefficient of $3x^4y^2z^2$ in the second line is
\[
= 6lyz^2+(-1+4l^3)x(y^2+z^2)-l(1+2l^3)x^2yz;
\]
or reducing by the equation of the curve,
\[
= (1-4l^3)x^3+(2l-32l^4)x^2yz+6l^2xy^2z^2.
\]

We thus have
\begin{multline*}
2f=18(1+8l^3)x^3y^2z^3\\
+6x^4yz^2\{(1-4l^3)x^3+(2l-32l^4)x^2yz+6l^2xy^2z^2\}\\
+2(x^5y^2z+6l^2x^3y^2z^2+8l^3x^2y^3z^3-y^4z^4)(-2lx^3-8l^2x^2yz+y^2z^2).
\end{multline*}

Reducing the expressions of $a$ and $2f$, we find for the coefficients $(a,b,c,f,g,h)$:
\begin{align*}
a &= x^{10}+10lx^8yz+40l^2x^7y^2z^2+(5+120l^3)x^5y^3z^3-10lx^4y^4z^4-ly^5z^5,\\
2f &= -4lx^{10}-40l^2x^8yz+(5-120l^3)x^7y^2z^2+40l^2x^5y^3z^3+8l^3x^4y^4z^4-2y^5z^5,
\end{align*}
which gives the completely developed form of the equation of the five-pointic conic.

44. I investigate the coordinates of the point of simple intersection of the cubic and the five-pointic conic as follows: the equations of the two curves are
\[
X^3+Y^3+Z^3+6lXYZ=0,\qquad (a,b,c,f,g,h\,\fat\, X,Y,Z)^2=0;
\]
or if we write
\begin{align*}
a &= 1, & h &= c,\\
\beta &= 2lXY, & B &= 2(gX+fY),\\
\gamma &= X^2+Y^2, & C &= aZ^2+2hXY+bY^2,
\end{align*}
then the two equations are
\[
aZ^2+\gamma+\alpha=0,\qquad AZ^2+BZ+C=0,
\]
and the result of the elimination of $Z$ will be
\[
(aZ_1^2+\gamma Z_1+\alpha)(aZ_2^2+\gamma Z_2+\alpha)=0,
\]
where $Z_1$, $Z_2$ are the roots of the equation $AZ^2+BZ+C=0$; that is, we have
\begin{align*}
\frac{B^2-4AC}{A^2} &= \frac{\gamma^2-4a\alpha}{a^2},\\
\frac{C(B^2-2AC)}{A^3} &= \frac{\gamma(\gamma^2-2a\alpha)}{a^3},\\
\frac{C^2}{A^4} &= \frac{\gamma^2\alpha^2}{a^4},
\end{align*}
and the result is
\[
a^2C^2+a\gamma A(2AC-B^2)+A^2(\gamma^2-4a\alpha)(\gamma^2-a\alpha)=0.
\]

45. But the result of the elimination must obviously be
\[
(Xy_1-Yx_1)(Xy_2-Yx_2)=0,
\]
if $(x_1,y_1,z_1)$ are the coordinates of the point of simple intersection. Comparing the two results, and forming the analogous third equation, we may write
\begin{align*}
a^4x_1 &= b^2+c^2-8f^2+6bcf,\\
y_1^4 &= c^2+a^2-8g^2+6cag,\\
z_1^4 &= a^2+b^2-8h^2+6abh,
\end{align*}
where the value of $a^4x_1$ may also be written
\[
(b+c-2f)(b+c\omega-2\omega^2f)(b+c\omega^2-2\omega f),
\]
$\omega$ being an imaginary cube root of unity, and so for the other two terms.

I remark, that putting $x=0$, we have
\[
bY^2+2fYZ+cZ^2=-y^2z^2(lY+Z^2)^2,
\]
and hence writing $1$ for $Y$, and $-1$, $-\omega$, $-\omega^2$ for $Z$, we have
\begin{align*}
b+c-2f &= -y^2z^2(l-z^2)^2,\\
b+c\omega-2\omega^2f &= -y^2z^2(l-\omega z^2)^2,\\
b+c\omega^2-2\omega f &= -y^2z^2(l-\omega^2z^2)^2;
\end{align*}
and hence the product of the three factors is $-y^6z^6(l-z^2)^2(l-\omega z^2)^2(l-\omega^2z^2)^2$, which is equal to $-y^6z^6(l-z^2)^2(l+z^2)^2$, which vanishes in virtue of the assumed equation $x=0$. This shows that the function $b^2+c^2-8f^2+6bcf$ contains the factor $x$. I have not verified \textit{a posteriori}, but I assume it to be true, that it contains in fact the factor $a^4$, and consequently that the expressions for $x_1$, $y_1$, $z_1$ are rational and integral functions of $(x^2,y^2,z^2)$ of the degree 25, and containing respectively the factors $x$, $y$, $z$.

46. In the theory of the cubic, a point which depends linearly upon a given point may be termed a \textit{derivative} of such point. According to a very beautiful theorem of Professor Sylvester's, the coordinates of a derivative point are necessarily rational and integral functions of a square degree of the coordinates $(x,y,z)$ of the given point; and moreover, there is but one derivative point having its coordinates of any given square degree $m^2$, or, as we may express it, only one derivative point of the degree $m^2$. The successive tangentials are derivative points of the degrees 4, 16, 64, \&c.; the third point of intersection with the cubic, of the line joining two derivative points of the degrees $m^2$ and $n^2$ respectively, is a derivative point of the degree $(m+n)^2$. Thus the third point of intersection with the cubic, of the line joining the given point with its second tangential, is a derivative point of the degree $(4+1)^2$, and it is easy to see that the degree is not 9; it is therefore 25. The point of simple intersection of the five-pointic conic is a derivative point of the degree 25; it is therefore, according to Professor Sylvester's general theory, identical with the point given by the former construction; this agrees with the before-mentioned theorem of Mr~Salmon.

\subsection*{IV. Independent investigation for the Cubic}

47. The following is, in substance, the method by which I first obtained the equation of the five-pointic conic, for the cubic
\[
X^3+Y^3+Z^3+6lXYZ=0.
\]
Write for shortness
\begin{align*}
U &= x^3+y^3+z^3+6lxyz,\\
V &= (x^2+2lyz)X+(y^2+2lzx)Y+(z^2+2lxy)Z,\\
W &= (X^2+2lYZ)x+(Y^2+2lXZ)y+(Z^2+2lXY)z,\\
T &= X^3+Y^3+Z^3+6lXYZ,\\
P &= ax+by+cz,\\
\Pi &= aX+bY+cZ;
\end{align*}
then, $X$, $Y$, $Z$ being current coordinates, and $x$, $y$, $z$ the coordinates of a point of the cubic (so that $U=0$), the equation of the cubic will be
\[
W=0,
\]
and the equation of a conic having with it an ordinary (two-pointic) contact at the point $(x,y,z)$, will be\footnote{I have introduced the factor 2, to make this correspond with the form $D\Pi-\Pi DU=0$, in the case in question, $m=3$.}
\[
2W-\Pi V=0.
\]

48. Now imagine from the point of contact lines drawn to the other four intersections of the two curves; in the case of the five-pointic conic, three of these lines will coincide with the tangent $V=0$, and the remaining line will be the line joining the point of contact with the point of simple intersection. The equations of the lines in question can be found by Joachimsthal's theorem, viz. if $(x,y,z)$ be the coordinates of a given point, and $(X,Y,Z)$ current coordinates, then if in the equations of any two curves we substitute for the coordinates, $\lambda x+\mu X$, $\lambda y+\mu Y$, $\lambda z+\mu Z$, and between the equations so obtained eliminate $\lambda$, $\mu$, the resulting equation will be that of the lines drawn from the point $(x,y,z)$ to the points of intersection of the two curves. The point $(x,y,z)$ is any point whatever, and it may therefore be a point of intersection, or, as in the present instance, a point of contact of the two curves; the only difference is, that in either case the degree of each equation as regards $(\lambda,\mu)$ is reduced by unity, and the degree of the resulting equation in $X,Y,Z$ is also reduced by unity: in the case of a point of simple intersection this is the only reduction; but in the case of a point of contact, the resulting equation contains the equation of the tangent as a factor, and rejecting this factor, the reduction in degree is two units.

49. Applying the method to the two equations, $T=0$, $2W-\Pi V=0$, and substituting therein for the original current coordinates $X$, $Y$, $Z$ the values $\lambda x+\mu X$, $\lambda y+\mu Y$, $\lambda z+\mu Z$, the equations become
\begin{align*}
\lambda^3U+3\lambda^2\mu\cdot W+3\lambda\mu^2\cdot V+\mu^3T &= 0,\\
2(\lambda^2U+2\lambda\mu W+\mu^2V)-(\lambda P+\mu\Pi)(\lambda V+\mu W) &= 0;
\end{align*}
or writing $U=0$, and omitting from each equation the factor $\mu$, the equations become
\begin{align*}
3\lambda^2W+3\lambda\mu V+\mu^2T &= 0,\\
\lambda(4-P)V+\mu(2W-\Pi V) &= 0;
\end{align*}
and putting in the first equation $\lambda=2W-\Pi V$, $\mu=-(4-P)V$, the result of the elimination contains the factor $V$, rejecting which it becomes
\[
3(2W-\Pi V)^2-3(4-P)(2W-\Pi V)W+(4-P)^2VT=0,
\]
which is of the fourth degree in $(X,Y,Z)$, as it should be, and represents therefore the lines drawn from the point of contact to the other four points of intersection of the conic and cubic.

50. The equation may be written
\[
-3(2W-\Pi V)\{(2-P)W+\Pi V\}+(4-P)^2VT=0,
\]
and we obtain at once the condition that this may contain the factor $V$, viz. this condition is $P=2$; and if this be satisfied the conic will have a three-pointic conic, and there will be three other points of intersection. And writing $P=2$, and throwing out the factor $V$, we find
\[
3\Pi^2V^2-6\Pi VW+4T=0
\]
for the equation of the lines from the point of contact to the three points of intersection. And we have now to determine $\Pi$ so that the function on the left hand may divide by $V^2$.

51. I simplify my original method by the use of a theorem of Mr~Salmon's, viz.
\begin{align*}
T &= X^3+Y^3+Z^3+6lXYZ, & H &= l^2(X^3+Y^3+Z^3)-(1+2l^3)XYZ,\\
W &= (X^2+2lYZ)x+\ldots, & H_1 &= (3l^2X^2-(1+2l^3)YZ)x+\ldots,\\
&\quad\vdots & &\quad\vdots\\
U &= x^3+y^3+z^3+6lxyz, & H_0 &= l^2(x^3+y^3+z^3)-(1+2l^3)xyz,
\end{align*}
we have identically
\[
TH_0-UH_1^2=WH_1-VH_0,
\]
and in the present case, since $U=0$,
\[
TH_0=WH_1-VH_0.
\]
Hence, multiplying by $H_0$ and substituting this value of $TH_0$, the equation becomes
\[
3H_0W^2-6H_0\Pi VW+4WH_1-4VH_0=0,
\]
or, as we may write it,
\[
V(3H_0\Pi^2-4H_0)+2W(2H_1-3H_0\Pi)=0;
\]
and we can at once make the equation divide by $V$, viz. by assuming
\[
\Pi=\tfrac{2H_1}{3H_0}+\tfrac{1}{2}\Lambda V,
\]
where $\Lambda$ is arbitrary: we have thus a four-pointic contact. And substituting for $\Pi$, and throwing out the factor $V$, the equation becomes
\[
3\bigl(\tfrac{2H_1}{3H_0}+\tfrac{1}{2}\Lambda V\bigr)^2-4\tfrac{H_1}{H_0}+2T\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\Lambda=0;
\]
or reducing,
\[
\tfrac{16}{3}(H_1^2-H_0H_1)-36H_0^2\Lambda W+24H_0H_1\Lambda^2V+9H_0^2\Lambda^2V^2=0,
\]
which is the equation of the lines drawn from the point of contact to the remaining two points of intersection.

52. I write for greater convenience
\[
\Lambda=\frac{\Theta}{H_0},
\]
$\Theta$ being as yet indeterminate; the equation is thus reduced to
\[
\tfrac{16}{3}(H_1^2-H_0H_1)+9\Theta^2V^2-6H_0H_1\Theta V+\Theta^2\cdot\text{(something)}=0,
\]
and we have then to determine $\Theta$ so that the left-hand side may divide by $V$; or, what is the same thing, we must determine $\Theta$ so that
\[
H(H_1^2-3H_0J)+\tfrac{9}{4}\Theta V^2
\]
may divide by $V$. This implies the existence of an identical equation,
\[
H(H_1^2-3H_0J)+\tfrac{9}{4}\Theta V^2=MU+NV,
\]
which for $U=0$ would give the decomposition in question; but I have not investigated the values of $M$ and $N$. I assume at the outset $U=0$, and putting as before
\[
\Theta=\frac{2H_1}{3H_0}+\tfrac{1}{2}\Lambda V,
\]
and writing also
\begin{multline*}
S=Xx\{(1+8l^3)x^4+(4l+4ll^4)x^3yz+(-2l^2+2l^5)y^3z^3\}\\
+Yy\{(1+8l^3)y^4+(4l+4ll^4)y^3zx+(-2l^2+2l^5)z^3x^3\}\\
+Zz\{(1+8l^3)z^4+(4l+4ll^4)z^3xy+(-2l^2+2l^5)x^3y^3\},
\end{multline*}
I remark that for $U=0$ we have
\[
-xyzH_1^2+VS+(H_1-3J)\{QW-3x^2Y^2Z^2\}=0,
\]
an equation which, observing that
\[
J=-(1+8l^3)xyz,
\]
and assuming also
\[
\Theta=(1+8l^3)^2Q,
\]
may be written
\[
U(H_1^2-3H_0J)+\Theta V^2=-(1+8l^3)V\Theta,
\]
which gives the required decomposition, viz., $\Theta$ having this value, the conic will have a five-pointic contact. Reducing by the last equation, and throwing out the factor $V$, we find
\[
-9(1+8l^3)V^2-\tfrac{16}{3}H_1^2+8H_0H_1+\Theta V^2=0
\]
for the equation of the line joining the point of contact with the point of simple intersection. And if in this equation we write $H_1=-(1+8l^3)xyz$, and $\Theta=(1+8l^3)^2Q$, we obtain, finally, for the equation of the line in question,
\[
9x^2y^2z^2\cdot S-6x^4y^4z^4\cdot Q\Theta+Q^2V^2=0,
\]
which is the before-mentioned result.

53. It only remains to verify the assumed equation
\[
-xyzH_1^2+VS+(H_1-3J)\{QW-3x^2Y^2Z^2\}=0.
\]
We may write
\[
-Q=x^6+6lx^4yz+12l^2x^3y^2z^2-x^4y^4,
\]
and then observing that
\begin{align*}
W &= xX^2+\ldots+2lxYZ+\ldots,\\
H_1 &= 3l^2xX^2+\ldots-(1+2l^3)xYZ-\ldots,
\end{align*}
we find at once
\begin{multline*}
-xyzH_1^2=-(1+8l^3)x^5y^3z^3X^2\\
+\{2l\cdot x^7+11l^2\cdot x^5yz-3x^4y^2z^2-2l\cdot x^3y^3z^3\}YZ
\end{multline*}
Next writing
\begin{align*}
H_1 &= (3l^2x^2-(1+2l^3)yz)X+\ldots,\\
V &= (x^2+2lyz)X+\ldots,\\
S &= x\{(1+8l^3)x^4+(4l+4ll^4)x^3yz+(-2l^2+2l^5)y^3z^3\}X+\ldots,
\end{align*}
and forming the expression for $-xyzH_1^2+VS$, the coefficient of $X^2$ is
\begin{multline*}
-xyz\{3l^2x^2-(1+2l^3)yz\}^2\\
+(x^2+2lyz)x\{(1+8l^3)x^4+(4l+4ll^4)x^3yz+(-2l^2+2l^5)y^3z^3\},
\end{multline*}
which is
\[
=(1+8l^3)\{x^6+6lx^4yz+12l^2x^3y^2z^2-x^4y^4\};
\]
the coefficient of $YZ$ is
\begin{multline*}
-2xyz(3ly^2-(1+2l^3)zx)(3lz^2-(1+2l^3)xy)\\
+(y^2+2lzx)z\{(1+8l^3)z^4+(4l+4ll^4)z^3xy+(-2l^2+2l^5)x^3y^3\}\\
+(z^2+2lxy)y\{(1+8l^3)y^4+(4l+4ll^4)y^3zx+(-2l^2+2l^5)x^3y^3\},
\end{multline*}
which is
\[
=(1+8l^3)\{y^4z^2(x^4+y^3)+3l\cdot x^2y^3z^3+12l^2x^3yz(x^4+y^3)-2lx(y^4+z^4)-2xy^3z^3\};
\]
or substituting for $y^4z^2$ and $y^4+z^4$ the values $-x^4-6lx^3yz$ and $(x^4+6lx^3yz)^2-2y^4z^4$ respectively, this is
\[
=(1+8l^3)(2lx^7+12l^2x^5yz-3x^4y^2z^2-2lx^3y^3z^2).
\]
We have thus
\begin{multline*}
-xyzH_1^2+VS\\
=(1+8l^3)\begin{aligned}[t]
&\{(x^6+6lx^4yz+12l^2x^3y^2z^2-x^4y^4)X^2\\
&+(2lx^7+12l^2x^5yz-3x^4y^2z^2-2lx^3y^3z^3)YZ\},
\end{aligned}
\end{multline*}
and the equation
\[
-xyzH_1^2+VS+(1+8l^3)\{QW-3x^2Y^2Z^2\}=0
\]
is thus verified.

\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Addition.} --- The foregoing memoir was communicated to Mr~Salmon, and I am indebted to him for two notes, containing the extension to a curve of any order, of the preceding investigation for the case of a cubic; I reproduce this extension in the following section.

\subsection*{V. Extension of the last preceding method to a curve of any order}

54. Consider the curve of the $m$th order $U=0$, and in the place of the coordinates write $\lambda x+\mu X$, $\lambda y+\mu Y$, $\lambda z+\mu Z$, where, as before, $(x,y,z)$ are the coordinates of the point of the curve, and $X$, $Y$, $Z$ current coordinates; the term involving $\lambda^m$ vanishes, and dividing out the factor $\mu$ the equation becomes
\[
\lambda^{m-1}DU+\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda^{m-2}\mu D^2U+\tfrac{1}{6}\lambda^{m-3}\mu^2D^3U+\&c.=0.
\]
Making the like substitution in $D^2U-\Pi\cdot DU=0$, the assumed equation of the five-pointic conic, the factor $\mu$ divides out and the equation becomes
\begin{multline*}
\lambda^{m-2}(D^2U-\Pi\cdot DU)+\lambda^{m-3}\mu\{D^3U-\Pi\cdot D^2U\}\\
+\lambda^{m-4}\mu^2\{\tfrac{1}{2}D^4U-\Pi\cdot\tfrac{1}{6}D^3U\}+\&c.=0,
\end{multline*}
or, what is the same thing,
\[
\lambda DU\{2(m-1)-\Pi\}+\mu\{D^2U-\Pi\cdot DU\}=0;
\]
and if from the two equations we eliminate $\lambda$, $\mu$, the result, throwing out the factor $DU$, is
\begin{multline*}
(D^2U-\Pi\cdot DU)\lambda^{m-2}-\tfrac{1}{2}\{2(m-1)-\Pi\}DU\cdot(D^2U-\Pi\cdot DU)\lambda^{m-3}\\
+\&c.\ (DU)^2\ldots=0,
\end{multline*}
where all the terms after the second contain the factor $DU$; the condition in order that the equation may divide by $DU$, is consequently $2(m-1)-\Pi=2$, or $\Pi=2(m-2)$, the condition of a three-pointic contact. Substituting this value, and dividing by $DU$, the equation becomes
\begin{multline*}
(D^2U-\Pi\cdot DU)\lambda^{m-2}-U(D^2U-\Pi\cdot DU)\lambda^{m-3}\\
+\tfrac{1}{2}D^2U\cdot DU(D^2U-\Pi\cdot DU)\lambda^{m-4}+\&c.=0,
\end{multline*}
which will be divisible by $DU$ if $-\Pi D^2U+\tfrac{1}{3}D^3U$ is divisible by $DU$, and the condition for this is found to be, as before,
\[
U=\tfrac{2}{3}DH+\Lambda\cdot DU,
\]
where $\Lambda$ is arbitrary; we have thus the conditions of a four-pointic contact.

55. Substituting this value of $\Pi$, we see that $D^2U-\tfrac{2}{3}DH\cdot D\cdot U$ divides by $DU$, viz. there exists an identical equation,
\[
D^2U-\tfrac{2}{3}DH\cdot D^2U=IU+J\cdot DU;
\]
and hence if $U=0$,
\[
\tfrac{1}{2}(D^2U-\tfrac{2}{3}DH\cdot D^2U)=J,
\]
where $J$ is a quadric function of $(X,Y,Z)$. I do not know the general form of this function, but Mr~Salmon has obtained a result which may be generalized as follows, viz. writing for $X$, $Y$, $Z$ the values $B\nu-C\mu$, $C\lambda-A\nu$, $A\mu-B\lambda$ (where, as before, $A$, $B$, $C$ are the first derived functions of $U$ and $\lambda$, $\mu$, $\nu$ are arbitrary), the expression for $J$ is
\[
\frac{D^2U}{\tfrac{1}{2}(m-1)(m-2)H}-\frac{(D^2U)^2}{\{(m-1)H\}^2},
\]
a formula which will be presently useful.

56. The foregoing equation may be written
\begin{multline*}
(D^2U)^{m-2}\{-\Pi D^2U+\tfrac{1}{3}D^3U\}\\
+(D^2U)^{m-3}DU\{(m-2)W D^2U-\tfrac{1}{2}(m-3)UD^3U-\tfrac{1}{6}D^4U\}+\&c.\ (DU)^2\ldots=0;
\end{multline*}
and the term $-\Pi D^2U+\tfrac{1}{3}D^3U$ is equal to
\[
\tfrac{2}{3}(D^2U-\tfrac{2}{3}DH\cdot D^2U)-\Lambda\cdot DU\cdot D^2U=J\cdot DU-\Lambda\cdot DU\cdot D^2U.
\]
Substituting this value the equation divides by $DU$, and throwing out this factor it becomes
\begin{multline*}
(D^2U)^{m-2}(J-\Lambda D^2U)\\
+(D^2U)^{m-3}\{(m-2)W D^2U-\tfrac{1}{2}(m-3)UD^3U-\tfrac{1}{6}D^4U\}+\&c.\ DU=0;
\end{multline*}
or observing that $\tfrac{1}{3}\Pi D^3U=\Pi\cdot D\cdot\tfrac{1}{3}D^2U+\text{term containing }DU$, this may be written
\[
(D^2U)^{m-2}(\tfrac{1}{3}J-\Lambda D^2U)+(D^2U)^{m-3}\{\Pi\cdot D\cdot\tfrac{1}{3}D^2U+\tfrac{1}{6}D^4U\}+\&c.\ DU=0.
\]

57. If the equation divides by $DU$ we shall have a five-pointic contact; the condition for this is that
\[
-\Lambda(D^2U)^2+\tfrac{1}{3}J\cdot D^2U+\tfrac{1}{3}W D^3U+\tfrac{1}{6}D^4U
\]
may divide by $DU$, or more simply that
\[
-\Lambda(D^2U)^2+\tfrac{1}{3}J\cdot D^2U+\tfrac{1}{3}(\tfrac{2}{3}DH)D^2U-\tfrac{1}{6}D^4U
\]
may divide by $DU$, or, what is the same thing, the function in question must vanish in virtue of the substitution of the values $B\nu-C\mu$, $C\lambda-A\nu$, $A\mu-B\lambda$ in the place of $X$, $Y$, $Z$. The expression for $J$ has just been given; we have besides
\[
\tfrac{1}{3}D^2U=\tfrac{1}{2}(m-1)(m-2)H,\quad D^2U=-(m-1)^2H,
\]
where the values of $Q\!f$, $Q_2$ are given (ante, No.~27); we have thus
\[
\tfrac{1}{3}(D^2U)^2=\tfrac{1}{4}(m-1)^2(m-2)^2H^2,\qquad \tfrac{1}{3}J\cdot D^2U=\tfrac{1}{6}(m-1)^2(m-2)H\cdot J,
\]
and consequently
\[
\Lambda=\frac{(m-2)H}{4}-\frac{J}{\tfrac{1}{2}(m-1)(m-2)H^2},
\]
which agrees with the result before obtained, and thus the present method gives the complete solution of the problem.\hfill$\square$

\newpage
%======================================================================
\section[262. On the Equation of Differences]{262. On the Equation of Differences for an Equation of Any Order, and in Particular for the Equations of the Orders Two, Three, Four, and Five}
%======================================================================

\textbf{[From the \textit{Philosophical Transactions} of the Royal Society of London, vol.~CL (1860), pp.~93--112. Received March~2, --- Read March~29, 1860.]}

The term, \textit{equation of differences}, denotes the equation for the squared differences of the roots of a given equation; the equation of differences afforded a means of determining the number of real roots, and also limits for the real roots, of a given numerical equation, and was upon this account long ago sought for by geometers.

In the Philosophical Transactions for 1763, Waring gives, but without demonstration or indication of the mode of obtaining it, the equation of differences for an equation of the fifth order wanting the second term: the result was probably obtained by the method of symmetric functions. This method is employed in the \textit{Meditationes Algebraicae} (1782), where the equation of differences is given for the equations of the third and fourth orders wanting the second terms; and in p.~85 the before-mentioned result for the equation of the fifth order wanting the second term, is reproduced.

The formulae for obtaining by this method the equation of differences, are fully developed by Lagrange in the \textit{Trait\'{e} des \'{E}quations Num\'{e}riques} (1808); and he finds by means of them the equation of differences for the equations of the orders two and three, and for the equation of the fourth order wanting the second term; and in Note~III. he gives, after Waring, the result for the equation of the fifth order wanting the second term.

It occurred to me that the equation of differences could be most easily calculated by the following method. The coefficients of the equation of differences, \textit{qua} functions of the differences of the roots of the given equation, are leading coefficients of covariants, or (to use a shorter expression) they are ``Seminvariants,''\footnote{The term ``Seminvariant'' seems to me preferable to M.~Brioschi's term ``Peninvariant.''} that is, each of them is a function of the coefficients which is reduced to zero by one of the two operators which reduce an invariant to zero. In virtue of this property they can be calculated, when their values are known for the particular case in which one of the coefficients of the given equation is zero.

To fix the ideas, let the given equation be $(\,\flat\,\frown\, v,1)^n=0$; then, when the last coefficient or constant term vanishes, the equation breaks up into $v=0$ and into an equation of the degree $n-1$, which I call the \textit{reduced equation}; the equation of differences will break up into two equations, one of which is the equation of differences for the reduced equation, the other is the equation for the squares of the roots of the same reduced equation. This hardly requires a proof; let the roots of the given equation be $\alpha$, $\beta$, $\gamma$, $\delta$, \&c., those of the equation of differences are $(\alpha-\beta)^2$, $(\alpha-\gamma)^2$, $(\alpha-\delta)^2$, \&c., $(\beta-\gamma)^2$, $(\beta-\delta)^2$, $(\gamma-\delta)^2$, \&c.; but in putting the constant term equal to zero, we in effect put one of the roots, say $\alpha$, equal to zero; the roots of the equation of differences thus become $\beta^2$, $\gamma^2$, $\delta^2$, \&c., $(\beta-\gamma)^2$, $(\beta-\delta)^2$, $(\gamma-\delta)^2$, \&c. The equation for the squares of the roots can be found without the slightest difficulty; hence if the equation of differences for the reduced equation of the order $n-1$ is known, we can, by combining it with the equation for the squares of the roots, form the equation of differences for the given equation with the constant term put equal to zero, and thence by the above-mentioned property of the Seminvariancy of the coefficients, find the equation of differences for the given equation.

The present memoir shows the application of the process to equations of the orders two, three, four, and five: part of the calculation for the equation of the fifth order was kindly performed for me by the Rev.~R.~Harley. It is to be noticed that the best course is to apply the method in the first instance to the forms $(a,b,\ldots\,\flat\,\frown\, v,1)^n=0$, without numerical coefficients (or, as they may be termed, the \textit{denumerate} forms), and to pass from the results so obtained to those which belong to the forms $(a,b,\ldots\,\frown\, v,1)^n=0$, or \textit{standard} forms. The equation of differences, for $(a-\beta)^2$, \&c., the coefficients of which are seminvariants, naturally leads to the consideration of a more general equation having for its roots $(\alpha-\beta)^2(\alpha-\gamma)^2(x-\delta)^2\ldots$ \&c., the coefficients of which are covariants; and in fact, when, as for equations of the orders two, three, and four, all the covariants are known, such covariant equation can be at once formed from the equation of differences; for equations of the fifth order, however, where the covariants are not calculated beyond a certain degree, [they are now all calculated, see 141 and 143], only a few of the coefficients of the covariant equation can be thus at once formed. At the conclusion of the memoir, I show how the equation of differences for an equation of the order $n$ can be obtained by the elimination of a single quantity from two equations each of the order $n-1$; and by applying to these two equations the simplification which I have made in Bezout's abridged method of elimination, I exhibit the equation of differences for the given equation of the order $n$, in a compendious form by means of a determinant; the first-mentioned method is, however, that which is best adapted for the actual development of the equation of differences for the equation of a given order.

\vspace{6pt}

The equations successively considered are
\begin{align*}
(a,b,c\,\flat\,\frown\, v,1)^2 &= 0,\\
(a,b,c,d\,\flat\,\frown\, v,1)^3 &= 0,\\
(a,b,c,d,e\,\flat\,\frown\, v,1)^4 &= 0,\\
(a,b,c,d,e,f\,\flat\,\frown\, v,1)^5 &= 0.
\end{align*}
The equation of differences for the quadric, and that for the squares of the roots, are considered to be known, and the other results are derived from them: it will be convenient to write down in the first instance the results for the quadric, the cubic, and the quartic equations, and then explain the process of obtaining them.

\subsection*{Results for the quadric equation}

For the quadric equation,

Equation of differences is, $0=$
\[
a^2\theta+4(ac-b^2)=0.
\]

Equation for the squares of the roots is, $0=$
\[
a^2x+(c^2+1)+2(ac+2b^2)x+(b^2-1)=0.
\]

\subsection*{For the cubic equation}

Equation of differences is, $0=$
\[
a^4\theta^3\cdot(ac-8b^2)+a^2\theta^2\cdot(a^2d^2+\ldots)+\ldots=0,
\]
the explicit form being
\begin{multline*}
0=a^4\theta^3\cdot(ac-8b^2)\\
+a^2\theta^2\cdot(a^2d^2+27a^2b^2c+96ab^2d-4b^3c)\\
+\theta\cdot(18a^3bcd-4a^3c^3+6a^2b^3d+a^2b^2c^2-27a^4d^2)\\
+(b^2c^2-4b^3d-4ac^3+18abcd-27a^2d^2).
\end{multline*}

Equation for the squares of the roots is, $0=$
\begin{multline*}
a^3x^3+(c^3+1)x^3+(abc-2)x^2+(ab^2c+4)x\\
+(d^3+1)+(bd-2)+\ldots=0.
\end{multline*}

\subsection*{For the quartic equation}

For the quartic equation,

Equation of differences is, $0=$
\begin{multline*}
a^6\theta^6\cdot(a^2e^2+\ldots)+a^4\theta^5\cdot(a^2bc^2e+\ldots)+a^2\theta^4\cdot(\ldots)\\
+\theta^3\cdot(\ldots)+\theta^2\cdot(\ldots)+\theta\cdot(\ldots)+\Disc=0,
\end{multline*}
where the discriminant is the last coefficient.

The multiplication of the equation of differences and the equation for the squares of the roots of the quadric equation, gives the equation, $0=$
\begin{multline*}
a^3\theta^3\cdot(ac+6)+a^4\theta^2\cdot(a^2b^2c+16)+\ldots\\
+(b^4d^2-1)+\ldots,
\end{multline*}
