\newpage
\section*{[276]}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{276. On the Analytical Theory of the Conic}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC.}
\end{center}
\markboth{ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC.}{ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC.}

\textbf{16.} The last equation shows that $(a,..\!\qform{\xi}{\eta\zeta' - \eta'\zeta,\ \zeta\xi' - \zeta'\xi,\ \xi\eta' - \xi'\eta})^{2}$, considered as a function of $(\xi', \eta', \zeta')$, breaks up into factors. Or since the expression is not altered by interchanging $(\xi', \eta', \zeta')$ and $(\xi, \eta, \zeta)$, the same expression, considered as a function of $(\xi, \eta, \zeta)$, breaks up into factors. It is in fact easy to see that any quantic whatever, $(*\!\qform{\xi}{\eta\zeta' - \eta'\zeta,\ \zeta\xi' - \zeta'\xi,\ \xi\eta' - \xi'\eta})^{m}$, considered as a function of $(\xi, \eta, \zeta)$, breaks up into linear factors; for in virtue of the equation $\xi'(\eta\zeta' - \eta'\zeta) + \eta'(\zeta\xi' - \zeta'\xi) + \zeta'(\xi\eta' - \xi'\eta) = 0$, any one of the quantities $\eta\zeta' - \eta'\zeta$, $\zeta\xi' - \zeta'\xi$, $\xi\eta' - \xi'\eta$ can be expressed as a linear function of the other two; so that the quantic can be expressed as a linear function of any two of the three quantities; and \textit{qu\`a} homogeneous function of two quantities, it of course breaks up into factors, linear functions of these two quantities.

We may in all the formul\ae\ interchange $(x', y', z')$ and $(x, y, z)$, writing $(a', b', c', f', g', h')$ in the place of $(a, b, c, f, g, h)$.

\textbf{17.} Putting, in like manner,
\[
(A, B, C, F, G, H \!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,\ zx' - z'x,\ xy' - x'y})^{2}
= (\mfA, \mfB, \mfC, \mfF, \mfG, \mfH \!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2},
\]
so that
\begin{alignat*}{3}
\mfA &= \phantom{-}Cy^{2} + Bz^{2} - 2Fyz, &\qquad
\mfB &= \phantom{-}Az^{2} + Cx^{2} - 2Gzx, \\
\mfC &= \phantom{-}Bx^{2} + Ay^{2} - 2Hxy, \\
\mfF &= -Ayz - Fx^{2} + \phantom{-}Gxy + Hzx, \\
\mfG &= -Bzx + Fxy - \phantom{-}Gy^{2} + Hyz, \\
\mfH &= -Cxy + Fzx + \phantom{-}Gyz - Hz^{2},
\end{alignat*}
we obtain
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
\mfA, & \mfH, & \mfG \\
\mfH, & \mfB, & \mfF \\
\mfG, & \mfF, & \mfC
\end{vmatrix}
\!\qform{}{x, y, z} = (0, 0, 0),
\]
viz.
\[
\mfA x + \mfH y + \mfG z = 0,\ \&\mathrm{c}.
\]
Also
\begin{multline*}
(\mfB\mfC - \mfF^{2},\ \mfC\mfA - \mfG^{2},\ \mfA\mfB - \mfH^{2},\ \\
\mfG\mfH - \mfA\mfF,\ \mfH\mfF - \mfB\mfG,\ \mfF\mfG - \mfC\mfH) \\
= (x, y, z)^{2} \cdot K(a, b, c, f, g, h \!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2};
\end{multline*}
that is
\[
\mfB\mfC - \mfF^{2} = x^{2}\, K(a, b, c, f, g, h \!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2},\ \&\mathrm{c}.;
\]
whence also
\begin{align*}
(\mfB\mfC - \mfF^{2},..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})^{2}
&= (\lambda x + \mu y + \nu z)^{2} \cdot K(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2},\\
(\mfB\mfC - \mfF^{2},..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{\lambda', \mu', \nu'})
&= (\lambda x + \mu y + \nu z)(\lambda' x + \mu' y + \nu' z) \cdot K(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2};
\end{align*}
and moreover
\[
\mfA\mfB\mfC - \mfA\mfF^{2} - \mfB\mfG^{2} - \mfC\mfH^{2} + 2\mfF\mfG\mfH = 0.
\]

\newpage
\section*{[276] (continued)}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC (continued).}
\end{center}

The last equation shows that $(A,..\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,\ zx' - z'x,\ xy' - x'y})^{2}$, considered as a function of $(x', y', z')$, breaks up into factors, or (what is the same thing) this expression, considered as a function of $(x, y, z)$, breaks up into factors; we may in all the formul\ae\ interchange $(x, y, z)$ and $(x', y', z')$, writing $(\mfA', \mfB', \mfC', \mfF', \mfG', \mfH')$ in the place of $(\mfA, \mfB, \mfC, \mfF, \mfG, \mfH)$.

\medskip
\begin{center}
\textit{Article Nos.\ 18 to 28, relating to a single conic in connexion with a point or line.}
\end{center}
\medskip

\textbf{18.} I apply the decomposition formula to the function $(A,..\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..})^{2}$, which, considered as a function of $(x, y, z)$, breaks up into factors. We have
\begin{align*}
&(A,..\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..})^{2} = (\mfA',..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2}\\
&\qquad = \frac{1}{(\mfA',..\!\qform{}{l, m, n})^{2}} \text{ Product of}\\
&\qquad\qquad (\mfA',..\!\qform{}{l, m, n}\!\qform{}{x, y, z})
\pm \frac{(\mfB'\mfC' - \mfF'^{2},..\!\qform{}{mz - ny,..}\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})}
{\sqrt{- (\mfB'\mfC' - \mfF'^{2},..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})^{2}}}.
\end{align*}
But we have
\begin{align*}
&(\mfA',..\!\qform{}{l, m, n})^{2} &&= (A,..\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..})^{2},\\
&(\mfA',..\!\qform{}{l, m, n}\!\qform{}{x, y, z}) &&= (A,..\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..}\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..}),\\
&(\mfB'\mfC' - \mfF'^{2},..\!\qform{}{mz - ny,..}\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})\\
&\qquad &&= [x'(mz - ny) + y'(nx - lz) + z'(ly - mx)](\lambda x' + \mu y' + \nu z')\, K(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2},\\
&(\mfB'\mfC' - \mfF'^{2},..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})^{2} &&= \phantom{[x'(mz - ny) + y'(nx - lz) + z'(ly - mx)]}
(\lambda x' + \mu y' + \nu z')^{2}\, K(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2},
\end{align*}
and thence
\[
\frac{(\mfB'\mfC' - \mfF'^{2},..\!\qform{}{mz - ny,..}\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})}
{\sqrt{- (\mfB'\mfC' - \mfF'^{2},..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})^{2}}}
=
\begin{vmatrix}
x, & y, & z \\
x', & y', & z' \\
l, & m, & n
\end{vmatrix}
\sqrt{- K(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2}},
\]
whence we have
\begin{align*}
&(A,..\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..})^{2}
= \frac{1}{(A,..\!\qform{}{mz' - ny'})^{2}} \text{ Product of}\\
&\qquad (A,..\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..}\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..})
\pm
\begin{vmatrix}
x, & y, & z \\
x', & y', & z' \\
l, & m, & n
\end{vmatrix}
\sqrt{- K(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2}};
\end{align*}
and the identical equation
\[
(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} \cdot (a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2} - [(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z}\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})]^{2}
= (A,..\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..})^{2}
\]

\newpage
\section*{[276] (continued)}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC (continued).}
\end{center}

now gives
\[
\text{I.}\quad
(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} = \text{Quotient by }(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2}\text{ of}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
&[(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z}\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})]^{2}\\
&+ \text{Quotient by }(A,..\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..})^{2}\text{ of Product}\\
&\Big\{(A,..\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..}\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..})
\pm
\begin{vmatrix}
x, & y, & z \\
x', & y', & z' \\
l, & m, & n
\end{vmatrix}
\sqrt{-K(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2}}\Big\},
\end{aligned}
\right.
\]
where the Product part may also be written
\begin{multline*}
(a,..\!\qform{}{l, m, n}\!\qform{}{x', y', z'}) \cdot (a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z}\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})\\
- (a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2} \cdot (a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z}\!\qform{}{l, m, n})\\
\pm \sqrt{-K(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2}}
\begin{vmatrix}
x, & y, & z \\
x', & y', & z' \\
l, & m, & n
\end{vmatrix}.
\end{multline*}

\textbf{19.} Writing in the formula I.
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
a, & h, & g \\
h, & b, & f \\
g, & f, & c
\end{vmatrix}
\!\qform{}{x', y', z'} = (\xi', \eta', \zeta'),
\]
we have
\[
(x', y', z') = \frac{1}{K}
\begin{vmatrix}
A, & H, & G \\
H, & B, & F \\
G, & F, & C
\end{vmatrix}
\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'},
\]
and thence
\begin{align*}
K(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'}) &= (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2},\\
(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z}\!\qform{}{x', y', z'}) &= \xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z.
\end{align*}
Assume
\[
(l, m, n) = (\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',\ \lambda\zeta' - \nu\xi',\ \mu\xi' - \lambda\eta'),
\]
then from the foregoing values of $(x', y', z')$
\begin{align*}
mz' - ny' &= \frac{1}{K}\Big\{ (\lambda\zeta' - \nu\xi')(G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta')
- (\mu\xi' - \lambda\eta')(H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta') \Big\}\\
&= \frac{1}{K}\Big\{ \lambda\, [\xi'(A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta')
+ \eta'(H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta')
+ \zeta'(G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta')]\\
&\qquad\quad - \lambda\xi'(A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta')
- \mu\xi'(H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta')
- \nu\xi'(G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta') \Big\},
\end{align*}

\newpage
\section*{[276] (continued)}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC (continued).}
\end{center}

that is
\[
mz' - ny' = \frac{1}{K}\Big\{ \lambda\,(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}
- \xi'(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}) \Big\},
\]
and similarly
\begin{align*}
nx' - lz' &= \frac{1}{K}\Big\{ \mu\,(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}
- \eta'(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}) \Big\},\\
ly' - mx' &= \frac{1}{K}\Big\{ \nu\,(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}
- \zeta'(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}) \Big\};
\end{align*}
and thence
\begin{align*}
&(A, H, G \!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..})\\
&\qquad = \frac{1}{K}\Big\{\phantom{-}(A, H, G \!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}) \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}\\
&\qquad\qquad\quad - (A, H, G \!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}) \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}) \Big\},
\end{align*}
with the like equations, writing $H$, $B$, $F$ and $G$, $F$, $C$ in the place of $A$, $H$, $G$ successively: and we then have
\begin{align*}
&(A,..\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..})^{2}\\
&\qquad = \frac{1}{K}\Big\{\phantom{-}(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..}) \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}\\
&\qquad\qquad\quad - (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..}) \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}) \Big\}.
\end{align*}
But the foregoing values of $mz' - ny'$, $nx' - lz'$, $ly' - mx'$ give also
\begin{align*}
&(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..})\\
&\qquad = \frac{1}{K}\Big\{(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})^{2} \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}
- [(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})]^{2}\Big\},\\
&(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..})\\
&\qquad = \frac{1}{K}\Big\{(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}) \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}
- (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2} \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})\Big\} = 0;
\end{align*}
so that
\begin{align*}
&(A,..\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..})^{2}\\
&\qquad = \frac{1}{K^{2}}(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}
\cdot \{(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})^{2} \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}
- [(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})]^{2}\}\\
&\qquad = \frac{1}{K}(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2} \cdot (a,..\!\qform{}{\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',..})^{2}.
\end{align*}

\newpage
\section*{[276] (continued)}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC (continued).}
\end{center}

Similarly,
\begin{align*}
&(A,..\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..}\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..})\\
&\qquad = \frac{1}{K}\Big\{\phantom{-}(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..}) \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}\\
&\qquad\qquad\quad - (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..}) \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}) \Big\}.
\end{align*}
But
\begin{align*}
&(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..})\\
&\qquad = \phantom{+} (A\lambda + H\mu + G\nu)(yz' - y'z)\\
&\qquad\quad + (H\lambda + B\mu + F\nu)(zx' - z'x)\\
&\qquad\quad + (G\lambda + F\mu + C\nu)(xy' - x'y),\\
&\qquad = \phantom{+} x\,[(G\lambda + F\mu + C\nu) - z'(H\lambda + B\mu + F\nu)]\\
&\qquad\quad + y\,[z'(A\lambda + H\mu + G\nu) - x'(G\lambda + F\mu + C\nu)]\\
&\qquad\quad + z\,[x'(H\lambda + B\mu + F\nu) - y'(A\lambda + H\mu + G\nu)],
\end{align*}
which, substituting for $x'$, $y'$, $z'$ their values
\[
(x', y', z') = \frac{1}{K}
\begin{vmatrix}
A, & H, & G \\
H, & B, & F \\
G, & F, & C
\end{vmatrix}
\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'},
\]
becomes
\begin{multline*}
= \frac{1}{K}\Big\{\phantom{+} x\,[(BC - F^{2})(\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta') + (FG - CH)(\lambda\zeta' - \nu\xi') + (HF - BG)(\mu\xi' - \lambda\eta')]\\
+ y\,[(FG - CH)(\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta') + (CA - G^{2})(\lambda\zeta' - \nu\xi') + (GH - AF)(\mu\xi' - \lambda\eta')]\\
+ z\,[(HF - BG)(\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta') + (GH - AF)(\lambda\zeta' - \nu\xi') + (AB - H^{2})(\mu\xi' - \lambda\eta')]\Big\},
\end{multline*}
which is
\[
= (a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z}\!\qform{}{\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',..});
\]
and by merely writing $(\xi', \eta', \zeta')$ in the place of $(\lambda, \mu, \nu)$, we have
\[
(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..}) = 0;
\]
so that we find
\begin{align*}
&(A,..\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..}\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..})\\
&\qquad = \frac{1}{K}(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2} \cdot (a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z}\!\qform{}{\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',\ \lambda\zeta' - \nu\xi',\ \mu\xi' - \lambda\eta'}).
\end{align*}

\newpage
\section*{[276] (continued)}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC (continued).}
\end{center}

Now, writing the formula I.\ in the form
\[
(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} = \text{Quotient by } K(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2}\text{ of}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
&K\,[(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z}\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})]^{2}\\
&+ \text{Quotient by } K(A,..\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..})^{2}\text{ of}\\
&K^{2}\,\{[(A,..\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..}\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..})]^{2}
+
\begin{vmatrix}
x, & y, & z \\
x', & y', & z' \\
l, & m, & n
\end{vmatrix}^{2}
K(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2}\},
\end{aligned}
\right.
\]
the right-hand side is
\[
= \text{Quotient by }(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}\text{ of}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
&K\,(\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z)^{2}\\
&+ \text{Quotient by }(a,..\!\qform{}{\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',..})^{2}\,(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}\text{ of}\\
&\{[(a,..\!\qform{}{\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',..}\!\qform{}{x, y, z}) \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}]^{2}
+ \Pi^{2}\,(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}\},
\end{aligned}
\right.
\]
where
\[
\Pi = K
\begin{vmatrix}
x, & y, & z \\
x', & y', & z' \\
l, & m, & n
\end{vmatrix},
\]
or, what is the same thing,
\[
\Pi =
\begin{vmatrix}
x, & y, & z \\
Kx', & Ky', & Kz' \\
l, & m, & n
\end{vmatrix}
=
\begin{vmatrix}
x, & y, & z \\
A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta', & H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta', & G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta' \\
\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta', & \lambda\zeta' - \nu\xi', & \mu\xi' - \lambda\eta'
\end{vmatrix}.
\]
More simply, the right-hand side is
\[
= \text{Quotient by }(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}\text{ of}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
&K\,(\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z)^{2}\\
&+ \text{Quotient by }(a,..\!\qform{}{\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',..})^{2}\text{ of}\\
&\{[(a,..\!\qform{}{\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',..}\!\qform{}{x, y, z})]^{2} \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2} + \Pi^{2}\};
\end{aligned}
\right.
\]
or restoring the left-hand side, and resolving into its linear factors the function in $\{\}$, we have
\[
\text{II.}\quad
(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} = \text{Quotient by }(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}\text{ of}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
&K\,(\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z)^{2}\\
&+ \text{Quotient by }(a,..\!\qform{}{\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',..})^{2}\text{ of Product}\\
&\{\Pi \pm \sqrt{-(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2} \cdot (a,..\!\qform{}{\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',..}\!\qform{}{x, y, z})}\},
\end{aligned}
\right.
\]

\newpage
\section*{[276] (continued)}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC (continued).}
\end{center}

where $\Pi$ has the value given above, which may also be written
\begin{align*}
\Pi &= \phantom{-} (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})(\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z)\\
&\quad - (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}(\lambda x + \mu y + \nu z).
\end{align*}

\textbf{20.} We deduce at once the inverse or reciprocal formul\ae
\[
\text{III.}\quad
(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi, \eta, \zeta})^{2} = \text{Quotient by }(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}\text{ of}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
&[(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi, \eta, \zeta}\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})]^{2}\\
&+ \text{Quotient by }(a,..\!\qform{}{\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',..})^{2}\text{ of } K \text{ into Product}\\
&\{(a,..\!\qform{}{\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',..}\!\qform{}{\eta\zeta' - \eta'\zeta,..})
\pm \sqrt{-(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}}
\begin{vmatrix}
\xi, & \eta, & \zeta \\
\xi', & \eta', & \zeta' \\
\lambda, & \mu, & \nu
\end{vmatrix}\},
\end{aligned}
\right.
\]
where the Product part may also be written
\begin{multline*}
\text{Product }\Big[
\frac{1}{K}(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}) \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}\!\qform{}{\xi, \eta, \zeta})\\
- \frac{1}{K}(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2} \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{\xi, \eta, \zeta})\\
\pm \sqrt{-(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}}
\begin{vmatrix}
\xi, & \eta, & \zeta \\
\xi', & \eta', & \zeta' \\
\lambda, & \mu, & \nu
\end{vmatrix}
\Big].
\end{multline*}

\textbf{21.} And also
\[
\text{IV.}\quad
(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi, \eta, \zeta})^{2} = \text{Quotient by }(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2}\text{ of}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
&K(\xi x' + \eta y' + \zeta z')^{2}\\
&+ \text{Quotient by }K(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda y' - mz',..})^{2}\text{ of Product}\\
&\{K\Phi \pm \sqrt{-K(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2}}\,(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda y' - mz',..}\!\qform{}{\xi, \eta, \zeta})\},
\end{aligned}
\right.
\]
where
\[
\Phi =
\begin{vmatrix}
\xi, & \eta, & \zeta \\
ax' + hy' + gz', & hx' + by' + fz', & gx' + fy' + cz' \\
ny' - mz', & lz' - nx', & mx' - ly'
\end{vmatrix},
\]
which may also be written
\begin{align*}
&= \phantom{-} (a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'}\!\qform{}{l, m, n})(x'\xi + y'\eta + z'\zeta)\\
&\quad - (a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2} \cdot (l\xi + m\eta + n\zeta).
\end{align*}

\textbf{22.} The geometrical signification is obvious. The formul\ae\ I.\ and II.\ each of them show that the equation
\[
(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} = 0
\]

\newpage
\section*{[276] (continued)}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC (continued).}
\end{center}

of the conic may be written in the form
\[
W^{2} + \frac{1}{M}QR = 0,
\]
where $Q = 0$, $R = 0$ are any two tangents of the conic, and $W = 0$ is the line joining the points of contact, or chord of contact corresponding to the two tangents; viz., in the formula I.\ we have
\begin{align*}
W &= (a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'}\!\qform{}{x, y, z}),\\
\left.\begin{array}{c} Q \\ R \end{array}\right\}
&= (A,..\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..}\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..})
\pm \sqrt{-K(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2}}
\begin{vmatrix}
x, & y, & z \\
x', & y', & z' \\
l, & m, & n
\end{vmatrix},
\end{align*}
(or for a different form of $Q$, $R$ see the formula). The quantities $(x', y', z')$ are the coordinates of the point of intersection of the two tangents, or pole of the chord of contact: $(l, m, n)$ are supernumerary arbitrary quantities, the values whereof do not affect the result\footnote{In a different point of view, viz.\ if we consider the formula I.\ as a transformation of the function $(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2}$, then $(x', y', z')$ and $(l, m, n)$ would be each of them supernumerary arbitrary quantities: and so in the other like cases.}. And in the formula II.\ we have
\begin{align*}
W &= \xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z,\\
\left.\begin{array}{c} Q \\ R \end{array}\right\}
&= \Pi \pm \sqrt{-(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}}\, (a,..\!\qform{}{\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',..}\!\qform{}{x, y, z}),
\end{align*}
(for the value of $\Pi$ see the formula). The quantities $(\xi', \eta', \zeta')$ are the line-coordinates of the chord of contact (viz.\ the point-equation of this line is $\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z = 0$); $(\lambda, \mu, \nu)$ are supernumerary arbitrary quantities.

\textbf{23.} In the like manner the formul\ae\ III.\ and IV.\ each of them show that the line-equation
\[
(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi, \eta, \zeta})^{2} = 0
\]
of the conic may be written in the form
\[
W^{2} + \frac{1}{M}QR = 0,
\]
where $Q = 0$, $R = 0$ are any two ineunts of the conic, and $W = 0$ is the point of intersection of the corresponding tangents; viz.\ in the formula III.\ we have
\begin{align*}
W &= (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}\!\qform{}{\xi, \eta, \zeta}),\\
\left.\begin{array}{c} Q \\ R \end{array}\right\}
&= (a,..\!\qform{}{\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',..})
\pm \sqrt{-(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}}
\begin{vmatrix}
\xi, & \eta, & \zeta \\
\xi', & \eta', & \zeta' \\
\lambda, & \mu, & \nu
\end{vmatrix},
\end{align*}
(for another form of $Q$, $R$ see the formula).

\newpage
\section*{[276] (continued)}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC (continued).}
\end{center}

The quantities $\xi'$, $\eta'$, $\zeta'$ are the line-coordinates of the line through the two ineunts, or chord of contact; $(\lambda, \mu, \nu)$ are supernumerary arbitrary quantities; and so in the formula IV.\ we have
\begin{align*}
W &= x'\xi + y'\eta + z'\zeta,\\
\left.\begin{array}{c} Q \\ R \end{array}\right\}
&= K\Phi \pm \sqrt{-K(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2}}\,(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda y' - mz',..}\!\qform{}{\xi, \eta, \zeta})
\end{align*}
(for the value of $\Phi$ see the formula), where $x', y', z'$ are the point-coordinates of the intersection of tangents at the two ineunts, or pole of the chord of contact; $(l, m, n)$ are supernumerary arbitrary quantities.

\textbf{24.} We may, instead of the supernumerary arbitrary quantities $(l, m, n)$ of the formula I., introduce the quantities $(\lambda, \mu, \nu)$, where
\[
(l, m, n) = \frac{1}{K}
\begin{vmatrix}
A, & H, & G \\
H, & B, & F \\
G, & F, & C
\end{vmatrix}
\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}.
\]
This gives
\begin{align*}
&(A, H, G \!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..})\\
&\qquad = A(mz' - ny') + H(nx' - lz') + G(ly' - mx')\\
&\qquad = x'(Hn - Gm) + y'(Gl - An) + z'(Am - Hl)\\
&\qquad = \frac{1}{K}\Big\{\phantom{+} x'\,[H(G\lambda + F\mu + C\nu) - G(H\lambda + B\mu + F\nu)]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad + y'\,[G(A\lambda + H\mu + G\nu) - A(G\lambda + F\mu + C\nu)]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad + z'\,[A(H\lambda + B\mu + F\nu) - H(A\lambda + H\mu + G\nu)]\Big\}\\
&\qquad = \phantom{\frac{1}{K}\Big\{} x'(g\mu - h\nu) + y'(f\mu - b\nu) + z'(c\mu - f\nu)\\
&\qquad = \phantom{\frac{1}{K}\Big\{} \mu(gx' + fy' + cz') - \nu(hx' + by' + fz');
\end{align*}
we have thus the system
\begin{align*}
(A, H, G \!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..}) &= \mu(gx' + fy' + cz') - \nu(hx' + by' + fz'),\\
(H, B, F \!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..}) &= \nu(ax' + hy' + gz') - \lambda(gx' + fy' + cz'),\\
(G, F, C \!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..}) &= \lambda(hx' + by' + fz') - \mu(ax' + hy' + gz'),
\end{align*}
and thence
\begin{align*}
&(A,..\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..}\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..})\\
&\qquad = -
\begin{vmatrix}
yz' - y'z, & zx' - z'x, & xy' - x'y \\
ax' + hy' + gz', & hx' + by' + fz', & gx' + fy' + cz' \\
\lambda, & \mu, & \nu
\end{vmatrix};
\end{align*}
or observing that the term in $\lambda$ is
\[
-(zx' - z'x)(gx' + fy' + cz') + (xy' - x'y)(hx' + by' + fz'),
\]

\newpage
\section*{[276] (continued)}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC (continued).}
\end{center}

which is
\begin{align*}
&= \phantom{-} x\,(x'(ax' + hy' + gz') + y'(hx' + by' + fz') + z'(gx' + fy' + cz'))\\
&\quad - x \cdot x'(ax' + hy' + gz')\\
&\quad - y \cdot x'(hx' + by' + fz')\\
&\quad - z \cdot x'(gx' + fy' + cz')\\
&= -x'(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z}\!\qform{}{x', y', z'}) + x(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2},
\end{align*}
with similar expressions for the terms in $\mu$, $\nu$, we have
\begin{align*}
&(A,..\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..}\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..})\\
&\qquad = -(\lambda x' + \mu y' + \nu z') \cdot (a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z}\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})
+ (\lambda x + \mu y + \nu z) \cdot (a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2};
\end{align*}
and so also
\begin{align*}
&(A,..\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..})^{2}\\
&\qquad = -(\lambda x' + \mu y' + \nu z') \cdot (a,..\!\qform{}{l, m, n}\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})
+ (\lambda l + \mu m + \nu n) \cdot (a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2},
\end{align*}
where
\begin{align*}
(a,..\!\qform{}{l, m, n}\!\qform{}{x', y', z'}) &= \lambda x' + \mu y' + \nu z',\\
\lambda l + \mu m + \nu n &= \frac{1}{K}(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})^{2},
\end{align*}
so that
\[
(A,..\!\qform{}{mz' - ny',..})^{2}
= -(\lambda x' + \mu y' + \nu z')^{2} + \frac{1}{K}(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})^{2} \cdot (a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2}.
\]
Moreover,
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
x, & y, & z \\
x', & y', & z' \\
l, & m, & n
\end{vmatrix}
= \frac{1}{K}\Big\{(A\lambda + H\mu + G\nu)(yz' - y'z)
+ (H\lambda + B\mu + F\nu)(zx' - z'x)
+ (G\lambda + F\mu + C\nu)(xy' - x'y)\Big\},
\]
which is
\[
\cdot = \frac{1}{K}(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..});
\]
and hence instead of the formula I.\ we have
\[
\text{I. (bis)}\quad
(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} = \text{Quotient by }(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2}\text{ of}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
&[(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z}\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})]^{2}\\
&+ \text{Quotient by }(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})^{2}(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2} - K(\lambda x' + \mu y' + \nu z')^{2}\text{ of } K \text{ into Product}\\
&\Big\{(\lambda x' + \mu y' + \nu z') \cdot (a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z}\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})
- (\lambda x + \mu y + \nu z) \cdot (a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2}\Big\}\\
&\quad \pm \frac{1}{K}\sqrt{-K(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2}}\,(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,..}).
\end{aligned}
\right.
\]

\textbf{25.} If, in like manner, in the formula II.\ we introduce, instead of $(\lambda, \mu, \nu)$, the new quantities $(l, m, n)$, where
\[
(\lambda, \mu, \nu) =
\begin{vmatrix}
a, & h, & g \\
h, & b, & f \\
g, & f, & c
\end{vmatrix}
\!\qform{}{l, m, n},
\]

\newpage
\section*{[276] (continued)}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC (continued).}
\end{center}

or, what is the same thing,
\[
(l, m, n) = \frac{1}{K}
\begin{vmatrix}
A, & H, & G \\
H, & B, & F \\
G, & F, & C
\end{vmatrix}
\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu},
\]
then we have
\begin{align*}
(a, h, g \!\qform{}{\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',..}) &= n\,(H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta') - m\,(G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta'),\\
(h, b, f \!\qform{}{\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',..}) &= l\,(G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta') - n\,(A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta'),\\
(g, f, c \!\qform{}{\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',..}) &= m\,(A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta') - l\,(H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta');
\end{align*}
and thence
\begin{align*}
&(a,..\!\qform{}{\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',..}\!\qform{}{x, y, z}) =
\begin{vmatrix}
x, & y, & z \\
A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta', & H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta', & G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta' \\
l, & m, & n
\end{vmatrix}\\
&\qquad = (A,..\!\qform{}{mz - ny,..}\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}),
\end{align*}
\begin{align*}
(a,..\!\qform{}{\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta',..})^{2}
&= \frac{1}{K}\Big\{(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})^{2} \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}
- [(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})]^{2}\Big\}\\
&= (a,..\!\qform{}{l, m, n})^{2} \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2} - K(l\xi' + m\eta' + n\zeta')^{2};
\end{align*}
\begin{align*}
V &=
\begin{vmatrix}
x, & y, & z \\
A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta', & H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta', & G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta' \\
\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta', & \lambda\zeta' - \nu\xi', & \mu\xi' - \lambda\eta'
\end{vmatrix}\\
&= (\nu\eta' - \mu\zeta') \cdot y\,(G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta') - z\,(H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta')\\
&\quad + (\lambda\zeta' - \nu\xi') \cdot z\,(A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta') - x\,(G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta')\\
&\quad + (\mu\xi' - \lambda\eta') \cdot x\,(H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta') - y\,(A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta')\\
&= \lambda\,\{(\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z)(A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta') - x(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}\}\\
&\quad + \mu\,\{(\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z)(H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta') - y(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}\}\\
&\quad + \nu\,\{(\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z)(G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta') - z(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}\}\\
&= (\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z) \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})
- (\lambda x + \mu y + \nu z) \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}\\
&= K(l\xi' + m\eta' + n\zeta') \cdot (\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z) - (a,..\!\qform{}{l, m, n}\!\qform{}{x, y, z}) \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2};
\end{align*}
and the formula II.\ thus becomes
\[
\text{II. (bis)}\quad
(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} = \text{Quotient by }(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}\text{ of}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
&K(\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z)^{2}\\
&+ \text{Quotient by }(a,..\!\qform{}{l, m, n})^{2} \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2} - K(l\xi' + m\eta' + n\zeta')^{2}\text{ of Product}\\
&\Big\{K(l\xi' + m\eta' + n\zeta') \cdot (\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z) - (a,..\!\qform{}{l, m, n}\!\qform{}{x, y, z}) \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}\Big\}\\
&\quad \pm \sqrt{-(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}}\,(A,..\!\qform{}{mz - ny,..}\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}).
\end{aligned}
\right.
\]

\newpage
\section*{[276] (continued)}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC (continued).}
\end{center}

\textbf{26.} And from these we at once deduce the inverse or reciprocal formul\ae
\[
\text{III. (bis)}\quad
(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi, \eta, \zeta})^{2} = \text{Quotient by }(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}\text{ of}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
&[(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi, \eta, \zeta}\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})]^{2}\\
&+ \text{Quotient by }(a,..\!\qform{}{l, m, n})^{2} \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2} - K(l\xi' + m\eta' + n\zeta')^{2}\text{ of } K \text{ into Product}\\
&\Big\{(l\xi' + m\eta' + n\zeta')(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi, \eta, \zeta}\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})
- (l\xi + m\eta + n\zeta)(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}\Big\}\\
&\quad \pm \sqrt{-(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}}\,(a,..\!\qform{}{l, m, n}\!\qform{}{\eta\zeta' - \eta'\zeta,..}).
\end{aligned}
\right.
\]

\textbf{27.} And
\[
\text{IV. (bis)}\quad
(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi, \eta, \zeta})^{2} = \text{Quotient by }(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2}\text{ of}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
&K(x'\xi + y'\eta + z'\zeta)^{2}\\
&+ \text{Quotient by }(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})^{2} \cdot (a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2} - K(\lambda x' + \mu y' + \nu z')^{2}\text{ of Product}\\
&\Big\{K(\lambda x' + \mu y' + \nu z')(x'\xi + y'\eta + z'\zeta) - (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi, \eta, \zeta}\!\qform{}{l, m, n})(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2}\Big\}\\
&\quad \pm \sqrt{-K(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2}}\,(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'}\!\qform{}{\mu\zeta - \nu\eta,..}).
\end{aligned}
\right.
\]

These four formul\ae\ have the same geometrical significations with the original four formul\ae\ to which they correspond respectively.

\textbf{28.} The eight formul\ae\ become all of them the same or very similar for the quadric form $(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} = x^{2} + y^{2} + z^{2}$, which of course implies $(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi, \eta, \zeta})^{2} = \xi^{2} + \eta^{2} + \zeta^{2}$. Thus selecting any one of them at pleasure, e.g.\ the formula II.\ (bis), this becomes
\begin{align*}
&\{(x^{2} + y^{2} + z^{2})(\xi'^{2} + \eta'^{2} + \zeta'^{2}) - (\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z)^{2}\}\\
&\times \{(l^{2} + m^{2} + n^{2})(\xi'^{2} + \eta'^{2} + \zeta'^{2}) - (l\xi' + m\eta' + n\zeta')^{2}\}\\
&= \{(l\xi' + m\eta' + n\zeta')(x\xi' + m\eta' + n\zeta') - (lx + my + nz)(\xi'^{2} + \eta'^{2} + \zeta'^{2})\}^{2}\\
&\quad + (\xi'^{2} + \eta'^{2} + \zeta'^{2})
\begin{vmatrix}
\xi', & \eta', & \zeta' \\
x, & y, & z \\
l, & m, & n
\end{vmatrix}^{2},
\end{align*}
where the terms independent of $\xi'^{2} + \eta'^{2} + \zeta'^{2}$ destroy each other. Omitting these terms, and dividing by $\xi'^{2} + \eta'^{2} + \zeta'^{2}$, the resulting equation is found to be
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
\xi', & \eta', & \zeta' \\
x, & y, & z \\
l, & m, & n
\end{vmatrix}^{2}
=
\begin{vmatrix}
\xi'^{2} + \eta'^{2} + \zeta'^{2}, & \xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z, & \xi'l + \eta'm + \zeta'n \\
x\xi' + y\eta' + z\zeta', & x^{2} + y^{2} + z^{2}, & xl + ym + zn \\
l\xi' + m\eta' + n\zeta', & lx + my + nz, & l^{2} + m^{2} + n^{2}
\end{vmatrix},
\]
which is a well-known identical equation.

\newpage
\section*{[276] (continued)}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC (continued).}
\end{center}

\medskip
\begin{center}
\textit{Article Nos.\ 29 to 33, relating to a single conic in connexion with an ineunt or a tangent\\of a conic of double contact.}
\end{center}
\medskip

\textbf{29.} The formul\ae\ assume a very simple form when the point of intersection of the two tangents, or the line of junction of the two ineunts of the conic, is an ineunt or a tangent of a conic having double contact with the first-mentioned conic. Thus, if to the conic
\[
(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} = 0
\]
tangents are drawn from a point $(x', y', z')$ of the conic
\[
(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} + (\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z)^{2} = 0,
\]
then we have
\[
(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2} = -(\xi'x' + \eta'y' + \zeta'z')^{2};
\]
and using the form I.\ (bis), and putting therein $(\xi', \eta', \zeta')$ in the place of the arbitrary quantities $(\lambda, \mu, \nu)$, the equation of the tangent divides out by $\xi'x' + \eta'y' + \zeta'z$, and omitting this factor it becomes
\begin{multline*}
(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'}\!\qform{}{x, y, z}) + (\xi'x' + \eta'y' + \zeta'z')(\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z)\\
\pm \frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}\!\qform{}{yz' - y'z,\ zx' - z'x,\ xy' - x'y}) = 0,
\end{multline*}
which is of the form
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
\alpha, & \beta, & \gamma \\
\alpha', & \beta', & \gamma' \\
\alpha'', & \beta'', & \gamma''
\end{vmatrix}
\!\qform{}{x', y', z'}\!\qform{}{x, y, z} = 0,
\]
where the matrix
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
\alpha, & \beta, & \gamma \\
\alpha', & \beta', & \gamma' \\
\alpha'', & \beta'', & \gamma''
\end{vmatrix}
\text{ is } =
\]
\begin{align*}
&a + \xi'^{2} &&,\quad h + \xi'\eta' + \tfrac{1}{\sqrt{K}}(G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta') &&,\quad g + \xi'\zeta' - \tfrac{1}{\sqrt{K}}(H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta')\\
&h + \xi'\eta' - \tfrac{1}{\sqrt{K}}(G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta') &&,\quad b + \eta'^{2} &&,\quad f + \eta'\zeta' + \tfrac{1}{\sqrt{K}}(A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta')\\
&g + \xi'\zeta' + \tfrac{1}{\sqrt{K}}(H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta') &&,\quad f + \eta'\zeta' - \tfrac{1}{\sqrt{K}}(A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta') &&,\quad c + \zeta'^{2}.
\end{align*}

\textbf{30.} But instead of further developing these formul\ae, I prefer to consider the formul\ae\ which give the points of contact of the tangents in question, viz.\ the ineunts of the conic $(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} = 0$, or the tangents through the point $(x', y', z')$ of the conic $(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} + (\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z)^{2} = 0$.

\newpage
\section*{[276] (continued)}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC (continued).}
\end{center}

We have as before
\[
(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'})^{2} = -(\xi'x' + \eta'y' + \zeta'z')^{2},
\]
and using the formula IV.\ (bis) and writing therein $(\xi', \eta', \zeta')$ in the place of the arbitrary quantities $(\lambda, \mu, \nu)$, the equation contains the factor $\xi'x' + \eta'y' + \zeta'z$, and dividing by this factor, and by $K$, the line-equation of the ineunt is
\begin{multline*}
x'\xi + y'\eta + z'\zeta + \frac{1}{K}(x'\xi' + y'\eta' + z'\zeta') \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'}\!\qform{}{\xi, \eta, \zeta})\\
\pm \frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}(a,..\!\qform{}{x', y', z'}\!\qform{}{\eta\zeta' - \eta'\zeta,..}) = 0.
\end{multline*}
Selecting the positive sign, the coordinates of the corresponding ineunt are
\begin{align*}
&x' + \frac{1}{K}(x'\xi' + y'\eta' + z'\zeta')(A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta')
+ \frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\{\eta'(gx' + fy' + cz') - \zeta'(hx' + by' + fz')\},\\
&y' + \frac{1}{K}(x'\xi' + y'\eta' + z'\zeta')(H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta')
+ \frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\{\zeta'(ax' + hy' + gz') - \xi'(gx' + fy' + cz')\},\\
&z' + \frac{1}{K}(x'\xi' + y'\eta' + z'\zeta')(G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta')
+ \frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\{\xi'(hx' + by' + fz') - \eta'(ax' + hy' + gz')\};
\end{align*}
and taking $(X, Y, Z)$ for the coordinates of the ineunt in question, and putting for shortness
\begin{alignat*}{3}
\alpha &= 1 - \tfrac{1}{\sqrt{K}}(g\eta' - h\zeta'), &\quad
\beta &= -\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{K}}(f\eta' - b\zeta'), &\quad
\gamma &= -\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{K}}(c\eta' - f\zeta'),\\[4pt]
\alpha' &= -\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{K}}(a\zeta' - g\xi'), &\quad
\beta' &= 1 - \tfrac{1}{\sqrt{K}}(h\zeta' - f\xi'), &\quad
\gamma' &= -\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{K}}(g\zeta' - c\xi'),\\[4pt]
\alpha'' &= -\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{K}}(h\xi' - a\eta'), &\quad
\beta'' &= -\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{K}}(b\xi' - h\eta'), &\quad
\gamma'' &= 1 - \tfrac{1}{\sqrt{K}}(f\xi' - g\eta'),
\end{alignat*}
we may write
\begin{align*}
(1+P)\,X &= (2-\alpha)\,x' - \beta\,y' - \gamma\,z' + \tfrac{1}{K}(A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta')(\xi'x' + \eta'y' + \zeta'z'),\\
(1+P)\,Y &= -\alpha'\,x' + (2-\beta')\,y' - \gamma'\,z' + \tfrac{1}{K}(H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta')(\xi'x' + \eta'y' + \zeta'z'),\\
(1+P)\,Z &= -\alpha''\,x' - \beta''\,y' + (2-\gamma'')\,z' + \tfrac{1}{K}(G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta')(\xi'x' + \eta'y' + \zeta'z'),
\end{align*}
where $P$, which is arbitrary, may be put
\[
= \frac{1}{K}(A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}.
\]

\newpage
\section*{[276] (continued)}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC (continued).}
\end{center}

\textbf{31.} These equations then give
\[
(x', y', z') =
\begin{vmatrix}
\alpha, & \beta, & \gamma \\
\alpha', & \beta', & \gamma' \\
\alpha'', & \beta'', & \gamma''
\end{vmatrix}
\!\qform{}{X, Y, Z},
\]
which can be verified without difficulty by reversing the process; and we have thus the coordinates $(X, Y, Z)$ in terms of $(x', y', z')$, and reciprocally.

\textbf{32.} If $(X_{1}, Y_{1}, Z_{1})$ are the coordinates of the other ineunt, we have, it is clear,
\[
(x', y', z') =
\begin{vmatrix}
2-\alpha, & -\beta, & -\gamma \\
-\alpha', & 2-\beta', & -\gamma' \\
-\alpha'', & -\beta'', & 2-\gamma''
\end{vmatrix}
\!\qform{}{X_{1}, Y_{1}, Z_{1}};
\]
or substituting for $(x', y', z')$ their values in terms of $(X, Y, Z)$,
\[
(2X_{1}, 2Y_{1}, 2Z_{1}) =
\begin{vmatrix}
\alpha, & \beta, & \gamma \\
\alpha', & \beta', & \gamma' \\
\alpha'', & \beta'', & \gamma''
\end{vmatrix}
\!\qform{}{X+X_{1}, Y+Y_{1}, Z+Z_{1}},
\]
so that $(X+X_{1}, Y+Y_{1}, Z+Z_{1})$ are the same linear functions of $2X_{1}, 2Y_{1}, 2Z_{1}$ that $(X, Y, Z)$ are of $(x', y', z')$; that is, we have
\begin{align*}
\tfrac{1}{2}(1+P)(X+X_{1}) &= (2-\alpha)\,X_{1} - \beta\,Y_{1} - \gamma\,Z_{1}
+ \tfrac{1}{K}(A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta')(\xi'x' + \eta'y' + \zeta'z'),\\
\tfrac{1}{2}(1+P)(Y+Y_{1}) &= -\alpha'\,X_{1} + (2-\beta')\,Y_{1} - \gamma'\,Z_{1}
+ \tfrac{1}{K}(H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta')(\xi'x' + \eta'y' + \zeta'z'),\\
\tfrac{1}{2}(1+P)(Z+Z_{1}) &= -\alpha''\,X_{1} - \beta''\,Y_{1} + (2-\gamma'')\,Z_{1}
+ \tfrac{1}{K}(G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta')(\xi'x' + \eta'y' + \zeta'z'),
\end{align*}
which equations may be written
\[
(1+P)(X, Y, Z) =
\begin{vmatrix}
\mathrm{a}, & \mathrm{b}, & \mathrm{c} \\
\mathrm{a}', & \mathrm{b}', & \mathrm{c}' \\
\mathrm{a}'', & \mathrm{b}'', & \mathrm{c}''
\end{vmatrix}
\!\qform{}{X_{1}, Y_{1}, Z_{1}},
\]
where the values of the coefficients are
\begin{align*}
\mathrm{a} &= (g\eta' - h\zeta') + \tfrac{1}{K}(A\xi'^{2} - B\eta'^{2} - 2F\eta'\zeta' - C\zeta'^{2}),\\
\mathrm{b} &= -\tfrac{2}{\sqrt{K}}(f\eta' - b\zeta') + \tfrac{2}{K}\eta'(A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta'),\\
\mathrm{c} &= -\tfrac{2}{\sqrt{K}}(c\eta' - f\zeta') + \tfrac{2}{K}\zeta'(A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta'),\\
\mathrm{a}' &= -\tfrac{2}{\sqrt{K}}(a\zeta' - g\xi') + \tfrac{2}{K}\xi'(H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta'),\\
\mathrm{b}' &= 1 - \tfrac{2}{\sqrt{K}}(h\zeta' - f\xi') + \tfrac{1}{K}(B\eta'^{2} - C\zeta'^{2} - 2G\zeta'\xi' - A\xi'^{2}),\\
\mathrm{c}' &= -\tfrac{2}{\sqrt{K}}(g\zeta' - c\xi') + \tfrac{2}{K}\zeta'(H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta'),\\
\mathrm{a}'' &= -\tfrac{2}{\sqrt{K}}(h\xi' - a\eta') + \tfrac{2}{K}\xi'(G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta'),\\
\mathrm{b}'' &= -\tfrac{2}{\sqrt{K}}(b\xi' - h\eta') + \tfrac{2}{K}\eta'(G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta'),\\
\mathrm{c}'' &= 1 - \tfrac{2}{\sqrt{K}}(f\xi' - g\eta') + \tfrac{1}{K}(C\zeta'^{2} - A\xi'^{2} - 2H\xi'\eta' - B\eta'^{2}).
\end{align*}

\newpage
\section*{[276] (continued)}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC (continued).}
\end{center}

and considering $(X, Y, Z)$ and $(X_{1}, Y_{1}, Z_{1})$ as quantities connected by the foregoing linear relations, we have identically
\[
(a,..\!\qform{}{X, Y, Z})^{2} = (a,..\!\qform{}{X_{1}, Y_{1}, Z_{1}})^{2}.
\]
The investigation leads thus to the automorphic transformation of the quadric function, a transformation first effected by M.~Hermite\footnote{See my ``Memoir on the Automorphic Transformation of a Bipartite Quadric Function,'' \textit{Phil.\ Trans.}\ vol.~cxlviii.\ (1858), pp.~39---46, [153].}.

\textbf{33.} It is to be remarked that the foregoing formul\ae\ show that $(x', y', z')$ being the coordinates of a point on the conic $(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} + (\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z)^{2} = 0$, from which point tangents are drawn to the conic $(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} = 0$, then the coordinates $(x', y', z')$ enter \textit{linearly} into the equations of the tangents, the ineunts (or points of contact), and the polar. And it may be added that the equation of the conic enveloped by the polar (that is, the polar conic of $(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} + (\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z)^{2} = 0$) has for its equation
\[
\{K + (A,..\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2}\}\,(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} - K(\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z)^{2} = 0,
\]
and that the coordinates of the point of contact of the polar with this conic are
\begin{align*}
&x' + \frac{1}{K}(A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta')(\xi'x' + \eta'y' + \zeta'z'),\\
&y' + \frac{1}{K}(H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta')(\xi'x' + \eta'y' + \zeta'z'),\\
&z' + \frac{1}{K}(G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta')(\xi'x' + \eta'y' + \zeta'z');
\end{align*}
so that $(x', y', z')$ also enter linearly into the expressions for the coordinates of the last-mentioned point.

\medskip
\begin{center}
\textit{Article Nos.\ 34 to 37, relating to two conics.}
\end{center}
\medskip

\textbf{34.} Considering now the two conics
\begin{align*}
U &= (a, b, c, f, g, h \!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} = 0,\\
U' &= (a', b', c', f', g', h' \!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} = 0;
\end{align*}
suppose that the conic
\[
\theta U + \theta' U' = (\theta a + \theta' a',.. \!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} = 0
\]
represents a pair of lines.

The condition for this is
\[
\text{Disct.}\ (\theta a + \theta' a',.. \!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} = 0,
\]
which is
\[
(\mfA, \mfB, \mfC, \mfD \!\qform{}{\theta, \theta'})^{3} = 0,
\]
where
\begin{align*}
\mfA &= K,\\
\mfB &= Aa' + Bb' + Cc' + 2Ff' + 2Gg' + 2Hh',\\
\mfC &= A'a + B'b + C'c + 2F'f + 2G'g + 2H'h,\\
\mfD &= K'.
\end{align*}

\newpage
\section*{[276] (continued)}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC (continued).}
\end{center}

(the significations of $K'$, $A'$, $B'$, $C'$, $F'$, $G'$, $H'$ being of course analogous to those of $K$, $A$, $B$, $C$, $F$, $G$, $H$). The three roots $\theta : \theta'$ correspond, it is clear, to the three pairs of lines which can be drawn through the intersections of the two conics.

\textbf{35.} The equation
\[
\text{Disct.}\ (\mfA, \mfB, \mfC, \mfD \!\qform{}{\theta, \theta'})^{3} = 0,
\]
which is of the fourth order in $\mfA$, $\mfB$, $\mfC$, $\mfD$, and of the sixth order as regards $(a, b, c, f, g, h)$ and $(a', b', c', f', g', h')$ respectively, is the condition in order that the two conics may touch each other. Assuming that it is satisfied, the cubic equation in $\theta : \theta'$ has a pair of equal roots; or say there is a twofold root and a onefold root; the twofold root gives the pair of lines drawn from the point of contact to the other two points of intersection, the onefold root gives the pair made up of the common tangent and the line joining the other two points of intersection.

\textbf{36.} In particular, suppose that the two conics are
\begin{align*}
2(\rho x + \sigma y + \tau z)(\rho' x + \sigma' y + \tau' z) &= 0,\\
2(\lambda x + \mu y + \nu z)(\lambda' x + \mu' y + \nu' z) &= 0;
\end{align*}
so that
\begin{align*}
(a, b, c, f, g, h) &= (2\rho\rho', 2\sigma\sigma', 2\tau\tau', \sigma\tau' + \sigma'\tau, \tau\rho' + \tau'\rho, \rho\sigma' + \rho'\sigma),\\
(a', b', c', f', g', h') &= (2\lambda\lambda', 2\mu\mu', 2\nu\nu', \mu\nu' + \mu'\nu, \nu\lambda' + \nu'\lambda, \lambda\mu' + \lambda'\mu),\\
(A, B, C, F, G, H) &= -(\sigma\tau' - \sigma'\tau, \tau\rho' - \tau'\rho, \rho\sigma' - \rho'\sigma)^{2},\\
(A', B', C', F', G', H') &= -(\mu\nu' - \mu'\nu, \nu\lambda' - \nu'\lambda, \lambda\mu' - \lambda'\mu)^{2};
\end{align*}
and thence also
\begin{align*}
\mfA &= K &= 0,\\
\mfB &= Aa' + \&\mathrm{c}. &= -2
\begin{vmatrix}
\lambda, & \mu, & \nu \\
\rho, & \sigma, & \tau \\
\rho', & \sigma', & \tau'
\end{vmatrix}
\begin{vmatrix}
\lambda', & \mu', & \nu' \\
\rho, & \sigma, & \tau \\
\rho', & \sigma', & \tau'
\end{vmatrix},\\
\mfC &= A'a + \&\mathrm{c}. &= -2
\begin{vmatrix}
\rho, & \sigma, & \tau \\
\lambda, & \mu, & \nu \\
\lambda', & \mu', & \nu'
\end{vmatrix}
\begin{vmatrix}
\rho', & \sigma', & \tau' \\
\lambda, & \mu, & \nu \\
\lambda', & \mu', & \nu'
\end{vmatrix},\\
\mfD &= K' &= 0;
\end{align*}
and the equation in $(\theta, \theta')$ is
\[
\mfB\theta + \mfC\theta' = 0;
\]
hence writing $\theta = \mfC$, $\theta' = -\mfB$, the equation of the pair of lines is
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
\rho, & \sigma, & \tau \\
\lambda, & \mu, & \nu \\
\lambda', & \mu', & \nu'
\end{vmatrix}
\begin{vmatrix}
\rho', & \sigma', & \tau' \\
\lambda, & \mu, & \nu \\
\lambda', & \mu', & \nu'
\end{vmatrix}
(\rho x + \sigma y + \tau z)(\rho' x + \sigma' y + \tau' z)
-
\begin{vmatrix}
\lambda, & \mu, & \nu \\
\rho, & \sigma, & \tau \\
\rho', & \sigma', & \tau'
\end{vmatrix}
\begin{vmatrix}
\lambda', & \mu', & \nu' \\
\rho, & \sigma, & \tau \\
\rho', & \sigma', & \tau'
\end{vmatrix}
(\lambda x + \mu y + \nu z)(\lambda' x + \mu' y + \nu' z) = 0;
\]

\newpage
\section*{[276] (continued)}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE ANALYTICAL THEORY OF THE CONIC (continued).}
\end{center}

and it is easy to see that the left-hand side does in fact break up into factors, and that the equation is
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
x, & y, & z \\
\mu\tau' - \nu\sigma', & \nu\rho' - \lambda\tau', & \lambda\sigma' - \mu\rho' \\
\sigma\nu' - \tau\mu', & \tau\lambda' - \rho\nu', & \rho\mu' - \sigma\lambda'
\end{vmatrix}
\begin{vmatrix}
x, & y, & z \\
\mu\tau - \nu\sigma, & \nu\rho - \lambda\tau, & \lambda\sigma - \mu\rho \\
\sigma'\nu' - \tau'\mu', & \tau'\lambda' - \rho'\nu', & \rho'\mu' - \sigma'\lambda'
\end{vmatrix}
= 0,
\]
which of course might have been obtained at once by means of the four points which are the intersection of each component line of the first conic by each component line of the second conic.

\textbf{37.} Suppose that the first conic is
\[
(a, b, c, f, g, h \!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} = 0,
\]
while the second conic is the pair of lines
\[
2(\lambda x + \mu y + \nu z)(\lambda' x + \mu' y + \nu' z) = 0;
\]
then, putting as before,
\[
(\theta a + \theta' \cdot 2\lambda\lambda',.. \!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} = 0,
\]
we have
\[
(\mfA, \mfB, \mfC, \mfD \!\qform{}{\theta, \theta'})^{3} = 0,
\]
where
\begin{align*}
\mfA &= K,\\
\mfB &= 2\,(A, B, C, F, G, H \!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{\lambda', \mu', \nu'}),\\
\mfC &= -\,(a, b, c, f, g, h \!\qform{}{\mu\nu' - \mu'\nu,\ \nu\lambda' - \nu'\lambda,\ \lambda\mu' - \lambda'\mu})^{2},\\
\mfD &= 0;
\end{align*}
and the equation in $(\theta, \theta')$ is
\[
K\theta^{2} + 2\,(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{\lambda', \mu', \nu'})\,\theta\theta'
- (a,..\!\qform{}{\mu\nu' - \mu'\nu,..})^{2}\,\theta'^{2} = 0,
\]
which may be written
\begin{align*}
\{K\theta + (A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{\lambda', \mu', \nu'})\,\theta'\}^{2}
&= \{[(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{\lambda', \mu', \nu'})]^{2} + K(a,..\!\qform{}{\mu\nu' - \mu'\nu,..})^{2}\}\,\theta'^{2},\\
&= (A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})^{2} \cdot (A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda', \mu', \nu'})^{2} \cdot \theta'^{2},
\end{align*}
that is
\[
K\theta = [\pm\sqrt{(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})^{2}}\sqrt{(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda', \mu', \nu'})^{2}}
- (A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{\lambda', \mu', \nu'})]\,\theta';
\]
we may assume
\[
\theta = \pm\sqrt{(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})^{2}}\sqrt{(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda', \mu', \nu'})^{2}}
- (A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{\lambda', \mu', \nu'}),\quad \theta' = K,
\]
so that the conic
\begin{multline*}
\{\pm\sqrt{(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})^{2}}\sqrt{(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda', \mu', \nu'})^{2}}
- (A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{\lambda', \mu', \nu'})\}\,(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2}\\
+ 2K\,(\lambda x + \mu y + \nu z)(\lambda' x + \mu' y + \nu' z) = 0
\end{multline*}
breaks up into a pair of lines.

Putting for shortness
\[
\pm\sqrt{(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu})^{2}}\sqrt{(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda', \mu', \nu'})^{2}}
- (A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{\lambda', \mu', \nu'}) = \Omega
\]

% Continue article 37 - the conic with Ω notation
the coefficients on the left-hand side of the equation are
\[
\{\Omega a + 2K\lambda\lambda',..\ \Omega f + K(\mu\nu' + \mu'\nu),..\},
\]
whence, after all reductions, the inverse function is
\[
\{(A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}\!\qform{}{\lambda', \mu', \nu'})^{2}
+ (A,..\!\qform{}{\lambda', \mu', \nu'})^{2}\!\qform{}{\lambda, \mu, \nu}
\sqrt{\text{Disct.}}\},
\]
and the remainder of the process of decomposition is effected without difficulty.

\medskip
\begin{center}
\textit{Addition, 18 December, 1862.}
\end{center}

The formul\ae\ II.\ and II.\ (bis) each of them give the tangents of the conic
$(a,..\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} = 0$ at the ineunts of intersection with the line
$\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z = 0$. A very elegant formula for these ineunts themselves
was communicated to me by Mr~Spottiswoode, and I have since found that the same or
an equivalent formula is made use of by M.~Aronhold in his recent valuable memoir,
``Ueber eine neue algebraische Behandlungsweise der Integrale irrationaler
Differentiale, \&c.,'' \textit{Crelle}, t.~LXII. pp.~95---145 (1862). The formula is as
follows, viz.\ for the conic and line,
\begin{align*}
(a, b, c, f, g, h\!\qform{}{x, y, z})^{2} &= 0,\\
\xi'x + \eta'y + \zeta'z &= 0,
\end{align*}
then
\begin{multline*}
x : y : z =\\
(\xi'l + \eta'm + \eta'n)\,2f\xi' + \nu'(gl + fm + cn) - \zeta'(hl + bm + fy) + l\sqrt{\Delta} :\\
(\xi'l + \eta'm + \zeta'n)\,2g\eta' + \xi'(hl + bm + fn) - \nu'(al + hm + gn) + m\sqrt{\Delta} :\\
(\xi'l + \eta'm + \zeta'n)\,2h\zeta' + \eta'(al + hm + gn) - \xi'(gl + fm + cn) + n\sqrt{\Delta};
\end{multline*}
where
\[
\Delta =
\begin{vmatrix}
\xi', & \eta', & \zeta'\\
f, & g, & h\\
\xi', & \eta', & \zeta'
\end{vmatrix}^{2}
= -(A, B, C, F, G, H\!\qform{}{\xi', \eta', \zeta'})^{2},
\]
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
\xi', & \eta', & \zeta'\\
f, & a, & h, & g\\
\eta', & h, & b, & f\\
\zeta', & g, & f, & c
\end{vmatrix}
\]
so that $\xi'$, $\eta'$, $\zeta'$ are respectively
\[
= -(A\xi' + H\eta' + G\zeta'),\ -(H\xi' + B\eta' + F\zeta'),\ -(G\xi' + F\eta' + C\zeta'),
\]
and where $l$, $m$, $n$ are supernumerary arbitrary quantities.


\newpage
\section*{[277]}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{277. On the Wave Surface}
\begin{center}
{\large ON THE WAVE SURFACE.}
\end{center}
\markboth{ON THE WAVE SURFACE.}{ON THE WAVE SURFACE.}

[From the \textit{Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics}, vol.~III. (1860),
pp.~16---22.]

Some very beautiful results in relation to the Wave Surface have been recently
obtained by Herr~Zech, in the Memoirs ``Die Eigenschaften der Wellenfl\"achen der
zweiaxigen Krystalle mittels der h\"ohern Geometrie abgeleitet,'' \textit{Crelle}, t.~LII.
pp.~243---254 (1856), and ``Die Kr\"ummungslinien der Wellenfl\"ache zweiaxiger
Krystalle,'' \textit{Crelle}, t.~LIV. pp.~72---77 (1857). According to the former of Fresnel's
two modes of generation, the wave surface is the envelope of a plane whose
perpendicular distance $v$ is a certain given function of the direction cosines
$l$, $m$, $n$. For the same system of direction cosines, there are in fact two
values of the perpendicular distance: call these $v$ and $w$, and let the
corresponding planes (parallel of course to each other) be called P and~Q.
Then the entire system of the planes P and~Q envelope the wave surface, viz.\ the
planes P may be considered as enveloping one sheet and the planes Q the other
sheet of the surface. But if instead of considering the entire system of planes we
consider only the planes P and the parallel planes Q, for which the perpendicular on
the plane P has a given constant value $v$, then the planes P will envelope a
developable F, and the planes Q will envelope a developable G, these two
developables F and~G respectively touching the wave surface along two series of
curves. And it is shown in the second of the Memoirs above referred to that these
curves are the curves of curvature of the wave surface.

The developable F is obtained as the envelope of the plane P, whose equation is
\[
\xi x + \eta y + \zeta z = v,
\]
where $v$ has a given constant value and $\xi$, $\eta$, $\zeta$ are parameters which vary,
subject to the two conditions
\[
\xi^{2} + \eta^{2} + \zeta^{2} = 1,
\]
\[
\frac{\xi^{2}}{a^{2} - v^{2}} + \frac{\eta^{2}}{b^{2} - v^{2}} + \frac{\zeta^{2}}{c^{2} - v^{2}} = 0.
\]

And in like manner the developable G is obtained as the envelope of the plane Q,
whose equation is
\[
\xi x + \eta y + \zeta z = w
= \frac{abc}{v}\sqrt{\frac{\xi^{2}}{a^{2}} + \frac{\eta^{2}}{b^{2}} + \frac{\zeta^{2}}{c^{2}}},
\]
where $w$ has a given constant value and $\xi$, $\eta$, $\zeta$ are parameters which vary,
subject to the two conditions
\[
\xi^{2} + \eta^{2} + \zeta^{2} = 1,
\]
\[
\frac{\xi^{2}}{a^{2} - w^{2}} + \frac{\eta^{2}}{b^{2} - w^{2}} + \frac{\zeta^{2}}{c^{2} - w^{2}} = 0.
\]

(It is hardly necessary to remark that if in the last-mentioned system of
equations, the parameter $w$ is also treated as variable, we obtain the wave surface:
in fact $w$, $v^{2}$ being the roots of the equation
\[
\frac{\xi^{2}}{a^{2} - \theta} + \frac{\eta^{2}}{b^{2} - \theta} + \frac{\zeta^{2}}{c^{2} - \theta} = 0,
\]
we have, attending to the condition $\xi^{2} + \eta^{2} + \zeta^{2} = 1$, the identical equation
\[
v^{2}w^{2} - (v^{2} + w^{2})\theta + \&\mathrm{c}. =
(\theta - a^{2})(\theta - b^{2})(\theta - c^{2})
\left(\frac{\xi^{2}}{a^{2} - \theta} + \frac{\eta^{2}}{b^{2} - \theta} + \frac{\zeta^{2}}{c^{2} - \theta}\right),
\]
and thence
\[
vw = abc\sqrt{\frac{\xi^{2}}{a^{2}} + \frac{\eta^{2}}{b^{2}} + \frac{\zeta^{2}}{c^{2}}},
\]
which shows that $vw = abc\sqrt{\frac{\xi^{2}}{a^{2}} + \frac{\eta^{2}}{b^{2}} + \frac{\zeta^{2}}{c^{2}}}$.)

or in other words, that the planes P and Q are respectively tangents to the conic or
infinitely thin surface of the second order, which is the second of the last-mentioned
cone by the plane at infinity. Moreover it is obvious from the same equations that
the plane P is a tangent plane of the sphere whose equation is
\[
x^{2} + y^{2} + z^{2} = v^{2},
\]
and that the plane Q is a tangent plane of the ellipsoid whose equation is
\[
\frac{a^{2}b^{2}c^{2}}{a^{2}x^{2} + b^{2}y^{2} + c^{2}z^{2}} = w^{2}.
\]

Hence each of the developables F and~G is the envelope of a plane which is the
common tangent plane of two surfaces of the second order; such developables are in
general of the eighth order, see my paper ``On the Developable Surfaces which arise
from Two Surfaces of the Second Order,'' \textit{Camb.\ and Dubl.\ Math.
Journ.}, t.~V. pp.~46---57 (1850), [84], and it will be presently seen that this is in
fact the order of the developables F and~G respectively.

The before-mentioned cone
\[
\frac{\xi^{2}}{a^{2} - v^{2}} + \frac{\eta^{2}}{b^{2} - v^{2}} + \frac{\zeta^{2}}{c^{2} - v^{2}} = 0
\]
(Zech's cone K) is a cone having for its focal lines the optic axes (or normals to
the circular sections) of the ellipsoid $a^{2}x^{2} + b^{2}y^{2} + c^{2}z^{2} = 1$ (Zech's ellipsoid~E)
which is used in the theory of the Wave Surface in the place of Fresnel's Surface
of Elasticity. The complementary cone
\[
(a^{2} - v^{2})x^{2} + (b^{2} - v^{2})y^{2} + (c^{2} - v^{2})z^{2} = 0
\]
is the cone which standing on the same ellipsoid~E has for its focal lines the optic
axes of the reciprocal ellipsoid $\frac{x^{2}}{a^{2}} + \frac{y^{2}}{b^{2}} + \frac{z^{2}}{c^{2}} = 1$.

To find the equation of the developable F, writing for shortness
\[
p = v^{2} - a^{2},\quad q = v^{2} - b^{2},\quad r = v^{2} - c^{2},
\]
then the system of equations is
\begin{align*}
\xi x + \eta y + \zeta z - v &= 0,\\
\xi^{2} + \eta^{2} + \zeta^{2} - 1 &= 0,\\
\frac{\xi^{2}}{p} + \frac{\eta^{2}}{q} + \frac{\zeta^{2}}{r} &= 0,
\end{align*}
treating them by the method of arbitrary multipliers in the usual manner, we obtain
\begin{align*}
x + 2\lambda\xi + \frac{2\mu\xi}{p} &= 0,\\
y + 2\lambda\eta + \frac{2\mu\eta}{q} &= 0,\\
z + 2\lambda\zeta + \frac{2\mu\zeta}{r} &= 0,
\end{align*}
equations which give in the first instance $v + p = 0$ or $p = -v$, and then substituting
this value,
\[
\xi = \frac{x(v^{2} - a^{2})}{2v\theta},\quad
\eta = \frac{y(v^{2} - b^{2})}{2v\theta},\quad
\zeta = \frac{z(v^{2} - c^{2})}{2v\theta},
\]
and thence
\begin{align*}
\frac{x^{2}(v^{2} - a^{2})}{4v^{2}\theta^{2}} + \frac{y^{2}(v^{2} - b^{2})}{4v^{2}\theta^{2}}
+ \frac{z^{2}(v^{2} - c^{2})}{4v^{2}\theta^{2}} &= 0,\\
\frac{x^{2}(v^{2} - a^{2})^{2}}{4v^{2}\theta^{2}} + \frac{y^{2}(v^{2} - b^{2})^{2}}{4v^{2}\theta^{2}}
+ \frac{z^{2}(v^{2} - c^{2})^{2}}{4v^{2}\theta^{2}} &= \theta,
\end{align*}
the latter of which is the derived equation of the former with respect to the parameter
$\theta$; hence writing the former equation under the form
\[
\{\theta + v(v^{2} - a^{2})\}\,\{\theta + v(v^{2} - b^{2})\}\,\{\theta + v(v^{2} - c^{2})\}
- \&\mathrm{c}. = 0,
\]
or what is the same thing
\[
(A, B, C, D\!\qform{}{\theta, 1})^{3} = 0,
\]
where
\begin{align*}
A &= 3v^{2},\\
B &= (v^{2} - a^{2})(v^{2} - b^{2}) + (v^{2} - b^{2})(v^{2} - c^{2}) + (v^{2} - c^{2})(v^{2} - a^{2}),\\
C &= (v^{2} - y^{2} - z^{2})(v^{2} - b^{2})(v^{2} - c^{2}) + (v^{2} - z^{2} - x^{2})(v^{2} - c^{2})(v^{2} - a^{2})\\
&\quad + (v^{2} - x^{2} - y^{2})(v^{2} - a^{2})(v^{2} - b^{2}),\\
D &= (v^{2} - a^{2})(v^{2} - b^{2})(v^{2} - c^{2}) - x^{2}(v^{2} - b^{2})(v^{2} - c^{2})
- y^{2}(v^{2} - c^{2})(v^{2} - a^{2})\\
&\quad - z^{2}(v^{2} - a^{2})(v^{2} - b^{2}),
\end{align*}
the equation of the developable F is
\[
(AD - BC)^{2} - 4(AC - B^{2})(BD - C^{2}) = 0.
\]

The investigation for the developable G is very similar to the preceding. Write for
shortness
\[
p' = \frac{1}{w^{2}} - \frac{1}{a^{2}},\quad
q' = \frac{1}{w^{2}} - \frac{1}{b^{2}},\quad
r' = \frac{1}{w^{2}} - \frac{1}{c^{2}},
\]
then the system of equations is
\begin{align*}
\xi x + \eta y + \zeta z &= w,\\
\xi^{2} + \eta^{2} + \zeta^{2} &= 1,\\
\frac{\xi^{2}}{a^{2} - w^{2}} + \frac{\eta^{2}}{b^{2} - w^{2}} + \frac{\zeta^{2}}{c^{2} - w^{2}} &= 0,
\end{align*}
and we then have
\[
\xi = \frac{x/a^{2}}{\lambda + \mu/w^{2} + \mu(v^{2} - a^{2})},\ \&\mathrm{c}.,
\]
equations which give $p = 0$, and substituting this value, we obtain
\[
\xi = \frac{x(w^{2} - a^{2})}{wA},\quad
\eta = \frac{y(w^{2} - b^{2})}{wA},\quad
\zeta = \frac{z(w^{2} - c^{2})}{wA},
\]
where $A$ is a parameter. Substituting these values in the equations
\[
\xi^{2} + \eta^{2} + \zeta^{2} = 1,
\]
\[
\frac{x^{2}(w^{2} - a^{2})}{w^{2}A^{2}} + \frac{y^{2}(w^{2} - b^{2})}{w^{2}A^{2}}
+ \frac{z^{2}(w^{2} - c^{2})}{w^{2}A^{2}} = 0,
\]
we find
\begin{align*}
\frac{x^{2}(w^{2} - a^{2})^{2}}{w^{2}A^{2}} + \frac{y^{2}(w^{2} - b^{2})^{2}}{w^{2}A^{2}}
+ \frac{z^{2}(w^{2} - c^{2})^{2}}{w^{2}A^{2}} &= A,\\
\frac{x^{2}(w^{2} - a^{2})}{w^{2}} + \frac{y^{2}(w^{2} - b^{2})}{w^{2}}
+ \frac{z^{2}(w^{2} - c^{2})}{w^{2}} &= A^{2} - (w^{2} - a^{2})(w^{2} - b^{2})(w^{2} - c^{2}),
\end{align*}
and multiplying the first equation by $-\frac{1}{w^{2}}$ and the second equation by $A^{2}$ and adding,
we obtain
\[
\frac{x^{2}(w^{2} - a^{2})}{a^{2}} + \frac{y^{2}(w^{2} - b^{2})}{b^{2}}
+ \frac{z^{2}(w^{2} - c^{2})}{c^{2}} = \frac{abc}{w},
\]
of which equation the latter of the foregoing two equations is the derived equation
with respect to the parameter~$A^{2}$.

Comparing this with the foregoing equation
\[
(AD - BC)^{2} - 4(AC - B^{2})(BD - C^{2}) = 0,
\]
we see that it is deduced from it by writing
\[
a^{2}x^{2},\ b^{2}y^{2},\ c^{2}z^{2},\ -\frac{1}{a^{2}},\ -\frac{1}{b^{2}},\ -\frac{1}{c^{2}},\ w^{2},\ A^{2}
\]
in the place of
\[
x^{2},\ y^{2},\ z^{2},\ v^{2} - a^{2},\ v^{2} - b^{2},\ v^{2} - c^{2},\ v^{2},\ \theta
\]
respectively, and the equation of the developable G is therefore
\[
(A'D' - B'C')^{2} - 4(A'C' - B'^{2})(B'D' - C'^{2}) = 0,
\]
where $A'$, $B'$, $C'$, $D'$ are what $A$, $B$, $C$, $D$ become when
\[
x^{2},\ y^{2},\ z^{2},\ v^{2} - a^{2},\ v^{2} - b^{2},\ v^{2} - c^{2},\ v^{2},\ \theta
\]
are replaced by
\[
a^{2}x^{2},\ b^{2}y^{2},\ c^{2}z^{2},\ -\frac{1}{a^{2}},\ -\frac{1}{b^{2}},\ -\frac{1}{c^{2}},\ w^{2},\ A^{2}.
\]

The equations of the developables F and~G, although radically distinct from each
other, are consequently similar in form, and each is at once deducible from the other.

\begin{flushright}
2, Stone Buildings, W.C., 9th March, 1858.
\end{flushright}


\newpage
\section*{[278]}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{278. Note on the Singular Solutions of Differential Equations}
\begin{center}
{\large NOTE ON THE SINGULAR SOLUTIONS OF DIFFERENTIAL EQUATIONS.}
\end{center}
\markboth{NOTE ON THE SINGULAR SOLUTIONS OF DIFFERENTIAL EQUATIONS.}{NOTE ON THE SINGULAR SOLUTIONS OF DIFFERENTIAL EQUATIONS.}

[From the \textit{Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics}, vol.~III. (1860),
pp.~36, 37.]

The following investigation (which has been in my possession for a good many
years) affords I think a simple explanation of the theory of the singular solutions of
differential equations.

Let the primitive equation be
\[
c^{n} + Pc^{n-1} + Qc^{n-2} + \ldots = 0,
\]
where $c$ is the arbitrary constant and $P$, $Q$, \ldots\ are any functions of $x$, $y$; then the
differential equation is obtained by eliminating $c$ from the foregoing equation and the
derived equation
\[
n'c^{n-1} + Q'c^{n-2} + \ldots = 0,
\]
and the result may be represented by
\[
F(P, Q, \ldots,\ P', Q', \ldots) = 0.
\]

Assume now
\[
c^{n} + Pc^{n-1} + Qc^{n-2} + \ldots = (c + X)(c + Y)(c + Z)\ldots,
\]
then we have
\begin{align*}
P &= X + Y + Z + \&\mathrm{c}.,\\
Q &= XY + XZ + YZ + \&\mathrm{c}.,\\
&\ \;\vdots
\end{align*}
and consequently
\begin{align*}
P' &= X' + Y' + Z' + \&\mathrm{c}.,\\
Q' &= (Y + Z + \&\mathrm{c}.)X' + \&\mathrm{c}.,\\
&\ \;\vdots
\end{align*}

and substituting these values in the function $F(P, Q, \ldots,\ P', Q', \ldots)$ it is clear that we
shall have $F(P, Q, \ldots,\ P', Q', \ldots) = UX'Y'Z'\ldots$ where $U$ is a symmetrical function of
$X$, $Y$, $Z$, \&c., and therefore a function of $P$, $Q$, \ldots; and this equation will be identically
true whatever values we attribute to $X'$, $Y'$, $Z'$, \ldots, hence putting these quantities
respectively equal to unity, we have
\begin{align*}
P' &= n,\\
Q' &= (n - 1)P,\\
R' &= (n - 2)Q,\\
&\ \;\vdots
\end{align*}
and with these values
\[
U = F(P, Q, \ldots,\ n,\ (n - 1)P,\ \ldots),
\]
that is $U = 0$ is the result obtained by eliminating $c$ from the primitive equation and
the equation
\[
nc^{n-1} + (n - 1)Pc^{n-2} + \ldots = 0,
\]
which is the equation obtained by differentiating the primitive equation with respect to
the arbitrary constant $c$: that is, $U = 0$ being the singular solution, the differential
equation is
\[
UX'Y'Z'\ldots = 0.
\]

It is to be remarked that ($P$, $Q$, \&c.\ being rational and integral functions) then
if the roots $X$, $Y$, $Z$, \&c.\ are also rational and integral functions, the differential
equation contains $U^{n}$ as a separable rational and integral factor, but if the roots are
irrational then the differential equation does not really contain the rational and integral
factor $U$, but $X'Y'Z'\ldots$ is here a rational fraction containing $U$ in the denominator
and $UX'Y'Z'\ldots$ is an indecomposable rational and integral function. This is easily
verified \`a~\textit{posteriori} for a quadratic equation.

\begin{flushright}
2, Stone Buildings, W.C., 28th January, 1858.
\end{flushright}


\newpage
\section*{[279]}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{279. On a Theorem Relating to Spherical Conics}
\begin{center}
{\large ON A THEOREM RELATING TO SPHERICAL CONICS.}
\end{center}
\markboth{ON A THEOREM RELATING TO SPHERICAL CONICS.}{ON A THEOREM RELATING TO SPHERICAL CONICS.}

[From the \textit{Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics}, vol.~III. (1860),
p.~53.]

The following theorem was given by Prof.~MacCullagh: ``If three lines at right
angles to each other pass through a fixed point~$O$ so that two of them are confined
to given planes: the third line traces out a cone of the second order whose sections
parallel to the given planes are circles, and the plane containing the other two lines
envelopes a cone of the second order whose sections by planes parallel to the given
planes are parabolas.''

Referring the figure to the sphere we have a trirectangular triangle $XYZ$, of which
two angles $X$, $Y$ lie on fixed arcs $A$, $B$. The angle~$Z$ generates a spherical conic~$U$
having $A$, $B$ for its cyclic arcs. The side~$XY$ envelopes a spherical conic~$V$ touched
by the arcs $A$, $B$. The conic~$V$ is evidently the supplementary conic of~$U$, hence
the poles of $A$, $B$ are the foci of~$U$. We may drop altogether the consideration of
the triangle $XYZ$ and consider only the side~$XY$, we have then the theorem:
\textit{If a quadrantal arc $XY$ slides between the two fixed arcs $A$, $B$, the envelope of
$XY$ is a spherical conic~$U$ touched by the fixed arcs $A$, $B$, and which has for its
foci the poles of these same arcs $A$, $B$.}

It is worth while to notice the great reduction of order which takes place in
consequence of the arc~$XY$ being a quadrant. If $XY$ had been an arc of a given
magnitude~$\theta$, the envelope would have been a spherical curve of an order certainly
higher than~6. For considering the corresponding problem in piano, the envelope in
the particular case where the fixed lines $A$, $B$ are at right angles to each other
is a curve of the sixth order, and in the general case where the two fixed lines are
not at right angles the order is higher: the problem in piano corresponds of course,
not to the general problem on the sphere, but to that in which $\theta$ is indefinitely small.
\clearpage

% ============================================================
\section*{280. On the Conics which Touch Four Given Lines}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{280. On the Conics which Touch Four Given Lines}
\label{sec:280}

[From the \textit{Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics}, vol.~III. (1860), pp.~94--96.]

There are considerable practical difficulties in drawing a figure of the system of conics which touch four given lines, but a notion of the figure of the system may be obtained as follows:

Figure 20 may be taken to represent any quadrilateral whatever, having all its sides real; and if we attend only to the unshaded spaces, it will be seen that there are five regions which are called the inner, upper, lower, right-hand, and left-hand regions respectively. The inner diagonals are $AG$ the vertical diagonal and $BD$ the horizontal diagonal; the former of these traverses the inner, upper, and lower regions; the latter the inner, right-hand, and left-hand regions; the outer diagonal is $EF$ which traverses the upper region and the right-hand and left-hand regions. The inner or vertical and horizontal diagonals meet in the inner region, the vertical and outer diagonals meet in the upper region, the horizontal and outer diagonals meet in the left-hand region. No conic touching the four lines lies wholly or in part in the shaded regions, and every conic touching the four lines lies wholly in the inner region or wholly in the upper region, or partly in the upper and partly in the lower region, or partly in the right-hand and partly in the left-hand region. It will be convenient to consider,
\begin{enumerate}
    \item[$1^\circ$] the conics which lie in the inner region;
    \item[$2^\circ$] the conics which lie in the upper and lower regions, or in the upper region only;
    \item[$3^\circ$] the conics which lie in the right-hand and left-hand regions.
\end{enumerate}

$1^\circ$. The conics in the inner region are obviously ellipses; an extreme term is the finite right line $BD$, considered as an indefinitely thin ellipse; this gradually broadens out and there is (as a mean term) an ellipse which touches the four lines in the points in which they are intersected two and two by the lines joining the points $E$, $F$ with the point of intersection of the diagonals $AC$ and $BD$, the ellipse then narrows in the transverse direction and at length reduces itself to the finite line $AC$ considered as an indefinitely thin ellipse.

$2^\circ$. Considering first the conics which lie in the upper and lower regions, these are of course hyperbolas, an extreme term is the infinite portions $A\infty$ and $C\infty$ of the line $AC$, considered as an indefinitely thin hyperbola: we have then hyperbolas such that for each of them, one branch lies in the lower region and touches the two lines through $A$, while the other branch lies in the upper region and touches the two lines through $C$; the points of contact of the lower branch with the lines through $A$ gradually recede from $A$, but the point of contact on the line $AD$, which adjoins the left-hand region, recedes with the greater rapidity, and it at last becomes infinite while the point of contact with the line $AB$ which adjoins the right-hand region remains finite: we have thus a hyperbola having the line $AD$ for its asymptote; the point at infinity of this line belongs of course indifferently to the upper and lower regions; and we may therefore consider one branch as lying in the lower region and touching $AB$ and (at infinity) $AD$; the other branch as lying in the upper region and touching the two lines through $C$, and besides (at infinity) the line $AD$. We have next a series of hyperbolas such that for each of them, one branch lies in the lower region and touches only the line $AB$, the other branch lies in the upper region and touches the two lines through $C$ and besides the line $AD$. We arrive again at a limiting case when the point of contact on the line $AB$ passes off to infinity, or $AB$ becomes an asymptote; we have here in the lower region a branch touching (at infinity) $AB$ and in the upper region a branch touching the two lines through $C$, the line $AD$, and besides (at infinity) the line $AB$. To this succeeds a series of hyperbolas such that for each of them, one branch lies in the lower region but does not touch either of the lines through $A$, the other branch lies in the upper region and touches as well the two lines through $C$ as also the two lines through $A$. Finally the branch in the lower region passes off to infinity, or the conic becomes a parabola lying wholly in the upper region.

We have thus arrived at the conics which lie wholly in the upper region, the extreme term being a parabola: this passes into an ellipse touching of course the four lines, and gradually reducing itself to the finite line $EF$ considered as an indefinitely thin ellipse.

$3^\circ$. We come now to the conics which lie in the right-hand and left-hand regions; an extreme term is the conic composed of the infinite portions $E\infty$ and $F\infty$ of the line $EF$, considered as an indefinitely thin hyperbola; we have then a series of hyperbolas such that for each of them the branch in the right-hand region touches the two lines through $E$, and the branch in the left-hand region the two lines through $F$; we then arrive at a limiting case where the branch in the right-hand region touches the line $EG$ the upper boundary of the right-hand region at $\infty$, or where $EG$ is an asymptote; we may say that the branch in the right-hand region touches the line $EB$ and (at infinity) the line $EC$, while the branch in the left-hand region touches not only the two lines through $F$ but also (at infinity) the line $EG$; we have next a series of hyperbolas such that for each of them the branch in the right-hand region touches only the line $EB$ while the branch in the left-hand region touches as well the two lines through $F$ as the line $EC$; we have then again a limiting case, viz. the branch in the right-hand region touches the line $BG$ at infinity, or $BG$ is an asymptote; we may here say that the branch in the right-hand region touches the line $BE$ and (at infinity) the line $BG$, while the branch in the left-hand region touches the lines through $D$ and (at infinity) the line $BG$; and to this succeeds a series of hyperbolas such that for each of them the branch in the right-hand region touches the two lines through $B$ while the branch in the left-hand region touches the two lines through $D$; the hyperbola finally becomes the infinite portions $B\infty$ and $D\infty$ of the line $BD$, considered as an indefinitely thin hyperbola.

It is to be noticed that the entire system commences with the finite line $BD$ considered as an indefinitely thin ellipse and passes on continuously to the infinite portions $B\infty$ and $D\infty$ of the same line, considered as an indefinitely thin hyperbola: it is therefore a cyclical series, and any term whatever might have been taken for the commencement of the series.
