\newpage
\section*{286. Note on the Value of Certain Determinants, the Terms of which are the Squared Distances of Points in a Plane or in Space}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{286. Note on the Value of Certain Determinants}
\label{sec:286}

[From the \textit{Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics}, vol.~III. (1860), pp.~275--277.]

The values of the several determinants mentioned in my paper ``On a Certain Theorem in the Geometry of Position,'' Cambridge Mathematical Journal, Old Series, t.~II. (1842), p.~267, [1], are as follows:

(1)
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
0, & 12^2, & 13^2, & 1 \\
21^2, & 0, & 23^2, & 1 \\
31^2, & 32^2, & 0, & 1 \\
1, & 1, & 1, & 0
\end{vmatrix}
= 2\,12^2\cdot\,21^2\cdot\,23^2,
\]
where the $\Sigma$, $\Sigma$ contain 3 and 6 terms respectively.

(2)
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
0, & 12^2, & 13^2, & 14^2, & 1 \\
21^2, & 0, & 23^2, & 24^2, & 1 \\
31^2, & 32^2, & 0, & 34^2, & 1 \\
41^2, & 42^2, & 43^2, & 0, & 1 \\
1, & 1, & 1, & 1, & 0
\end{vmatrix}
\]
where the $\Sigma$, $\Sigma$, $\Sigma$ contain 24, 12 and 8 terms respectively.
\begin{align*}
&= 2\,12^2\cdot\,23^2\cdot\,34^2 - 2\,12^2\cdot\,34^2\cdot\,43^2 - 2\,12^2\cdot\,23^2\cdot\,31^2\\
&\quad - 2\,12^2\cdot\,23^2\cdot\,34^2\cdot\,45^2 - 2\,12^2\cdot\,23^2\cdot\,34^2\cdot\,43^2\\
&\quad + 2\,12^2\cdot\,23^2\cdot\,45^2\cdot\,54^2 + 2\,12^2\cdot\,23^2\cdot\,34^2\cdot\,41^2 + 2\,12^2\cdot\,23^2\cdot\,31^2\cdot\,45^2.
\end{align*}

(3)
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
0, & 12^2, & 13^2, & 14^2, & 15^2, & 1 \\
21^2, & 0, & 23^2, & 24^2, & 25^2, & 1 \\
31^2, & 32^2, & 0, & 34^2, & 35^2, & 1 \\
41^2, & 42^2, & 43^2, & 0, & 45^2, & 1 \\
51^2, & 52^2, & 53^2, & 54^2, & 0, & 1 \\
1, & 1, & 1, & 1, & 1, & 0
\end{vmatrix}
\]
where the $\Sigma$, $\Sigma$, $\Sigma$, $\Sigma$, $\Sigma$ contain 120, 15, 60, 30, and 40 terms respectively.

(4)
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
0, & 12^2, & 13^2, & 14^2 \\
21^2, & 0, & 23^2, & 24^2 \\
31^2, & 32^2, & 0, & 34^2 \\
41^2, & 42^2, & 43^2, & 0
\end{vmatrix}
\]
where the $\Sigma$, $\Sigma$ contain 3 and 6 terms respectively.
\begin{align*}
&= 2\,12^2\cdot\,21^2\cdot\,34^2\cdot\,43^2 - 2\,12^2\cdot\,23^2\cdot\,34^2\cdot\,41^2,\\
&\quad 2\,12^2\cdot\,23^2\cdot\,34^2\cdot\,45^2\cdot\,51^2 - 2\,12^2\cdot\,23^2\cdot\,31^2\cdot\,45^2\cdot\,54^2.
\end{align*}

(5)
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
0, & 12^2, & 13^2, & 14^2, & 15^2 \\
21^2, & 0, & 23^2, & 24^2, & 25^2 \\
31^2, & 32^2, & 0, & 34^2, & 35^2 \\
41^2, & 42^2, & 43^2, & 0, & 45^2 \\
51^2, & 52^2, & 53^2, & 54^2, & 0
\end{vmatrix}
\]
where the $\Sigma$, $\Sigma$ contain 24 and 20 terms respectively.

And it is proper to remark that it is not in the preceding formul\ae\ (as in the memoir above referred to in which $12$ denotes a distance between two points 1 and 2) assumed that $12$ and $21$ are equal.

The formula (1) gives, if $a, b, c$ represent the sides, the well-known expression for the area of a triangle
\[
(\text{area})^2 = \tfrac{1}{16}(2b^2c^2 + 2c^2a^2 + 2a^2b^2 - a^4 - b^4 - c^4).
\]
Similarly the formula (2), if $a, b, c, f, g, h$ represent the edges, viz. $23 = a$, $31 = b$, $12 = c$, $14 = f$, $24 = g$, $34 = h$, gives for the volume of a tetrahedron
\begin{align*}
288(\text{volume})^2 &= b^2c^2(g^2 + h^2) + c^2a^2(h^2 + f^2) + a^2b^2(f^2 + g^2)\\
&\quad + a^2f^2(b^2 + c^2) + b^2g^2(c^2 + a^2) + c^2h^2(a^2 + b^2)\\
&\quad - a^2f^2(a^2 + f^2) - b^2f^2(b^2 + g^2) - c^2h^2(c^2 + h^2)\\
&\quad - a^2g^2h^2 - b^2f^2h^2 - c^2f^2g^2 - a^2b^2c^2,\\
&= W\text{ suppose}.
\end{align*}

Now
\begin{align*}
16 \times (\text{surface})^2 &= 2g^2a^2 + 2a^2b^2 + 2b^2g^2 - a^4 - b^4 - g^4\\
&\quad + 2f^2h^2 + 2h^2b^2 + 2b^2f^2 - h^4 - b^4 - f^4\\
&\quad + 2g^2f^2 + 2f^2c^2 + 2c^2g^2 - g^4 - f^4 - c^4\\
&\quad + 2b^2c^2 + 2c^2a^2 + 2a^2b^2 - a^4 - b^4 - c^4,\\
&= x + y + z + w\text{ suppose},
\end{align*}
and the norm of this is
\[
(x^2 + y^2 + z^2 + w^2 - 2yz - 2zx - 2xy - 2xw - 2yw - 2zw)^2 = -64xyzw,
\]
which is of the sixteenth order, and must be of the form $W \cdot Q$ where $Q$ is a function of $a, b, c, f, g, h$ of the tenth order. The expression of this function is given by Prof Sylvester in his paper ``On the Relation between the Volume of a Tetrahedron \&c.,'' Camb. and Dubl. Math. Jour., t.~VIII. (1853), pp.~171--178, viz. the value is
\begin{align*}
Q &= a^2b^2c^2(f^4 + g^4 + h^4 + 5f^2g^2 + 5h^2f^2 + f^2g^2h^2 + c^2a^2 + a^2b^2\\
&\quad - (f^2 + g^2 + h^2)(a^2 + b^2 + c^2))\\
&+ a^2f^2h^2(f^4 + b^4 + c^4 + b^2c^2 + c^2f^2 + g^2h^2 + h^2f^2 + f^2b^2\\
&\quad - (f^2 + b^2 + c^2)(a^2 + g^2 + h^2))\\
&+ b^2g^2f^2(g^4 + c^4 + a^4 + c^2a^2 + a^2f^2 + g^2c^2 + h^2f^2 + f^2h^2\\
&\quad - (g^2 + c^2 + a^2)(b^2 + h^2 + f^2))\\
&+ c^2h^2g^2(h^4 + a^4 + b^4 + a^2b^2 + b^2g^2 + h^2a^2 + f^2g^2 + g^2f^2\\
&\quad - (h^4 + a^2 + b^2)(c^2 + f^2 + g^2)),
\end{align*}
and, as there remarked, the equation $Q = 0$ expresses the condition that the radius of the inscribed sphere may be infinite.

\hfill 2, Stone Buildings, W.C., June 10th, 1859.


% ============================================================
\newpage
\section*{287. Note on the Equation for the Squared Differences of the Roots of a Cubic Equation}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{287. Note on the Equation for the Squared Differences of the Roots of a Cubic Equation}
\label{sec:287}

[From the \textit{Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics}, vol.~III. (1860), pp.~307--309.]

The question of finding the equation for the squared differences of the roots, presents, in the case of a cubic equation, a peculiarity which does not occur for equations of a higher order, viz. we may in the first instance form the equation for the differences of the roots taken in a given cyclical order, and thence deduce the equation for the squared differences of the roots. Let the cubic equation be
\[
U = (a, b, c, d \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} x, 1)^3 = a(x - \alpha)(x - \beta)(x - \gamma) = 0,
\]
the function
\[
\Pi\{\theta - (\beta - \gamma)\},
\]
which equated to zero gives for $\theta$ the values $\beta - \gamma$, $\gamma - \alpha$, $\alpha - \beta$, which are the differences of the roots taken in a given circular order, has for any interchanges whatever of the roots, two values only, viz. that just written down, and the value $\Pi\{\theta - (\gamma - \beta)\}$, which may be deduced therefrom by changing first the sign of $\theta$ and then the sign of the entire expression (or what is the same thing, by changing the signs of the terms containing the even powers of $\theta$); we may consequently write
\[
\Pi\{\theta - (\beta - \gamma)\} = P - Q\theta\, i\,(\alpha, \beta, \gamma),
\]
which $P$, $Q$ are symmetrical functions of the roots, and $i\,(\alpha, \beta, \gamma)$ or $(\alpha - \beta)(\beta - \gamma)(\gamma - \alpha)$ is a function the square of which is a symmetrical function of the roots, and such symmetrical functions of the roots can of course be expressed as functions of the coefficients. We have in fact
\begin{align*}
P &= a^2\theta^2 + \theta(2\beta\gamma - 3\alpha^2) = a^{-2}\{a^2\theta^2 + 9(ac - b^2)\theta\},\\
Q &= 1,
\end{align*}
and
\[
\Pi(\beta - \gamma)^2 = -27D,
\]
where $D$ is the discriminant of the cubic function,
\[
D = a^2d^2 - 6abcd + 4ac^3 + 4b^3d - 3b^2c^2.
\]
Consequently
\[
\Pi\{\theta - (\beta - \gamma)\} = a^{-2}\{a^2\theta^2 + 9(ac - b^2)\theta - \sqrt{-27D}\},
\]
and forming the similar equation
\[
\Pi\{\theta + (\beta - \gamma)\} = a^{-2}\{a^2\theta^2 + 9(ac - b^2)\theta + \sqrt{-27D}\},
\]
multiplying the two equations together and writing $u$ in the place of $\theta^2$, we find
\[
\Pi\{u - (\beta - \gamma)^2\} = a^{-4}\{[a^2u + 9(ac - b^2)]^2u + 27D\},
\]
and the equation for the squared differences of the roots is thus seen to be
\[
\{a^2u + 9(ac - b^2)\}^2u + 27D = 0,
\]
or what is the same thing
\[
a^6u^3 + 18a^4(ac - b^2)u^2 + 81(ac - b^2)^2u + 27D = 0.
\]

I remark that if $\omega$ is an imaginary cube root of unity (so that $(\omega - \omega^2)^2 = -3$, $\omega - \omega^2$ being thus only another form of $\sqrt{-3}$) then if in the expression for $\Pi\{\theta - (\beta - \gamma)\}$ we write $\frac{\theta}{a(\omega - \omega^2)}$ in the place of $\theta$, the equation assumes the more simple form
\[
\Pi\{\theta - \tfrac{1}{2}a(\omega - \omega^2)(\beta - \gamma)\} = \theta^2 - 3H\theta - a^{-2}\sqrt{D},
\]
which if $U$ be the cubic function, $H$ the Hessian $= (ac - b^2, ad - bc, bd - c^2 \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} x, y)^2$, and $D$ the discriminant as before, is a particular case (obtained by writing $x = 1$, $y = 0$) of the equation
\[
\Pi\{\theta - \tfrac{1}{2}a(\omega - \omega^2)(x - \alpha y)\} = \theta^2 - 3H\theta - U\sqrt{D},
\]
which equation can be at once obtained from the equation (where $\Phi$ is the cubicovariant of the cubic function)
\[
\sqrt[3]{(\Phi + U^2\sqrt{D})} - \sqrt[3]{(\Phi - U^2\sqrt{D})} = \tfrac{1}{2}a(\omega - \omega^2)(\beta - \gamma)(x - \alpha y),
\]
given in my Fifth Memoir on Quantics, Phil. Trans., t.~CXLVIII. (1858), [156]. For writing for a moment
\[
\sqrt[3]{(\Phi + U^2\sqrt{D})} = X,\quad \sqrt[3]{(\Phi - U^2\sqrt{D})} = Y,
\]
we find
\[
\theta^3 + 3\sqrt[3]{XY}\,\theta - (X - Y) = 0,
\]
or
\[
\theta^2 + 3\sqrt[3]{XY}\theta - (X - Y) = 0,
\]
where $\sqrt[3]{XY} = \sqrt[3]{(\Phi^2 - U^4D)}$, which by the equation (given in the Memoir) is $= -H$, and $(X - Y)$ is $= U^2\sqrt{D}$, so that the equation in $\theta$ is, as above, $\theta^2 - 3H\theta - U\sqrt{D} = 0$, an equation which is satisfied by $\theta = \tfrac{1}{2}a(\omega - \omega^2)(\beta - \gamma)(x - \alpha y)$; and the other two roots being of course of the like form, the cubic function in $\theta$ is equal to $\Pi\{\theta - \tfrac{1}{2}a(\omega - \omega^2)(\beta - \gamma)(x - \alpha y)\}$ which proves the theorem.

\hfill 2, Stone Buildings, W.C., Nov. 3rd, 1859.

\hfill C.~IV.\ 59



% ============================================================
\newpage
\section*{288. Note on the Curvature of a Plane Curve at a Double Point, and on the Curvature of Surfaces}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{288. Note on the Curvature of a Plane Curve at a Double Point, and on the Curvature of Surfaces}
\label{sec:288}

[From the \textit{Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics}, vol.~III. (1860), pp.~322--326.]

The radius of curvature of a plane curve at a double point is most readily determined as follows: viz. if $x, y$ be the coordinates of the double point, $\theta$ the inclination to the axis of $x$ of the tangent to the branch under consideration, and $u, v$ the coordinates measured from the double point as origin, parallel and perpendicular to the tangent, of the consecutive point on such branch, then the radius of curvature for the branch in question is
\[
\frac{u^2}{2v};
\]
the coordinates of the consecutive point are
\[
x + u\cos\theta - v\sin\theta,\quad y + u\sin\theta + v\cos\theta,
\]
and the value of $\frac{u^2}{2v}$ is to be found by substituting these expressions for $x, y$ in the equation of the curve. Let $U = 0$ be the equation of the curve; then at the double point, $U$ and the differential coefficients of the first order vanish, let those of the second order be $(a, b, c)$ and those of the third order $(a, b, c, d)$, then substituting we have
\[
\tfrac{1}{2}(a, b, c \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} u\cos\theta - v\sin\theta,\; u\sin\theta + v\cos\theta)^2 + \tfrac{1}{6}(a, b, c, d \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} u\cos\theta - v\sin\theta,\; u\sin\theta + v\cos\theta)^3 = 0.
\]
The coefficient of $u^2$ is
\[
\tfrac{1}{2}(a, b, c \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} \cos\theta, \sin\theta)^2,
\]
which vanishes, since it is by putting this function equal to zero that we find the direction of the tangents at the double point. For the present purpose we are concerned with one of the two branches only, and in all that follows the ratio $\cos\theta : \sin\theta$ will denote a determinate root of the quadratic equation; viz. the root which corresponds to the branch in question.

The equation takes therefore the form
\[
Buv + \tfrac{1}{2}Cv^2 + \tfrac{1}{3}Du^3 + \&c. = 0,
\]
which might be satisfied by assuming $Buv + \tfrac{1}{2}Cv^2 = 0$, but the values so obtained belong to the branch which does not touch the tangent; the proper solution is
\[
Buv + \tfrac{1}{3}Du^3 = 0,
\]
and thence
\[
\frac{u^2}{2v} = -\frac{3B}{D},
\]
or substituting for $B$, $D$ their values, the radius of curvature is
\[
-\frac{3\,(a, b, c \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} \cos\theta, \sin\theta)(-\sin\theta, \cos\theta)}{(a, b, c, d \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} \cos\theta, \sin\theta)^3},
\]
where the expression in the numerator is
\begin{align*}
&= (-a + c)\sin^2\theta\cos\theta + b(\cos^2\theta - \sin^2\theta),\\
&= \tfrac{1}{2}\{(c - a)\sin 2\theta + 2b\cos 2\theta\}.
\end{align*}

The curvature, at the point of contact, of the curve in which any surface is intersected by a tangent plane cannot be found by the ordinary theory of the curvature of surfaces, being (by reason that the point of contact is a double point on the curve) a case of exception from that theory. The foregoing method may be applied to the solution of the question as follows: Let $U = 0$ be the equation of the surface, and suppose that $(x, y, z)$ refer to the point of contact of the tangent plane. Let $L, M, N$ be the first differential coefficients of $U$ at this point, $(a, b, c, f, g, h)$ the second differential coefficients, $(a, b, c, f, g, h, i, j, k, l)$ the third differential coefficients. Let $\lambda, \mu, \nu$ be proportional to the cosines of the inclinations to the axis of the tangent to one of the two branches through the double point; the ratios $\lambda : \mu : \nu$ are determined by
\begin{align*}
(L, M, N \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} \lambda, \mu, \nu) &= 0,\\
(a, b, c, f, g, h \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} \lambda, \mu, \nu)^2 &= 0.
\end{align*}
Let $u, v$ be proportional to the coordinates of a consecutive point measured, from the point of contact as origin, in the direction of the tangent and in the perpendicular direction in the tangent plane. The cosines of the inclinations of $u$ to the axes are as $\lambda : \mu : \nu$; those for the inclinations of the normal to the axes are as $L : M : N$; hence for the coordinate $v$ which is perpendicular to the plane of the last-mentioned two lines, the cosines of the inclinations to the axes are as $N\mu - M\nu : L\nu - N\lambda : M\lambda - L\mu$, and the coordinates of the consecutive point may be taken to be
\begin{align*}
x &+ \lambda u + (N\mu - M\nu)v,\\
y &+ \mu u + (L\nu - N\lambda)v,\\
z &+ \nu u + (M\lambda - L\mu)v.
\end{align*}
Substituting these values in the equation of the surface, the terms involving the first powers of $u, v$ vanish, and the term involving $u^2$ also vanishes in virtue of the relation $(a, b, c, f, g, h \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} \lambda, \mu, \nu)^2 = 0$. The equation consequently becomes
\[
Buv + \tfrac{1}{2}Cv^2 + \tfrac{1}{3}Du^3 + \ldots = 0,
\]
and we have as before for the branch in question,
\[
\frac{u^2}{2v} = -\frac{3B}{D}.
\]

In the present case $u, v$ have been taken, not as before equal, but only proportional, to the coordinates of the consecutive point measured from the point of contact parallel and perpendicular to the tangent, the values of the coordinates are in fact
\[
\sqrt{\lambda^2 + \mu^2 + \nu^2}\,u,\quad \frac{\sqrt{L^2 + M^2 + N^2}}{\sqrt{\lambda^2 + \mu^2 + \nu^2}}\,v,
\]
and the expression for the radius of curvature is
\[
\frac{u^2}{2v}\sqrt{\lambda^2 + \mu^2 + \nu^2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{L^2 + M^2 + N^2}},
\]
or substituting for $\frac{u^2}{2v}$ the value $-\frac{3B}{D}$ and for $B$, $D$ their values, the radius of curvature is
\[
-\frac{3\sqrt{\lambda^2 + \mu^2 + \nu^2}}{\sqrt{L^2 + M^2 + N^2}}\frac{(a, b, c, f, g, h \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} \lambda, \mu, \nu)(N\mu - M\nu,\; L\nu - N\lambda,\; M\lambda - L\mu)}{(a, b, c, f, g, h, i, j, k, l \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} \lambda, \mu, \nu)^3},
\]
where, as already noticed, the ratios $\lambda : \mu : \nu$ are determined by the equations
\begin{align*}
(L, M, N \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} \lambda, \mu, \nu) &= 0,\\
(a, b, c, f, g, h \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} \lambda, \mu, \nu)^2 &= 0,
\end{align*}
the system of roots selected being that which corresponds to the branch under consideration. It may be noticed that these two equations give
\begin{multline*}
(2\mathfrak{A}, 2\mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H} \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} L, M, N)^2(a, b, c, f, g, h \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} \lambda, \mu, \nu)^2\\
- K\{(L, M, N \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} \lambda, \mu, \nu)\}^2 = 0,
\end{multline*}
where as usual
\begin{align*}
(2\mathfrak{A}, 2\mathfrak{B}, \mathfrak{C}, \mathfrak{F}, \mathfrak{G}, \mathfrak{H}) &= (bc - f^2,\; ca - g^2,\; ab - h^2,\; gh - af,\; hf - bg,\; fg - ch),\\
K &= abc - af^2 - bg^2 - ch^2 + 2fgh,
\end{align*}
and the expression on the left-hand side considered as a function of $(\lambda, \mu, \nu)$ is decomposable into a pair of factors. Selecting the proper factor and equating it to zero, we have in addition to the linear equation
\[
(L, M, N \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} \lambda, \mu, \nu) = 0,
\]
a new linear equation; these two equations determine the ratios $\lambda : \mu : \nu$.

\textbf{P.S.}---I remark that the process adopted for finding the radius of curvature at a double point of a plane curve is the most simple one for the case of an ordinary point on the curve. In fact let $U = 0$ be the curve, $(x, y)$ the coordinates of the point in question, $L, M$ the corresponding values of the first differential coefficients, $(a, b, c)$ those of the second differential coefficients. The coordinates of the consecutive point are
\[
x + u\cos\theta - v\sin\theta,\quad y + u\sin\theta + v\cos\theta.
\]
Substituting these in the equation of the curve, we have
\[
L(u\cos\theta - v\sin\theta) + M(u\sin\theta + v\cos\theta) + \tfrac{1}{2}(a, b, c \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} u\cos\theta - v\sin\theta,\; u\sin\theta + v\cos\theta)^2 = 0,
\]
the coefficient of $u$ must be zero, or we have
\[
L\cos\theta + M\sin\theta = 0,
\]
giving
\[
\sin\theta = \frac{L}{\sqrt{L^2 + M^2}},\quad \cos\theta = -\frac{M}{\sqrt{L^2 + M^2}},
\]
and the equation may be reduced to
\[
\sqrt{L^2 + M^2}\,v + \tfrac{1}{2}(a, b, c \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} \cos\theta, \sin\theta)^2 u^2 = 0,
\]
or what is the same thing
\[
(L^2 + M^2)^{\frac{3}{2}} v + \tfrac{1}{2}(a, b, c \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} M, -L)^2 u^2 = 0,
\]
whence the radius of curvature
\[
\frac{u^2}{2v} = \frac{(a, b, c \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} M, -L)^2}{(L^2 + M^2)^{\frac{3}{2}}},
\]
which is the ordinary form for the radius of curvature at any point of a curve represented by the equation $U = 0$.

\hfill 2, Stone Buildings, W.C., October 2nd, 1859.


% ============================================================
\newpage
\section*{289. On Some Numerical Expansions}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{289. On Some Numerical Expansions}
\label{sec:289}

[From the \textit{Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics}, vol.~III. (1860), pp.~366--369.]

There are in the theory of series and the calculus of finite differences and the differential calculus, various sets of numbers, such for instance as Bernoulli's Numbers, or the successive differences of $0^m$, which from their more or less frequent occurrence and the complexity of their law it is desirable to tabulate. To these belong, I think, the coefficients of the successive powers of $x$ in the expansion of a given power of $\frac{\log(1+x)}{x}$, or when the index of the power of the function is an indeterminate quantity $r$, then the coefficients of the several terms of the rational and integral functions of $r$ which form the coefficients of the successive powers of $x$ in the expansion in question. We have
\[
\log\left(\frac{\log(1+x)}{x}\right) = -\frac{x}{2} + \frac{x^2}{24} - \frac{x^4}{2880} + \frac{x^6}{181440} - \frac{x^8}{9676800} + \frac{x^{10}}{479001600} - \&c.,
\]
which is most easily verified by differentiating and multiplying by $\log(1+x)$, which gives on the left-hand side $\frac{1}{1+x}$, and on the right-hand side the expansion $\tfrac{1}{2} - x + x^2 - \&c$. And from the above, multiplying each side by $r$, and taking the exponential, we find
\[
\left(\frac{\log(1+x)}{x}\right)^r = 1 - r\frac{x}{2} + r\left(\frac{r}{2} + \frac{1}{4}\right)\frac{x^2}{6} - r\left(\frac{r^2}{6} + \frac{r}{4} + \frac{1}{8}\right)\frac{x^3}{24} + \&c.,
\]
which may be verified by writing $r = 1$.

As an instance of the use of the preceding table, let it be required to find expressions for the combinations without repetitions of the series of natural numbers $1, 2, 3, \ldots(n-1)$, or what is the same thing for the coefficients of the powers of $k$ in $k(k-1)(k-2)\ldots(k-n+1)$. We have
\begin{align*}
k(k-1)(k-2)\ldots(k-n+1) &= k^n - A_1^{(n)}k^{n-1} + A_2^{(n)}k^{n-2} - \&c.\\
&= n!\cdot\text{coefficient }x^n\text{ in }(1+x)^k;
\end{align*}
whence
\begin{align*}
(-)^nA_r^{(n)} &= n!\cdot\text{coefficient }x^nk^{n-r}\text{ in }(1+x)^k\\
&= n!\cdot\text{coefficient }x^nk^{n-r}\text{ in }e^{k\log(1+x)}\\
&= \frac{n!}{(n-r)!}\cdot\text{coefficient }x^n\text{ in }\{\log(1+x)\}^{n-r}\\
&= \frac{n!}{(n-r)!}\cdot\text{coefficient }x^r\text{ in }\left(\frac{\log(1+x)}{x}\right)^{n-r}.
\end{align*}

\[
A_2^{(n)} = n(n-1)\{H(n-2)\},
\]
\[
A_3^{(n)} = n(n-1)(n-2)\{\tfrac{1}{2}H(n-3)^2 + \tfrac{1}{4}H(n-3) + \tfrac{1}{8}\},
\]
and so on, as far as the expansion has been effected. It may be remarked that the general expression for the algebraical transcendent $A_r^{(n)}$ is given in Dr Schl\"{a}fli's paper, ``Sur les coefficients du d\'{e}veloppement du produit $(1+x)(1+2x)\ldots[1+(n-1)x]$ suivant les puissances ascendantes de $x$.'' Crelle, t.~XLIII. [1852], pp.~1--22, but the law is a very complicated one.

We have
\[
\log(1+x) = x - \tfrac{1}{2}x^2 + \tfrac{1}{3}x^3 - \tfrac{1}{4}x^4 + \tfrac{1}{5}x^5 - \tfrac{1}{6}x^6 + \tfrac{1}{7}x^7 - \&c.,
\]
and dividing by $x$, and taking the logarithm, we have as before
\[
\log\left(\frac{\log(1+x)}{x}\right) = -\tfrac{1}{2}x + \tfrac{1}{24}x^2 - \tfrac{1}{2880}x^4 + \tfrac{1}{181440}x^6 - \&c.,
\]
which may be considered as the first of a series of logarithmic derivatives, viz., dividing by $-\tfrac{1}{2}x$ and taking the logarithm we have
\[
\log\left(-\frac{2}{x}\log\frac{\log(1+x)}{x}\right) = -\tfrac{1}{24}x + \tfrac{1}{480}x^2 - \tfrac{1}{5376}x^3 + \tfrac{1}{92160}x^4 - \tfrac{1}{1351680}x^5 + \&c.,
\]
and by the like process
\[
\log\left(\frac{24}{x}\log\left(-\frac{2}{x}\log\frac{\log(1+x)}{x}\right)\right) = -\tfrac{1}{20}x - \tfrac{1}{112}x^2 + \&c.,
\]
and so on.

Suppose in general that
\[
\phi x = x + B_1x^2 + C_1x^3 + \ldots,
\]
and let it be required to find the $r$th function $\phi^r x$. It is easy to see that the successive coefficients are rational and integral functions of $r$ of the degrees $1, 2, 3, \&c$. respectively; we have in fact
\begin{multline*}
\phi^r x = \phi^n x + \frac{r}{1}(\phi' x - \phi^0 x) + \frac{r(r-1)}{1\cdot 2}(\phi^2 x - 2\phi^1 x + \phi^0 x)\\
+ \frac{r(r-1)(r-2)}{1\cdot 2\cdot 3}(\phi^3 x - 3\phi^2 x + 3\phi^1 x - \phi^0 x) + \&c.,
\end{multline*}
and by successive substitutions,
\begin{align*}
\phi^0 x &= x,\\
\phi^1 x &= x + B_1x^2 + C_1x^3 + D_1x^4 + \ldots,\\
\phi^2 x &= x + 2B_1x^2 + (2B_1^2 + 2C_1)x^3 + (B_1^3 + 5B_1C_1 + 2D_1)x^4 + \ldots,\\
\phi^3 x &= x + 3B_1x^2 + (9B_1^2 + 3C_1)x^3 + (27B_1^3 + 15B_1C_1 + 3D_1)x^4 + \ldots.
\end{align*}
Whence
\begin{align*}
\phi^r x &= x\\
&\quad + rB_1x^2\\
&\quad + \{(r^2 - r)B_1^2 + rC_1\}x^3\\
&\quad + \{(r^3 - \tfrac{3}{2}r^2 + \tfrac{1}{2}r)B_1^3 + (\tfrac{3}{2}r^2 - \tfrac{3}{2}r)B_1C_1 + rD_1\}x^4\\
&\quad + \&c.
\end{align*}
It would, I think, be worth while to continue the expansion some steps further.

\hfill 2, Stone Buildings, W.C., Oct. 2nd, 1859.



% ============================================================
\newpage
\section*{290. A Discussion of the Sturmian Constants for Cubic and Quartic Equations}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{290. A Discussion of the Sturmian Constants for Cubic and Quartic Equations}
\label{sec:290}

[From the \textit{Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics}, vol.~IV. (1861), pp.~7--12.]

For the cubic equation
\[
(a, b, c, d \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} x, 1)^3 = 0,
\]
the Sturmian Constants (or leading coefficients of the Sturmian functions) are
\[
a,\; a,\; b^2 - ac,\; -a^2d^2 + 6abcd - 4ac^3 - 4b^3d + 3b^2c^2.
\]
If the signs of the constants, that is, of the functions for $-\infty$, are then the signs of the functions for $+\infty$, are
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccc|c}
$+$ & $+$ & $+$ & $+$ & three real roots.\\
$-$ & $+$ & $-$ & $+$ & case cannot occur.\\
$+$ & $+$ & $-$ & $+$ & one real root.\\
$-$ & $+$ & $+$ & $+$ & three real roots.\\
$+$ & $+$ & $+$ & $-$ & one real root.\\
$-$ & $+$ & $-$ & $-$ & one real root.\\
$+$ & $+$ & $-$ & $-$ & one real root.\\
$-$ & $+$ & $+$ & $-$ & one real root.
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The second case would give a loss of variations of sign in passing from $\infty$ to $-\infty$, which is inconsistent with Sturm's theorem. To show \textit{a posteriori} that the case cannot occur, we may form the identical equation
\[
(a^2d - 3abc + 2b^3)^2 = -a^2(-a^2d^2 + 6abcd - 4ac^3 - 4b^3d + 3b^2c^2) + 4(b^2 - ac)^3,
\]
and, this being so, then in the case in question, the right-hand side would consist of two terms, each of them negative, while the left-hand side is essentially positive.

\hfill C.~IV.\ 60

In the particular case where the third constant vanishes, or $b^2 - ac = 0$, we have
\[
-a^2d^2 + 6abcd - 4ac^3 - 4b^3d + 3b^2c^2 = -(ad - bc)^2 + 4(b^2 - ac)(c^2 - bd) = -(ad - bc)^2,
\]
is negative; hence, regarding the evanescent term as being at pleasure positive or negative, we have in each case a combination of signs corresponding to one real root.

The general result (which is well known) is, that there are three real roots or one real root according as
\[
-a^2d^2 + 6abcd - 4ac^3 - 4b^3d + 3b^2c^2
\]
is positive or negative.

For the quartic equation
\[
(a, b, c, d, e \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} x, 1)^4 = 0,
\]
the Sturmian constants are
\[
a,\; a,\; b^2 - ac,\; 3a^2J + 2(b^2 - ac)I,\; I^3 - 27J^2,
\]
if, as usual,
\begin{align*}
I &= ae - 4bd + 3c^2,\\
J &= ace - ad^2 - b^2e + 2bcd - c^3.
\end{align*}
If the signs of the constants, that is, of the functions for $-\infty$, are then the signs of the functions for $+\infty$, are
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccccc|c}
$+$ & $+$ & $+$ & $+$ & $+$ & 4 real roots.\\
$-$ & $+$ & $-$ & $+$ & $+$ & no real root.\\
$+$ & $+$ & $-$ & $+$ & $+$ & 2 real roots.\\
$-$ & $+$ & $+$ & $+$ & $+$ & 2 real roots.\\
$+$ & $+$ & $+$ & $-$ & $+$ & 2 real roots.\\
$-$ & $+$ & $-$ & $-$ & $+$ & 2 real roots.\\
$+$ & $+$ & $-$ & $-$ & $+$ & 2 real roots.\\
$-$ & $+$ & $+$ & $-$ & $+$ & 2 real roots.\\
$+$ & $+$ & $+$ & $+$ & $-$ & 2 real roots.\\
$-$ & $+$ & $-$ & $+$ & $-$ & 2 real roots.\\
$+$ & $+$ & $-$ & $+$ & $-$ & 2 real roots.\\
$-$ & $+$ & $+$ & $+$ & $-$ & 2 real roots.\\
$+$ & $+$ & $+$ & $-$ & $-$ & no real root.\\
$-$ & $+$ & $-$ & $-$ & $-$ & 4 real roots.\\
$+$ & $+$ & $-$ & $-$ & $-$ & no real root.\\
$-$ & $+$ & $+$ & $-$ & $-$ & 2 real roots.
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The non-existing combination of signs is
\[
I^3 - 27J^2 = -,\quad 3a^2J + 2(b^2 - ac)I = +,\quad b^2 - ac = -.
\]
To show \textit{a posteriori} that this case cannot occur, write
\[
\Phi = a^2d - 3abc + 2b^3,\quad \Lambda = 3a^2J + 2(b^2 - ac)I,
\]
then we have identically
\[
9(3a^2J^2 + \Lambda^2)\Phi^2 = -4a^3\Lambda^3 + 36(b^2 - ac)^2\Lambda^2 - 4a^2(b^2 - ac)^3(I^3 - 27J^2),
\]
which is impossible under the given combination of signs, since the left-hand side would be positive, and the right-hand side negative.

To prove the above identity---the relation $\Phi U^2 - 4U^3H + a^2H^3 + \Lambda^2 = 0$, between the covariants of the quartic, gives
\[
a^3J + a^2(b^2 - ac)I - 4(b^2 - ac)^3 + \Phi^2 = 0,
\]
or, what is the same thing,
\[
\Phi^2 = -a^3J - a^2(b^2 - ac)I + 4(b^2 - ac)^3.
\]
But
\[
\Lambda = 3a^2J + 2(b^2 - ac)I,
\]
and thence
\[
3\Phi^2 + a^2\Lambda = -a^2(b^2 - ac)I + 12(b^2 - ac)^3,
\]
or
\[
3\Phi^2 = -a^2\Lambda - a^2(b^2 - ac)I + 12(b^2 - ac)^3,
\]
and the identity will be true, if
\[
(3\Phi^2 + 9a^4J^2)\Phi^2 = -4\Phi^2\Lambda + 36(b^2 - ac)^2\Phi^2 - 4(b^2 - ac)^3(I^3 - 27J^2).
\]
This gives
\[
(3\Phi^2 + 9a^4J^2)\{-\Lambda - (b^2 - ac)I\} = -4\Phi^2\Lambda - 4(b^2 - ac)^3I^3,
\]
or, what is the same thing,
\[
(3\Phi^2 + 9a^4J^2)\{\Lambda + (b^2 - ac)I\} = 4\{\Phi^2\Lambda + (b^2 - ac)^3I^3\},
\]
or, dividing by $\Lambda + (b^2 - ac)I$,
\[
3\Phi^2 + 9a^4J^2 = 4\{\Phi^2 - \Phi(b^2 - ac)I + (b^2 - ac)^2I^2\},
\]
and reducing
\[
\Phi^2 - 4\Phi(b^2 - ac)I - 9a^4J^2 + 4(b^2 - ac)^2I^3 = 0,
\]
or finally
\[
\{\Phi - 3a^2J - 2(b^2 - ac)I\}\{\Phi + 3a^2J - 2(b^2 - ac)I\} = 0,
\]
which is true in virtue of
\[
\Phi = 3a^2J + 2(b^2 - ac)I,
\]
and the identity is thus proved.

\hfill 60---2

The general conclusion is,

if $I^3 - 27J^2$ is positive, the four roots are all real or all imaginary, viz., all real if $b^2 - ac$ and $3a^2J + 2(b^2 - ac)I$ are both positive, imaginary if otherwise.

But if $I^3 - 27J^2$ is negative, the roots are two of them real, and the other two imaginary.

The following special cases may be noticed,

$1^\circ$. $b^2 - ac = 0$,

here $9(3a^4J^2 + \Lambda^2)\Phi^2 = -4a^3\Lambda^3$, or $\Lambda = 3a^2J + 2(b^2 - ac)I = 3a^2J$, is negative, so that,
\begin{itemize}
    \item if $I^3 - 27J^2$ is $+$, the roots are all imaginary;
    \item if $I^3 - 27J^2$ is $-$, the roots are two real and two imaginary.
\end{itemize}

$2^\circ$. $\Lambda = 3a^2J + 2(b^2 - ac)I = 0$,

here $27a^4J^2\Phi^2 = -4a^2(b^2 - ac)^3(I^3 - 27J^2)$, or $b^2 - ac$, $I^3 - 27J^2$ are of opposite signs, and if
\begin{itemize}
    \item $b^2 - ac = -$, $I^3 - 27J^2 = +$, the roots are all imaginary,
    \item $b^2 - ac = +$, $I^3 - 27J^2 = -$, the roots are two real and two imaginary.
\end{itemize}

$3^\circ$. $b^2 - ac = 0$, $\Lambda = 3a^2J + 2(b^2 - ac)I = 0$,

here $J = 0$, that is,
\[
2bcd - ad^2 - c^3 = 0,\quad \text{or}\quad (ad - bc)^2 + c^2(ac - b^2) = 0,\quad \text{or}\quad ad - bc = 0,
\]
and $I^3 - 27J^2 = I^3$,
\[
I = ae - 4bd + 3c^2 = ae - 4\frac{b^4}{a^2} + 3\frac{b^4}{a^2} = ae - \frac{b^4}{a^2} = \frac{1}{a^2}(a^3e - b^4),
\]
whence $I = +$, the roots are all imaginary. $I = -$, the roots are two real and two imaginary.

This is easily verified, in fact $ac - b^2 = 0$, $ad - bc = 0$, give $c = \frac{b^2}{a}$, $d = \frac{bc}{a} = \frac{b^3}{a^2}$, and the equation becomes
\[
(ax + b)^4 + (a^3e - b^4) = 0,
\]
or, which is the same thing,
\[
(ax + b)^4 + (a^3e - b^4) = 0,
\]
so that the roots are all imaginary, or two real and two imaginary, according to the sign of $a^3e - b^4$ as above.

It may be noticed that for a quintic equation
\[
(a, b, c, d, e, f \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} x, 1)^5 = 0,
\]
if the Sturmian Constants are
\[
a,\; a,\; C,\; D,\; E,\; F,
\]
where as before $a$ is positive, then the roots are real or imaginary as follows: viz.,
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccc|c}
$C$ & $D$ & $E$ & $F$ & \\\hline
$+$ & $+$ & $+$ & $+$ & 5 real roots.\\
$-$ & $+$ & $+$ & $+$ & \\[-4pt]
  & $+$ & $-$ & $+$ & \\[-4pt]
  & $+$ & $+$ & $-$ & 1 real root, 4 imaginary roots.\\
$+$ & $+$ & $+$ & $-$ & \\[-4pt]
  & $+$ & $+$ & $-$ & \\[-4pt]
  & $+$ & $-$ & $-$ & 3 real roots, 2 imaginary roots.\\
$-$ & $+$ & $-$ & $-$ & \\[-4pt]
  & $+$ & $-$ & $+$ & \\[-4pt]
  & $-$ & $+$ & $+$ & cases which do not occur.
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The values of $C, D, E$, and $F$ are given in my ``Tables of the Sturmian Functions for Equations of the Second, Third, Fourth, and Fifth Degrees,'' Phil. Trans., t.~147 (1857), pp.~733--736, [151], but I have not further examined this case.

\hfill 2, Stone Buildings, W.C., September 29, 1859.

\newpage
\section*{291. On the Demonstration of a Theorem Relating to the Moments of Inertia of a Solid Body}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{291. On the Demonstration of a Theorem Relating to the Moments of Inertia of a Solid Body}
\label{sec:291}

[From the \textit{Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics}, vol.~IV. (1861), pp.~25--27.]

Considering in the first instance the analogous question in piano, let $a = \int x^2\,dm$, $b = \int y^2\,dm$, $h = \int xy\,dm$, where the integration extends over any closed figure whatever, then it is to be shown that the equations
\[
(a, h, b \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} p, q)^2 = 1,\quad \text{and}\quad (a + b)(p^2 + q^2) - (a, h, b \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} p, q)^2 = 1,
\]
represent respectively ellipses.

If in the first case, by a transformation of coordinates,
\[
(a, h, b \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} p, q)^2 = a_1p_1^2 + b_1q_1^2,
\]
then $a_1$, $b_1$ are the roots of the quadratic equations,
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
a - \rho & h \\
h & b - \rho
\end{vmatrix} = 0,
\]
and if in the second case,
\[
(a + b)(p^2 + q^2) - (a, h, b \mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\frown$}}} p, q)^2 = a_1p_1^2 + b_1q_1^2,
\]
then $a_1$, $b_1$ are the roots of
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
b - \rho & -h \\
-h & a - \rho
\end{vmatrix} = 0,
\]
the two equations being in fact the same equation,
\[
\rho^2 - (a + b)\rho + ab - h^2 = 0,
\]
